\section{Overview}

The following notes are an introduction into the theory of nonlinear wave equations, and mathematical fluids that I've written in preparation for the mathematics PhD oral examination at Stony Brook. These notes cover a few fundamental results on the (local) well-posedness of (non)linear wave equations, energy estimates, Strichartz estimates, and related results in harmonic analysis and fluids. In some sense, these notes are meant to be an extensive log of everything I have studied, and therefore, does not contain many novel ideals. The content reflects my most current understanding of the key ideas, and were written alongside discussions with my advisor, Professor John Anderson. Moreover, these notes are strongly based on the corresponding notes of Professors Holzegel, Lawrie, and Luk. It goes without saying that any errors in the following notes are entirely my responsibility. 

\newpage 
\part{Functional and Harmonic Analysis}
\section{General Analytic Results}

In this subsection, we will be introducing some general analytic results in functional analysis and harmonic analysis, that we will later use in our study of the (non)linear wave equations (and hyperbolic PDE, in general) \cite{Stein}. 

\begin{figure}[h!]
	\centering 
	\includegraphics[width = 0.5\textwidth]{mickey}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Introductory Harmonic Analysis} 

In this subsection, we will see some applications of $L^{p}$ spaces. In particular, we introduce some rudimentary objects in \textbf{harmonic analysis}. Roughly speaking, harmonic analysis had its origins in trying to ``decompose'' functions into basic building blocks composed of elementary waves. In this sense, harmonic analysis closely resembles the related field of \textit{Fourier analysis}. In particular, the theory for Fourier analysis is especially well-developed in Hilbert spaces (e.g., like $L^{2}$), where basic results like orthogonality, Plancherel's theorem, and Parseval's identity make the analysis especially clear to study. But in general, studying only $L^{2}$ functions is incredibly restrictive, and might not necessarily illuminate the full range of properties of various operators that we study (e.g., the Fourier transform). Thus a natural question to ask is if the results that holds in $L^{2}$ (more broadly, Hilbert spaces) either hold, or have analogs in $L^{p}$ for $p \neq 2$. Thus we will begin by trying to develop $L^{p}$-analogs to the foundational \textit{Parseval's identity}, which will lead us to the \textbf{Riesz-Thorin interpolation theorem}. Next we will try to develop the $L^{p}$ equivalent of \textit{conjugate functions} and \textit{allied series}, which will lead to the \textbf{Hilbert transform}, and then we will encounter a few spaces that are better than $L^{1}, L^{\infty}$ spaces, which leads to the theory of \textbf{Hardy spaces}. 

\subsubsection{The Riesz-Thorin Interpolation Theorem} 

If $f \in L^{2}([0,2\pi])$ is square integrable, then the $L^{2}$ Parseval relation tells us that 
\begin{equation}
	\sum_{n = -\infty}^{\infty}|a_{n}|^{2} = \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|f(\theta)|^{2}\;d\theta, 
	\label{eq:L2_Parseval}
\end{equation}
where for each $n$, 
\begin{equation*}
	a_{n} = \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(\theta)e^{-in\theta}\;d\theta 
\end{equation*}
are the \textbf{Fourier coefficients} of $f$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
	f(\theta) \sim \sum_{n = -\infty}^{\infty}a_{n}e^{in\theta}. 
\end{equation*}
Likewise, if $\{a_{n}\} \in \ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})$, then there exists a unique $f \in L^{2}([0,2\pi])$ such that $f(\theta) \sim \sum_{n = -\infty}^{\infty}a_{n}e^{in\theta}$ and (\ref{eq:L2_Parseval}) holds. If we assume $f \in L^{p}([0,2\pi])$ or $\{a_{n}\} \in \ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})$ for some $p \neq 2$, does the $L^{2}$ Parseval relation generalize to these cases? In general, one has the \textit{Hausdorff-Young inequalities}, 
\begin{equation*}
	\left(\sum |a_{n}|^{q}\right)^{1/q} \leq \left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|f(\theta)|^{p}\;d\theta\right)^{1/p}, 
\end{equation*}
and 
\begin{equation*}
	\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|f(\theta)|^{q}\;d\theta\right)^{1/q} \leq \left(\sum |a_{n}|^{p}\right)^{1/p}. 
\end{equation*}

Thus we need a general way of proving these (and other) results. As we stated, the $p = 2$ case follows immediately from Parseval's identity. Likewise, the $p = 1$ case follows almost immediately. Thus we will now develop a general theorem that enables us to prove these types of results efficiently. 

More broadly, when establishing various estimates for PDEs, it can be easier to establish corresponding inequalities for certain ``endpoints'', and to ``interpolate'' in the corresponding estimates for all intermediate values. I.e., suppose one has the estimates $A_{0} \leq B_{0}$ and $A_{1} \leq B_{1}$. A natural question to ask is if for any $\theta \in (0,1)$, $A_{\theta} \lesssim_{\theta} B_{\theta}$. In this subsubsection, we will focus on estimates for linear operators between $L^{p}$ spaces, and we will give specific criteria under which interpolation in the above sense holds. In particular, we will answer the following question. 

\begin{quote}
	Suppose $(p_{0}, q_{0})$ and $(p_{1}, q_{1})$ are two pairs of indices with $ \leq p_{i}, q_{j} \leq \infty$. Assume also that $T$ is a linear operator and that, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{Tf}_{L^{q_{0}}} \leq M_{0}\norm{f}_{L^{p_{0}}} \qquad \norm{Tf}_{L^{q_{1}}} \leq M_{1}\norm{f}_{L^{p_{1}}}. 
	\end{equation*}
	Does it follow necessarily that $\norm{Tf}_{L^{q}} \leq M\norm{f}_{L^{p}}$ for other pairs $(p,q)$? 
\end{quote}


We begin with the following technical lemma. 

\begin{lemma}[Three-Lines Lemma]
	Suppose $\Phi(z)$ is a holomorphic function in the strip $S = \{z \in \mathbb{C}: 0 < \operatorname{Re}(z) < 1\}$, that is also continuous and bounded on the closure of $S$. If 
	\begin{equation*}
		M_{0} = \sup_{y \in \mathbb{R}}|\Phi(iy)| \qquad \text{and} \qquad M_{1} = \sup_{y \in \mathbb{R}}|\Phi(1 + iy)|, 
	\end{equation*}
	then 
	\begin{equation*}
		\sup_{y \in \mathbb{R}}|\Phi(t + iy)| \leq M_{0}^{1 - t}M_{1}^{t}, \qquad \text{for all $0 \leq t \leq 1$}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{lemma} 
\begin{proof}
	Let $\Phi$ be a holomorphic function in the strip $S = \{z \in \mathbb{C}: 0 < \operatorname{Re}(z) < 1\}$ that is also continuous and bounded on $\bar{S}$. If $\Phi$ is constant, then the claim follows trivially. So assume $\Phi$ is nonconstant. First, we assume that $M_{0}, M_{1} = 1$. We also assume that $\Phi(x + iy) \to 0$ as $|y| \to \infty$. By the hypotheses on $\Phi$, there exists some $M > 0$ such that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\sup_{z \in S}|\Phi(z)| = M. 
	\end{equation*}
	In particular, it follows that there exists some sequence $\{z_{n}\}_{1}^{\infty}\subset S$ such that $\Phi(z_{n}) \to M$ as $n \to\infty$. But by the hypotheses, the $z_{n}$ cannot converge to $\infty$, and thus, must converge to some $z_{0} \in S$. The maximum modulus principle and the fact that $\Phi$ is not constant forces $z_{0} \in \partial S$. Thus by the hypotheses on $M$, one has that $M \leq 1$. Therefore, the proof concludes under these restrictive hypotheses. 
	
	Now assume that $M_{0}, M_{1} = 1$, but that the decay rate is not necessarily satisfied. For each $\epsilon > 0$, define 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Phi_{\epsilon}(z) = \Phi(z) \exp\left(\epsilon(z^{2} - 1)\right). 
	\end{equation*}
	Moreover, it follows that on the lines $\operatorname{Re}(z) = 0$ and $\operatorname{Re}(z) = 1$, $|\Phi_{\epsilon}(z)| \leq 1$. Since $\Phi$ is bounded, it also follows that for each fixed $x \in [0,1]$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\sup_{x \in [0,1]}|\Phi_{\epsilon}(x + iy)| \xrightarrow{|y| \to \infty} 0. 
	\end{equation*}
	Therefore, the first part of the proof shows that on $\bar{S}$, $|\Phi_{\epsilon}| \leq 1$. Taking $\epsilon \to 0$ concludes the proof. 
	
	For the final part of the proof, assume the least possible number of hypotheses on $\Phi$, and define $\ti{\Phi} = M_{0}^{z - 1}M_{1}^{-z}$. Then $\ti{\Phi}$ satisfies the hypotheses of the previous case so that $|\ti{\Phi}| \leq 1$ in the strip, concluding the proof. 
\end{proof} 
\begin{remark}
	The geometric intuition for this lemma is clear to see. We specify bounds for $\Phi$ on the two vertical lines $\operatorname{Re}{\Phi} = 0$ and $\operatorname{Re}(z) = 1$. Then under the right hypotheses, the above lemma gives bounds for any third vertical line $\operatorname{Re}(z)= t$ between $\operatorname{Re}(z) = 0$ and $\operatorname{Re}(z) = 1$. 
\end{remark} 

From this result, we now have the following interpolation theorem for $L^{p}$ spaces. 

\begin{theorem}[Riesz-Thorin Interpolation Theorem]
	Suppose $T$ is a linear mapping from $L^{p_{0}} + L^{p_{1}}$ to $L^{q_{0}} + L^{q_{1}}$. Assume that $T$ is bounded from $L^{p_{0}}$ to $L^{q_{0}}$ and from $L^{p_{1}}$ to $L^{q_{1}}$: 
	\begin{equation*}
		\left\{
		\begin{alignedat}{3}
			&\norm{Tf}_{L^{q_{0}}} & \leq & M_{0}\norm{f}_{L^{p_{0}}}, \\
			&\norm{Tf}_{L^{q_{1}}} & \leq & M_{1}\norm{f}_{L^{p_{1}}}. 
		\end{alignedat} 
		\right. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Then $T$ is bounded from $L^{p}$ to $L^{q}$: $\norm{Tf}_{L^{q}} \leq M\norm{f}_{L^{p}}$, whenever the pair $(p, q)$ can be written as 
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{p} = \frac{1 - t}{p_{0}} + \frac{t}{p_{1}} \qquad \text{and} \qquad \frac{1}{q} = \frac{1 - t}{q_{0}} + \frac{t}{q_{1}}
	\end{equation*} 
	for some $t$ with $0 \leq t \leq 1$. Moreover, the bound $M$ satisfies $M \leq M_{0}^{1 - t}M_{1}^{t}$. 
\end{theorem} 
\begin{proof}
	Recall that the space of simple functions is dense in $L^{p}$ for all $p$. Thus, our strategy is as follows: (1) show that the result holds for all simple functions and for all $p < \infty$ and $q > 1$, (2) use the density of simple functions in $L^{p}$ to show the result for all $f \in L^{p}$ ($1 \leq p < \infty$), (3) show the result holds for $p = 1$ and $q = \infty$, and (4) finally show the result holds for $p < \infty$ and $q = 1$. We will also use the fact that to show that $\norm{Tf}_{L^{q}} \leq M \norm{f}_{L^p}$, it suffices to show that $\abs{\int Tfg\;dx} \leq M \norm{f}_{L^{p}}\norm{g}_{L^{q'}}$ for all simple functions $g$ with $\norm{g}_{L^{q'}} = 1$\footnote{Note: by $'$, we shall denote the conjugate exponent of an exponent.} \vspace{-0.25cm}
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label= (\arabic{*})]
		\item First assume that $p < \infty$ and $q > 1$, and assume $f \in L^{p}$ is simple with $\norm{f}_{L^{p}} = 1$. We wish to define some holomorphic function $\Phi$ that is bounded on $\operatorname{Re}(z) = 0, 1$ so that we can use the interpolation result for this function. Thus for $z \in \mathbb{C}$, define 
		\begin{align*}
			f_{z} &= |f|^{\gamma(z)}\frac{f}{|f|},  \qquad \gamma(z) = p\left(\frac{1 - z}{p_{0}} + \frac{z}{p_{1}}\right), \\
			g_{z} &= |g|^{\delta(z)}\frac{g}{|g|}, \qquad \delta(z) = q'\left(\frac{1 - z}{q_{0}'} + \frac{z}{q'_{1}}\right). 
		\end{align*}
		These definitions are useful for the following reasons. First, it is straightforward to see that for any $t \in [0,1]$, $f_{t} = f$ and $g_{t} = g$. Next, for $\operatorname{Re}(z) = 0$, we compute
		\begin{equation*}
			\norm{f_{z}}_{L^{p_{0}}}^{p_{0}} = \int \abs{|f|^{\gamma(z)}}^{p_{0}}\;d\nu = \int |f|^{p_{0}\cdot \operatorname{Re}(\gamma(z))} = \int |f|^{p} = \norm{f}_{L^{p}} = 1. 
		\end{equation*}
		Likewise, for $\operatorname{Re}(z) = 1$, we compute 
		\begin{equation*}
			\norm{f(z)}_{L^{p_{1}}}^{p_{1}} = \int \abs{|f|^{\gamma(z)}}^{p_{1}}\;d\nu = \int |f|^{p_{1} \cdot \operatorname{Re}(\gamma(z))} = \int |f|^{p} = \norm{f}_{L^{p}} = 1. 
		\end{equation*} 
		Likewise, one has 
		\begin{equation*}
			\norm{g_{z}}_{L^{q'_{0}}} = 1 \qquad \text{ and } \qquad \norm{g_{z}}_{L^{q'_{1}}} = 1. 
		\end{equation*} 
		Thus define 
		\begin{equation*}
			\Phi(z) = \int (Tf_{z})g_{z}\;d\nu. 
		\end{equation*} 
		Since $f, g$ are simple, assume 
		\begin{equation*}
			f_{z} = \sum |a_{k}|^{\gamma(z)}\frac{a_{k}}{|a_{k}|}\chi_{E_{k}} \qquad \text{ and }\qquad g_{z} = \sum |b_{j}|^{\delta(z)}\frac{b_{j}}{|b_{j}|}\chi_{F_{j}}, 
		\end{equation*} 
		where the $E_{k}$ and $F_{j}$ are sets of finite measure. This implies that 
		\begin{equation*}
			\Phi(z) = \sum_{j,k} |a_{k}|^{\gamma(z)}|b_{j}|^{\delta(z)}\frac{a_{k}}{|a_{k}|}\frac{b_{j}}{|b_{j}|}\left(\int T(\chi_{E_{k}})\chi_{F_{j}}\;d\nu\right) 
		\end{equation*} 
		so that (a) $\Phi$ is holomorphic on the strip $S$, and bounded, continuous on $\bar{S}$. For $\operatorname{Re}(z) = 0$, one has 
		\begin{equation*}
			|\Phi(z)| \leq \int|Tf_{z}||g_{z}|\;d\nu \leq \norm{Tf_{z}}_{L^{q_{0}}}\norm{g}_{L^{q_{0}'}} = \norm{Tf_{z}}_{L^{q_{0}}} \leq M_{0}\norm{f}_{L^{p_{0}}} = M_{0}. 
		\end{equation*}
		Likewise on $\operatorname{Re}(z) = 1$, one has 
		\begin{equation*}
			|\Phi(z)| \leq \int |Tf_{z}||g_{z}|\;d\nu \leq \norm{Tf_{z}}_{L^{q_{1}}}\norm{g}_{L^{q_{1}'}} = \norm{Tf_{z}}_{L^{q_{1}}} \leq M_{1}\norm{f}_{L^{p_{1}}}= M_{1}. 
		\end{equation*}  
		Therefore, by the three-lines lemma, one has that on the line $\operatorname{Re}(z) = t$ ($t \in [0,1]$), $|\Phi(z)| \leq M_{0}^{1 - t}M_{1}^{t}$. Thus this completes the proof. 
		
		\item Now fix some $f \in L^{p}$ for $1 \leq p < \infty$. Choose a sequence $\{f_{n}\}$ of simple functions in $L^{p}$ so that $\norm{f_{n} - f}_{L^{p}} \to 0$. Since $\norm{Tf_{n}}_{L^{q}} \leq M\norm{f_{n}}_{L^{p}}$, $Tf_{n}$ is a Cauchy sequence in $L^{q}$. Thus it suffices to show that $Tf_{n}\to Tf$ a.e. To prove this, let us define, 
		\begin{equation*}
			f^{U}(x) \coloneqq 
			\begin{cases}
				f(x), & \{x: |f(x)| \geq 1\}, \\
				0, & \text{otherwise}
			\end{cases} 
			\qquad \text{and} \qquad 
			f^{L}(x) \coloneqq 
			\begin{cases}
				f(x), & \{x: |f(x)| < 1\}, \\
				0, & \text{otherwise}. 
			\end{cases} 
		\end{equation*} 
		Then clearly $f = f^{U} + f^{L}$. Likewise, let $f_{n} = f^{U}_{n} + f^{L}_{n}$ for all $n$. Now wlog assume that $p_{0} \leq p_{1}$ so that $p_{0} \leq p \leq p_{1}$. Since $p_{0} \leq p$, and $1 \leq p$, 
		\begin{equation*}
			\int |f^{U}|^{p_{0}} \leq \int |f^{U}|^{p} \leq \int |f|^{p} < \infty, 
		\end{equation*} 
		so that $f^{U} \in L^{p_{0}}$. Since $f_{n} \to f$ in $L^{p}$, it follows that $f_{n}^{U} \to f^{U}$ in $L^{p_{0}}$. Thus there exists a subsequence $\{Tf_{n_{j}}^{U}\}$ that converges to $Tf^{U}$ pointwise a.e. Likewise, we have 
		\begin{equation*}
			\int |f^{L}|^{p_{1}} \leq \int |f^{L}|^{p} \leq \int |f|^{p} < \infty, 
		\end{equation*} 
		so that $f^{L} \in L^{p_{1}}$. Since $f_{n} \to f$ in $L^{p}$, it follows that $f_{n}^{L} \to f^{L}$ in $L^{p_{1}}$. Thus the sequence $f_{n_{j}}^{L} \to f^{L}$ in $L^{p_{1}}$ which means that $\{Tf_{n_{j}}^{L}\}$ converges to $Tf^{L}$ in $L^{q_{1}}$. This means there exists a subsubsequence $\{Tf_{n_{j_{k}}}^{L}\}$ that converges to $Tf^{L}$ pointwise a.e.; by construction, $Tf_{n_{j_{k}}}^{U}$ converges to $Tf^{U}$ pointwise a.e. Therefore, renaming this subsubsequence as $\{Tf_{n}\}$, we conclude that $Tf_{n} = Tf_{n}^{U} + Tf_{n}^{L}$ converges to $Tf$ a.e. so that $\norm{Tf}_{L^{q}} \leq M \norm{f}_{L^{p}}$. 
		
		\item Now consider the case $q = 1$ and $p = \infty$. Suppose first that $p = \infty$. Necessarily, it follows that $p_{0} = p_{1} = \infty$. Then H\:older's inequality concludes the proof. 
		\item Suppose $p < \infty$ and $q = 1$. Then $q_{0} = q_{1} = 1$. We can take $g_{z} = g$ for all $z$ and argue as in the case for $q > 1$. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{proof} 

In particular, the following definition highlights the key essence of the Riesz-Thorin theorem. 

\begin{definition}[Operator of Type $(p, q)$]
	Let $T: X \to Y$ be a linear operator that is initially defined on simple functions of $X$ and maps such functions to functions on $Y$ that are integrable on sets of finite measure. $T$ is said to be an \textbf{operator of type $(p, q)$} if there exists $M > 0$ such that $\norm{Tf}_{L^{q}} \leq M\norm{f}_{L^{p}}$ whenever $f$ is simple. 
\end{definition} 
Since the space of simple functions is dense in every $L^{p}$ space, it follows that if $T$ is an operator of type $(p, q)$ for some such pair, then the result extends easily to the entire $L^{p}$ space for $p < \infty$ ($p = \infty$ if $X$ has finite measure). We can denote the space of all such pairs $(p, q)$ using a \textit{Riesz diagram}.

\begin{definition}[Riesz Diagram]
	Let $T$ be a linear operator as defined above. Then the \textbf{Riesz diagram} of $T$ is the set of all points $(1/p, 1/q)$ in the unit square $[0,1]^{2}$ such that $T$ is of type $(p, q)$. We define $M_{x,y}$ to be the least $M$ for which $\norm{Tf}_{L^{q}} \leq M \norm{f}_{L^{p}}$ among all $(p, q)$ such that $T$ is of type $(p, q)$. 
\end{definition} 

For example, suppose $T$ is a linear operator that satisfies the Riesz-Thorin Interpolation Theorem for $(p_{0}, q_{0}) = (1,3.3)$, and $(q_{0}, q_{1}) = (1,4.6)$. Then the Riesz diagram for $T$ (as guaranteed by the Riesz-Thorin Interpolation Theorem) is the following, 
\begin{figure}[h!]
	\centering 
	\begin{tikzpicture}
		\draw[fill = black] (4,4) circle(1pt) node[anchor = north] {\tiny $(p,q) = (1,1)$};
		\draw[thick] (0,0) -- (4,0) node[anchor = north]{$1$}; 
		\draw[thick] (0,0) -- (0,4) node[anchor = east]{$1$}; 
		\draw[thick, dashed] (0,4) -- (4,4) -- (4,0);
		\draw[thick, dashed, ->] (4,4) -- (2.60606,2.43478261);
		\draw[very thick] (1.2121212, 0.86956522) -- (4,4); 
		\draw[fill = black] (1.2121212, 0.86956522) circle(1pt) node[anchor = north] {\tiny $(p,q) = (3.3,4.6)$};
	\end{tikzpicture} 
\end{figure}

We have the following corollary. 

\begin{corollary}[Riesz Convexity Theorem]
	With $T$ as before, \vspace{-0.25cm} 
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt, label = (\alph{*})]
		\item The Riesz diagram of $T$ is a convex set. 
		\item $\log(M_{x, y})$ is a convex function on this set. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary} 

Now we are interested in applications of this theorem. 

\begin{corollary}[Hausdorff-Young Inequality]
	Let $X = [0,2\pi]$ with the normalized Lebesgue measure $d\theta/(2\pi)$, and let $Y = \mathbb{Z}$ with its usual counting measure. Let $T$ be the map defined by $Tf = \{a_{n}\}$, wher each $a_{n}$ is the Fourier coefficient,  
	\begin{equation*}
		a_{n} = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{0}^{2\pi}f(\theta)e^{-in\theta}\;d\theta. 
	\end{equation*} 
	If $1 \leq p \leq 2$ and $1/p + 1/q = 1$, then 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{Tf}_{L^{q}(\mathbb{Z})} \leq \norm{f}_{L^{p}([0,2\pi])}. 
	\end{equation*} 
\end{corollary} 
\begin{proof}
	This is a classic example of a proof using interpolation. Since $[0,2\pi]$ has finite Lebesgue measure, one has that $L^{2}([0,2\pi]) \subset L^{1}([0,2\pi])$ and $L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}) \subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})$. Thus $L^{2}([0,2\pi]) + L^{1}([0,2\pi]) = L^{1}([0,2\pi])$, and $L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}) + L^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}) = L^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})$. 
	
	Now let $p_{0}, q_{0} = 2$. Parseval's identity implies that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{f}_{L^{p_{0}}([0,2\pi])} = \left(\sum_{n = -\infty}^{\infty}|a_{n}|^{2}\right)^{1/2} = \norm{Tf}_{L^{q_{0}}(\mathbb{Z})}. 
	\end{equation*}
	Thus the result follows immediately for this endpoint pair. Now suppose $p_{1} = 1$ and $q_{1} = \infty$. For any $n$, one has that 
	\begin{equation*}
		|a_{n}| \leq \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|f(\theta)|\;d\theta = \frac{1}{2\pi}\norm{f}_{L^{1}([0,2\pi])} \leq \norm{f}_{L^{1}([0,2\pi])}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Therefore, it follows immediately that $\norm{Tf}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})} \leq \norm{f}_{L^{1}([0,2\pi])}$. The Riesz-Thorin Interpolation Theorem then states that the result holds for all pairs $(p, q)$ with 
	\begin{equation*}
		p = \left(\frac{1 - t}{2} + t\right)^{-1} = \left(\frac{1 - t + 2t}{2t}\right)^{-1} = \frac{2t}{t + 1}, \qquad \forall t \in [0,1], 
	\end{equation*} 
	and $q$ the conjugate exponent of $p$; i.e., for all $1 \leq p \leq 2$ and $q$ the conjugate exponent of $p$. Thus the proof concludes. 
\end{proof} 

\begin{corollary}[The Fourier Transform]
	For simple functions $f$ on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$, define the \textbf{Fourier transform} of $f$ by 
	\begin{equation*}
		T(f)(\xi)= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)e^{-2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\;dx. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Then $\norm{Tf}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} \leq \norm{f}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$ so that $T$ has a unique extension to $L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ for which this inequality continues to hold (i.e., by the Hahn-Banach Theorem). By Plancherel's Theorem, $T$ also extends to $L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ as a unitary mapping. Thus, if $1 \leq p \leq 2$ and $1/p + 1/q = 1$, then the Fourier transform has a unique extension to a bounded map from $L^{p}$ to $L^{q}$ with $\norm{Tf}_{L^{q}} \leq \norm{f}_{L^{p}}$. 
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}[Young's Inequality for Convolutions]
	Let $f \in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ and $g \in L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Then the convolution, 
	\begin{equation*}
		(f \ast g)(x) \coloneqq \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x - y)g(y)\;dy
	\end{equation*} 
	is well-defined (i.e., $f(x - y)g(y)$ is integrable for almost every $x$), and 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{f \ast g}_{L^{q}} \leq \norm{f}_{L^{p}}\norm{g}_{L^{r}}, 
	\end{equation*} 
	where $1/q = 1/p + 1/r - 1$ ($1 \leq q \leq \infty$). 
\end{corollary} 


\subsubsection{The Hilbert Transform} 

We begin with the following observations for $L^{2}$. Let $f \in L^{2}$, and assume that
\begin{equation*}
	f(\theta) \sim \sum_{n = -\infty}^{\infty}a_{n}e^{in\theta}, 
\end{equation*} 
where the sum on the right is the Fourier series for $f$. We also define the \textit{conjugate function} or \textit{allied series} of $f$ as follows.

\begin{definition}[Allied Series]
	Let $f \in L^{2}$, and assume $f(\theta) \sim \sum_{-\infty}^{\infty}a_{n}e^{in\theta}$. Then we define the \textbf{conjugate function} or \textbf{allied series} of $f$ by 
	\begin{equation*}
		\ti{f}(\theta) \coloneqq  \sum_{n = -\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\operatorname{sign}(n)}{i}a_{n}e^{in\theta}. 
	\end{equation*} 
\end{definition}  
This specific definition is significant for the following reason:
\begin{equation*}
	\frac{1}{2}(f(\theta) + i\ti{f}(\theta) + a_{0}) = \sum_{n = 0}^{\infty}a_{n}e^{in\theta} = F(z), 
\end{equation*} 
where $F$ is the Cauchy integral (projection), 
\begin{equation*}
	F(z) = \frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{f(\theta)}{e^{i\theta} - z}ie^{i\theta}\;d\theta. 
\end{equation*} 
In particular, it is clear that if $f$ is real-valued (so that $\ti{f}$ is also real-valued), then $\ti{f}$ and $f + a_{0}$ represent, respectively, the real and imaginary parts of the boundary values of the analytic function $2F$ in the unit disc. Moreover, Parseval's identity then gives 
\begin{equation*}
	\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\ti{f}(\theta)|^{2}\;d\theta + |a_{0}|^{2}  = \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|f(\theta)|^{2}\;d\theta. 
\end{equation*} 
Now we would like to generalize this to other $L^{p}$ spaces. This leads to the theory of the \textit{Hilbert transform}. To make the transition clear, we begin with the $L^{2}$ formalism, and then use the Riesz-Thorin Interpolation theorem to generalize this result. We start with the following definition. 
\begin{definition}[Cauchy Integral]
	Let $f \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$. Then the \textbf{Cauchy integral} of $f$ is defined to be 
	\begin{equation}
		F(z) = C(f)(z) = \frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f(t)}{t - z}\;dt, \qquad \operatorname{Im}(z) > 0. 
		\label{eq:CauchyIntegralL2}
	\end{equation}
\end{definition} 
\begin{remark}
	The fact that we call $F(z)$ the \textit{Cauchy integral} of $f$ should look familiar. We recall that Cauchy's integral theorem tells us that if $\gamma$ is a closed contour in $\mathbb{C}$, $f(z)$ is holomorphic inside the region enclosed by $\gamma$ and on $\gamma$, and $z_{0}$ is a point contained inside the region enclosed by $\gamma$, then 
	\begin{equation*}
		f(z_{0}) = \frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\gamma}\frac{f(z)}{z - z_{0}}\;dz. 
	\end{equation*}
	For our analysis, we replace the unit circle with the real line $\mathbb{R}$, and the unit disk with the upper half-plane $\mathbb{R}_{+}$. Thus for any $z \in \mathbb{R}_{+}$ (i.e., with $\operatorname{Im}(z) > 0$), it would be reasonable to consider the quantity 
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f(t)}{t - z}\;dt. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Thus we denote this quantity the \textit{Cauchy integral} of $f$. 
\end{remark} 
The first thing we must check is if $F(z)$ is well-defined; i.e., if $F(z)$ converges for arbitrary $f \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$. We compute, 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		|F(z)| &\leq \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{|f(t)|}{|t - z|}\;dt \leq \norm{f}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\norm{\frac{1}{\cdot - z}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}. 
	\end{split} 
\end{align*} 
If we can show that $(t - z)^{-1} \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$, then we are done. Taking $z = x + iy$ (where $y > 0$), we have 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{|t - x -iy|^{2}}\;dt &= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(t - x)^{2} + y^{2}}\;dt \\
		&= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{u^{2} + y^{2}}\;du = \frac{\pi}{y} < \infty. 
	\end{split} 
\end{align*} 
Therefore, it follows that $F(z)$ converges for all $x + iy$ with $y > 0$. But there is also a more clever way of writing the Cauchy integral of $f$, 
\begin{equation*}
	F(z) = \int_{0}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)e^{2\pi i z\xi}\;d\xi, \qquad \operatorname{Im}(z) > 0. 
\end{equation*} 
This notation allows us to demonstrate the following, 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		\norm{F(\cdot + iy) - P(f)(\cdot)}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})} &= 
		\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\abs{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)\chi(\xi)e^{2\pi i x \xi}e^{-2\pi y \xi}\;d\xi - \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)\chi(\xi)e^{2\pi i x\xi}\;d\xi}^{2}\;dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}} \\
		&= \left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\abs{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)\chi(\xi)e^{2\pi i x \xi}(e^{-2\pi y \xi} - 1)\;d\xi}^{2}\;dx\right)^{1/2} \\
		&= \left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\abs{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(\xi)e^{2\pi i x \xi}\;d\xi}^{2}\;dx\right)^{1/2} \\
		&= \norm{\check{g}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})} = \norm{g}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}. 
	\end{split} 
\end{align*}
Using the definition of $g$, we have 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		\norm{g}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2} &= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}\chi(\xi)(e^{-2\pi y \xi} - 1)^{2}\;d\xi 
	\end{split} 
\end{align*}
Pointwise, in the limit $y \to 0^{+}$, the integrand converges to $0$. Thus by the Dominated Convergence Theorem, we conlude that $\norm{g}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})} \to 0$ as $y \to 0^{+}$. Therefore, we conclude that $F(x + iy) \xrightarrow{y \to 0^{+}} P(f)(x)$ under the $L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$ norm, where we define 
\begin{definition}[Orthogonal Projection Operator]
	Let $P: L^{2}(\mathbb{R}) \to L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$ be the orthogonal projection operator that projects each $f \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$ to a function $f'$ for which $\hat{f}'(\xi) = 0$ for almost every $\xi < 0$: 
	\begin{equation*}
		P(f)(x) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)\chi(\xi)e^{2\pi i x\xi}\;d\xi. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition} 
Now we define the \textit{Hilbert transform}; a motivation for the Hilbert transform will follow later. 
\begin{definition}[Hilbert Transform]
	Let $f \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$. Then we define the \textbf{Hilbert transform} of $f$ to be the function 
	\begin{equation}
		H(f)(x) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)\frac{\operatorname{sign}(\xi)}{i}e^{2\pi i x\xi}\;d\xi. 
		\label{eq:HilbertTransform1}
	\end{equation}
\end{definition} 
From the definition, we make the following observations: \vspace{-0.45cm} 
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\roman{*})]
	\item $P = \frac{1}{2}(I + iH)$, where $I$ is the identity operator. 
	\item $H$ is unitary on $L^{2}$, and $H \circ H= H^{2} = -I$; i.e., $\norm{H(f)}_{L^{2}} =\norm{f}_{L^{2}}$ and $H$ is invertible with $H^{-1} = -H$. 
\end{enumerate}
\begin{proof}
	$ $\newline \vspace{-0.65cm}
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\roman{*})]
		\item Let $f \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$. Then 
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split}
				\frac{1}{2}(I + iH)(f) &= \frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)e^{2\pi i x\xi}\;d\xi + \frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}(\xi)e^{2\pi i x\xi}\;d\xi \\
				&= \int_{0}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)e^{2\pi i x \xi}\;d\xi = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)\chi(\xi)e^{2\pi i x \xi}\;d\xi \eqqcolon P(f)(x), 
			\end{split} 
		\end{align*}
		where the integral over all $\xi \in (-\infty, 0]$ vanishes because of the $\operatorname{sign}(\xi)$ in the second integral.  
		\item Fix $f \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$. Then we compute, 
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split} 
				\hat{H}(f)(\xi) &= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}H(f)(x)e^{-2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\;dx \\
				&= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\eta)\frac{\operatorname{sign}(\eta)}{i}e^{2\pi i x \dot (\eta - \xi)}\;d\eta dx. \\
				\norm{H(f)}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2} &= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\abs{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)\frac{\operatorname{sign}(\xi)}{i}e^{2\pi i x \xi}\;d\xi}^{2}\;dx \\
				&= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\abs{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(\xi)e^{2\pi i x \xi}\;d\xi}^{2}\;dx  \\
				&= \norm{\hat{g}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2} = \norm{\hat{g}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}  = \norm{\hat{f}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2} = \norm{f}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}
			\end{split} 
		\end{align*} 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{proof} 

If we wish to extend the Hilbert transform to other $L^{p}$ spaces, then it is desirable for $H$ to be at least bounded (if not unitary). To work towards this goal, we have the following proposition. 
\begin{prop}[Hilbert Transform as a Singular Integral]
	If $f \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$, then 
	\begin{equation}
		H(f)(x) = \lim_{\epsilon \to 0}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{|t|\geq \epsilon}f(x - t)\frac{dt}{t}. 
		\label{eq:HilbertTransform2} 
	\end{equation}
	I.e., if for each $\epsilon > 0$, we denote 
	\begin{equation*}
		H_{\epsilon}(f)(x) \coloneqq \frac{1}{\pi} \int_{|t|\geq \epsilon}f(x - t)\frac{dt}{t}, 
	\end{equation*} 
	then $H_{\epsilon}(f) \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$ and the convergence is accordingly in the $L^{2}$ norm. 
\end{prop} 
\begin{remark}
	The proof needs to be incorporated. But the main idea is that for each $\epsilon > 0$, $\norm{H_{\epsilon}(f)}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})} \leq A \norm{f}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}$, where $A$ is independent of $\epsilon$ and $f$. 
\end{remark} 
In particular, the remark is suggestive of the following theorem of Riesz. 
\begin{theorem}[Riesz's Theorem for Hilbert Transform]
	Suppose $1 < p < \infty$. Then the Hilbert transform $H$, initially defined on $L^{2} \cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R})$ by either (\ref{eq:HilbertTransform1}) or (\ref{eq:HilbertTransform2}), satisfies the estimate 
	\begin{equation}
		\norm{H(f)}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})} \leq A_{p}\norm{f}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}, \qquad \forall f \in L^{2} \cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}), 
	\end{equation}
	where $A_{p}$ is independent of $f$. Thus the Hilbert transform has a unique extension to all of $L^{p}$ satisfying the same bound. 
	\label{thm:RieszThmHilbertTrans}
\end{theorem} 
\begin{proof}
	\textcolor{red}{[!! Complete Later !!]}
\end{proof} 

\subsubsection{Hardy Spaces}

Now we turn to the theory of the \textit{Hardy spaces}, $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Recall that many of the results we've been studying thus far have been based on $L^{p}$ space theory. Broadly speaking, we have been interested in one of the following three spaces: $L^{1}, L^{2}$, and $L^{\infty}$. But restricting ourselves to just one of these three spaces are indeed too restrictive. For instance, if $f \in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})$, then the only results we can say about their Fourier transforms is that they are bounded, and vanish at infinity (the \textit{Riemann-Lebesgue lemma}). But these transforms are not guaranteed to be integrable, nor do we have any other important constraints on their ``size''. But if $f \in \mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}$, then we actually have the following estimate (cf. exercises)
\begin{equation*}
	\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\hat{f}(\xi)|\;\frac{d\xi}{|\xi|^{d}} \leq A \norm{f}_{\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}}. 
\end{equation*} 
Thus the main advantage of working with $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}$ is that it is a Banach space that is, in some sense, ``close'' to $L^{1}$, while being more helpful in establishing estimates. Therefore, we will develop this theory now. 

 We begin with the notion of an \textit{atom}. 
\begin{definition}[Atom]
	$\mathfrak{a}$ is an \textit{atom} associated to a ball $B\subset \mathbb{R}^{d}$ if $\mathfrak{a}$ is a bounded measurable function on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ such that \vspace{-0.25cm}
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt, label = (\roman{*})]
		\item $\mathfrak{a}$ is supported in $B$, with $|\mathfrak{a}(x)| \leq 1/m(B)$ for all $x$, and 
		\item $\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}} \mathfrak{a}(x)\;dx = 0$. 
	\end{enumerate} 
\end{definition} 
Then, we define 
\begin{definition}[Hardy Space]
	The space $\mathbf{H}_{r}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ consists of all functions $f \in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ that can be written as 
	\begin{equation*}
		f = \sum_{k = 1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\mathfrak{a}_{k}, 
	\end{equation*} 
	where the $\mathfrak{a}_{k}$ are atoms, and the $\lambda_{k}$ are scalars with 
	\begin{equation*}
		\sum_{k = 1}^{\infty}|\lambda_{k}| < \infty. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition} 
Of course, the first thing we must check is if the above sum for $f$ actually converges. By definition of atoms, it follows that for each $k$, $\norm{\mathfrak{a}}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})} = 1$. Thus one has that the sum converges in the $L^{1}$ norm. We can turn $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}$ into a normed space as follows: 
\begin{definition}[Hardy Norm]
	Let $f \in \mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Then the $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ norm of $f$, is defined to be the quantity 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{f}_{\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\coloneqq \inf\left\{\sum |\lambda_{k}|: f = \sum_{1}^{\infty} \lambda_{k}\mathfrak{a}_{k}\right\}. 
	\end{equation*} 
\end{definition} 
We make the following observation. \vspace{-0.25cm}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\roman{*})]
	\item The space $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}$ is complete under the above norm, and thus is a Banach space. If $f \in \mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}$, then $f \in L^{1}$, and $\norm{f}_{L^1(\mathbb{R}^{d})} \leq \norm{f}_{\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}}$. Moreover, $\int f(x)\;dx = 0$. 
\end{enumerate}

We also have the following theorem. 
\begin{prop}[$L^p$, Bounded Support Functions and Hardy Spaces]
	Suppose $f \in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, $p > 1$, and $f$ has bounded support. Then $f$ belongs to $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ if and only if $\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\;dx = 0$. 
\end{prop} 

Now we will generalize the notion of atoms to include \textit{$p$-atoms}. 
\begin{definition}[$p$-Atoms]
	For any $p$ with $p >1$, define a \textbf{$p$-atom}, associated to a ball $B$ to be a measurable function $\mathfrak{a}$ which satisfies: \vspace{-0.35cm} 
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\roman{*})]
		\item $\mathfrak{a}$ is supported in $B$, and $\norm{\mathfrak{a}}_{L^{p}} \leq m(B)^{-1 + 1/p}$. 
		\item $\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathfrak{a}(x)\;dx = 0$. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}
Thus in defining $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}$, we no longer need to restrict ourselves to just working with atoms, and can instead also consider $p$-atoms, and obtain equivalent norms. Now, we arrive at another reason why the Hardy space is more desirable than $L^{1}$. Recall that the Hilbert transform is not bounded on $L^{1}$ (indeed, the transform is bounded on $L^{p}$ only for $1 < p < \infty$). However, we have the following boundedness result for Hardy spaces. 
\begin{theorem}[Hilbert Transform and Boundedness]
	Let $f \in \mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}(\mathbb{R})$. Then $H_{\epsilon}(f) \in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})$ for every $\epsilon > 0$. Moreover, $H_{\epsilon}(f)$ converges in the $L^{1}$ norm, as $\epsilon \to 0$. Its limit, defined as $H(f)$, satisfies 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{H(f)}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})} \leq A \norm{f}_{\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}(\mathbb{R})}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{theorem} 

\subsubsection{Maximal Functions} 
In this subsubsection, we introduce the notion of \textit{maximal functions}, which will become more useful when we investigate oscillatory integrals. We start with the following ``rough'' definition. 

\begin{definition}[Maximal Function]
	For appropriate functions $f$ given on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$, define the \textbf{maximal function} $f^{\ast}$ by 
	\begin{equation}
		f^{\ast}(x) \coloneqq \sup_{x \in B}\frac{1}{m(B)}\int_{B}|f(y)|\;dy, 
	\end{equation}
	where the supremum is taken over all balls $B$ containing $x$, and $m, dy$ denote the Lebesgue measure on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$. 
\end{definition} 

Then we have the following $L^{p}$ inequality. 
\begin{theorem}[$L^{p}$ Inequality for the Maximal Function]
	Suppose $f \in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ with $1 < p \leq \infty$. Then $f^{\ast} \in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, and 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{f^{\ast}}_{L^{p}} \leq A_{p}\norm{f}_{L^{p}}, 
	\end{equation*} 
	where $A_{p}$ depends on $p$, but is independent of $f$. 	
\end{theorem} 
\begin{remark}
	The proof for the case $p = \infty$ follows immediately: 
	\begin{equation*}
		|f^{\ast}(x)| = \sup_{x \in B}\frac{1}{m(B)}\int_{B}|f(y)|\;dy \leq \norm{f}_{L^{\infty}}\cdot \sup_{x \in B}\frac{1}{m(B)}\;dy = \norm{f}_{L^{\infty}}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Since this is true for any $x \in \mathbb{R}^{d}$, $\norm{f^{\ast}}_{L^{\infty}} \leq \norm{f}_{L^{\infty}}$. What about for the case $p = 1$? Let $f$ be the characteristic function of the unit ball $B$ so that $f \in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Let $f^{\ast}$ be the maximal function for $f$. Fix $x \in B$, and define $B'$ to be the ball centered at the origin of radius $1 + |x|$. Then 
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{m(B')}\int_{B'}|f(y)|\;dy = \frac{m(B \cap B')}{m(B')} = \frac{1}{(1 + |x|)^{d}}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Thus by definition of the maximal function, $|f^{\ast}(x)| \geq 1/(1 + |x|^{d})$. But the right side of the estimate is not integrable at infinity. However, we do have a class of \textit{weak-type inequalities}. 
\end{remark} 
\begin{prop}[Weak-Type Inequalities]
	Let $f \in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Then there is a bound $A$ (independent of $f$) such that 
	\begin{equation*}
		m(\{x: f^{\ast}(x) >\alpha\}) < \frac{A}{\alpha}\norm{f}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}. 
	\end{equation*}
	The proof follows using the Vitali covering lemma. 
\end{prop} 

Now we will turn to the application of Hardy space theory to maximal functions. The maximal function we defined above is not immediately applicable to here, since it requires the absolute value of $f$, and does not adequately exploit the properties of being contained in $\textbf{H}^{1}_{r}$. Moreover, the characteristic functions of balls is not smooth enough for our purposes. Thus we begin with approximations of identity. 

\begin{lemma}[Approximation to Identity]
	Fix $\Phi$ be a bounded function with compact support such that $\int \Phi = 1$, and for every $\epsilon > 0$, set $\Phi_{\epsilon}(x) = \epsilon^{-d}\Phi(x/\epsilon)$. Then for any $f \in L^{1}$, $$(f \ast \Phi_{\epsilon}(x)) \to f(x), \qquad \text{as $\epsilon \to 0$ for a.e. $x$}.$$ 
\end{lemma} 
Thus we define the \textit{maximal function} in the following new (albeit related) way. 
\begin{definition}[Maximal Function]
	Having fixed $\Phi$ as above, and $f \in L^{1}$, we define the \textbf{maximal function} of $f$ by 
	\begin{equation*}
		M(f)(x) = \sup_{\epsilon > 0}|(f \ast \Phi_{\epsilon})(x)|. 
	\end{equation*} 
\end{definition}
Thus we have the following theorem. 
\begin{theorem}[Maximal Function is a Bounded Operator]
	Suppose $\Phi$ is a $C^{1}$ function with compact support on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$. With $M$ defined as above, $M(f) \in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, whenever $f \in \mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Moreover, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{M(f)}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} \leq A\norm{f}_{\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{theorem} 
\begin{proof}
	Suppose $f \in \mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ and $f = \sum \lambda_{k}\frak{a}_{k}$ is an atomic decomposition. Then 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			|(f \ast \Phi_{\epsilon})(x)| &= \abs{\left(\left(\sum_{1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}\mathfrak{a}_{k}\right) \ast \Phi_{\epsilon}\right)(x)} \\
			&= \abs{\sum_{1}^{\infty}\left(\lambda_{k}(\frak{a}_{k} \ast \Phi_{\epsilon})(x)\right)} \\
			&\leq \sum_{1}^{\infty}|\lambda_{k}||(\frak{a}_{k} \ast \Phi_{\epsilon})(x)|. 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*} 
	Thus taking the suprema of both sides over all $\epsilon > 0$, we conclude that $M(f) \leq \sum_{1}^{\infty}|\lambda_{k}|M(\frak{a}_{k})$. Thus it suffices to prove the bound when $f$ is an atom $\frak{a}$. Now we observe the following property of atoms: for $r > 0$, if $\frak{a}_{r}(x) = r^{d}\frak{a}(rx)$, then $(\frak{a}_{r}\ast\Phi_{\epsilon})(x) = r^{d}(\frak{a}\ast \Phi_{\epsilon r})(rx)$ so that $M(\frak{a}_{r})(x) = r^{d}M(\frak{a})(rx)$. Moreover, the mapping $\frak{a} \mapsto M(\frak{a})$ commutes with translations. Thus, it suffices to assume that the atom $\frak{a}$ is associated to the unit ball centered at the origin. Now we consider the following two steps. \vspace{-0.25cm}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item ($|x| \leq 2$) Since $M(\frak{a})(x) \leq c$, $\int_{|x|\leq 2}M(\frak{a})(x)\;dx \leq c'$. 
		\item ($|x| > 2$) We start by writing 
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split}
				(a \ast \Phi_{\epsilon})(x) &= \epsilon^{-d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frak{a}(y)\Phi\left(\frac{x - y}{\epsilon}\right)\;dy \\
				&= \epsilon^{-d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frak{a}(y)\left[\Phi\left(\frac{x - y}{\epsilon}\right) - \Phi\left(\frac{x}{\epsilon}\right)\right]\;dy, 
			\end{split} 
		\end{align*} 
		where we used the fact that $\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frak{a}(y)\;dy = 0$. However, since $|x|\geq 2$, and $|y| \leq 1$, $|x - y|\geq |x|/2$. Moreover, since $\Phi \in C^{1}$ we have that 
		\begin{equation*}
			\abs{\Phi\left(\frac{x - y}{\epsilon}\right) - \Phi\left(\frac{x}{\epsilon}\right)} \leq \frac{c|y|}{\epsilon} \leq \frac{c}{\epsilon}. 
		\end{equation*}
		Since $\Phi$ has compact support, $(\frak{a} \ast \Phi_{\epsilon})(x)$ vanishes unless $|(x - y)/\epsilon| \leq A$ for some bound $A$. This then implies $\epsilon > |x|/(2A)$.
	\end{enumerate}
	Thus this concludes the proof. 
\end{proof} 
\paragraph{The Space $\text{BMO}$}
In this section, we introduce the space \textit{BMO}, which we will show is a natural substitute for the space $L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. We begin with the following definition of what it means for a function to be of \textit{bounded mean oscillation}. 

\begin{definition}[BMO Function]
	A locally integrable function $f$ on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ is said to be of \textbf{bounded mean oscillation} (\textbf{BMO}) if 
	\begin{equation}
		\norm{f}_{\textup{BMO}} = \sup_{B}\frac{1}{m(B)}\int_{B}|f(x) - f_{B}|\;dx < \infty, 
		\label{eq:BMOnorm}
	\end{equation} 
	where 
	\begin{equation*}
		f_{B} \coloneqq \dashint_{B}f(x)\;dx. 
	\end{equation*}
	$\norm{\empspace}_{\textup{BMO}}$ defines a norm on the space of all BMO functions. 
\end{definition} 
I.e., the space BMO consists of all locally integrable functions with the property that on any ball $B$, the average difference between $f$ and its average on the ball is bounded. We make the following observations about this space. 
\begin{itemize}[itemsep =-2pt]
	\item The null elements of the norm are the constant functions. To see this, assume $\norm{f}_{\text{BMO}} = 0$. Then on any ball $B$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_{B}|f(x) - f_{B}|\;dx = 0, 
	\end{equation*} 
	which means $|f(x)- f_{B}| = 0$ a.e. But this means that $f = f_{B}$ a.e. so that $f$ is constant a.e. 
	\item $L^{\infty}$ functions are contained in BMO. To see this, assume that $f \in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ be locally integrable. Then fix an arbitrary ball $B$, and let $f_{B}$ be the average of $f$ over $B$. Then 
	\begin{equation}
		\frac{1}{m(B)} \int_{B}|f(x) - f_{B}|\;dx \leq \frac{1}{m(B)}\int_{B}\norm{f}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} + |f_{B}|\;dx = \norm{f}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} + |f_{B}|. 
		\label{eq:bmo_Linfty_bound1}
	\end{equation} 
	On the other hand, one has that 
	\begin{equation*}
		|f_{B}| \leq \frac{1}{m(B)}\int_{B}|f(x)|\;dx \leq \norm{f}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Thus, since the upper bound for (\ref{eq:bmo_Linfty_bound1}) is independent of $B$, taking the supremum over all balls, we conclude that $\norm{f}_{\text{BMO}}\leq 2\norm{f}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} < \infty$. 
\end{itemize} 
The spaces BMO and $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}$ are actually more connected than it would originally seem. In particular, we will soon show that BMO is the dual space of $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}$. 

\begin{theorem}[BMO as the Dual of $\mathbf{H}_{r}^{1}$]
	Suppose $g \in \textup{BMO}$. Then the linear functional $\ell$ defined by 
	\begin{equation*}
		\ell(f) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)g(x)\;dx, 
	\end{equation*} 
	initially considered for $f \in H_{0}^{1}$ (the dense subspace of $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}$ consisting of finite linear combinations of atoms), has a unique extension to $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}$ that satisfies 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{\ell} \leq c\norm{g}_{\textup{BMO}}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Conversely, every bounded linear functional $\ell$ on $\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r}$ can be written in the form of the linear functional above with $g \in \text{BMO}$ and 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{g}_{\text{BMO}} \leq c'\norm{\ell} \coloneqq \norm{\ell}_{(\mathbf{H}^{1}_{r})^{\ast}}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{theorem} 
\newpage 

\subsection{Distributions and Generalized Functions} \label{subsec:distributions}

In this subsection, we introduce the notion of \textit{distributions}, which will become essential in our future study of nonlinear wave equations. Classically, we are used to the idea of a function $f$ (on $\mathbb{R}^{n}$) as assigning a definite value $f(x)$ for every $x \in \mathbb{R}^{n}$. But we also saw in measure theory that it suffices to define a function up to a set of measure zero (i.e., we can allow $f$ to remain undefined on points contained in a set of measure zero).\footnote{which is to say that a function is really just an equivalence class of functions that agree pointwise a.e.} Now, we will see that it suffices to relax this definition even more in certain cases, leading to the idea of \textbf{distributions} or \textbf{generalized functions}. Rather than assigning a definite value at each point in its domain, we define a distribution by its averages taken with respect to a certain class of test functions (e.g., $C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$). As we will see in Section \S.\ref{sec:lwe}, one might not have classical solutions of desired regularity (in the sense of ordinary functions) for various partial differential equations. However, there could exist generalized functions, that although do not necessarily have the regularity to satisfy the original differential equations, that satisfy other properties that a normal classical solution would have. For example, consider the one-dimensional wave equation, 
\begin{equation*}
	\frac{\partial u}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial u}{\partial x} = 0.  
\end{equation*}
Multiplying both sides by an arbitrary smooth function, $\varphi(x,t)$ with compact support, and integrating one has, 
\begin{equation*}
	\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}\varphi(x, t)\;dtdx + \int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}\varphi(x, t)\;dtdx. 
\end{equation*}
Using Fubini's theorem, and integration by parts, one obtains 
\begin{equation*}
	\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(t, x) \frac{\partial \varphi}{\partial t}\;dtdx + \int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(t, x)\frac{\partial \varphi}{\partial x}\;dtdx = 0. 
\end{equation*}
Thus any solution to the wave equation satisfies the above equation as long as it is at least $C^{1}$. However, it is possible that there exist functions $u$ that satify the above equation for all such $\varphi$ but is possibly not differentiable (so that $u$ does not satisfy the wave equation, itself). Hence, as we will see, distributions enable us to make sense of such $u$ (which we shall call \textit{weak solutions} to a differential equation). 

Thus we have the following definitions. 

\begin{definition}[Test Functions on $\Omega$]
Fix an open set $\Omega$ in $\mathbb{R}^{d}$. The \textbf{test functions} are those functions $\varphi \in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)$ (or equivalently, $C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)$) - the set of all smooth functions with compact support in $\Omega$. We denote the space of all test functions on $\Omega$ by the symbol $\mathcal{D}(\Omega)$. 
\end{definition} 

\begin{definition}[Convergence in $\mathcal{D}(\Omega)$]
	Let $\{\varphi_{n}\} \subset \mathcal{D}(\Omega)$ and $\varphi \in \mathcal{D}$. $\varphi_{n}$ is said to converge to $\varphi$ if the supports of $\varphi_{n}$ are contained in a common compact set, and for every multiindex $\alpha$, one has that $\partial_{x}^{\alpha} \varphi_{n} \to \partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varphi$ uniformly in $x$ as $n \to \infty$. 
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Distributions on $\Omega$]
	A \textbf{distribution} $F$ on $\Omega$ is a complex-valued linear functional $\varphi \mapsto F(\varphi)$, defined for all $\varphi \in \mathcal{D}$, that is continuous in the sense that $F(\varphi_{n}) \to F(\varphi)$ whenever $\varphi_{n}\to \varphi$ in $\mathcal{D}$. We shall denote the vector space of all distributions on $\Omega$ by $\mathcal{D}^{\ast}(\Omega)$. 
\end{definition} 

For example, all signed Radon measures on an open subset $\Omega$, and all locally integrable function on $\Omega$ are examples of distributions on $\Omega$. Our ultimate goal is to ``calculus'' using distributions. For smooth functions $f$, one of the most important results we have (as we shall see in the Section \S.\ref{sec:lwe}) is \textbf{integration by parts}: for any $f \in C^{\infty}(\Omega)$ and $\varphi \in \mathcal{D}(\Omega)$, a simple inductive argument yields, 
\begin{equation*}
	\int_{\Omega}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}f)\varphi(x)\;dx = (-1)^{|\alpha|}\int_{\Omega}f(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varphi)\;dx,  
\end{equation*}
where the boundary terms are identically zero because $\varphi$ has compact support. Thus, generalizing this definition, 

\begin{definition}[Derivative of a Distribution]
	If $F \in \mathcal{D}^{\ast}(\Omega)$, for any multiindex $\alpha$, one defines the \textbf{distributional derivative of $F$}, $(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}F)$ as follows: for any $\varphi \in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)$, 
	\begin{equation}
		(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}F)(\varphi) = (-1)^{|\alpha|}F(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varphi). 
		\label{eq:dist_der}
	\end{equation}
\end{definition}
Thus while an arbitrary locally integrable function $f$ may not have sufficient regularity to talk about its classical derivatives, we can certainly talk about its partial derivatives as distributions (which exist). 

We can generalize some basic properties of functions to distributions. 

\begin{definition}[Some Basic Operations on Distributions]
	Let $\Omega$ be an open subset of $\mathbb{R}^{d}$, and let $F \in \mathcal{D}^{\ast}$. Then we define the following. \vspace{-1.2em}
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt]
		\item If $\psi \in C^{\infty}(\Omega)$, then the product $\psi \cdot F$ is defined by $(\psi \cdot F)(\varphi) \coloneqq F(\psi \varphi)$ for every $\varphi \in \mathcal{D}$. It stands that $\psi \cdot F \in \mathcal{D}^{\ast}$. 
		\item Let $\tau_{\empspace}$ denote the translation operator: $\tau_{h}f(x) \coloneqq f(x - h)$ for any $h \in \mathbb{R}^{d}$. Then the corresponding definition for distributions is, 
		\begin{equation*}
			\tau_{h}(F)(\varphi) = F(\tau_{-h}\varphi), \qquad \forall \varphi \in \mathcal{D}. 
		\end{equation*}
		\item If $L$ is a \textit{nonsingular} linear transformation on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$, then if $f_{L}(x) \coloneqq f(Lx)$ for functions, on distributions, one has 
			\begin{equation*}
				F_{L}(\varphi) = |\det{L}|^{-1} \cdot F(\varphi_{L^{-1}}), \qquad \forall \varphi \in \mathcal{D}. 
			\end{equation*}
	\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

Another property we would like to generalize is convolution of a distribution with a test function. We make the following definitions: 

\begin{definition}[Convolution of a Distribution with Test Functions]
	Let $F \in \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, and $\psi \in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Then we make the definitions, \vspace{-1.2em}
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt]
		\item (As a function): $(F \ast \psi)(x) \coloneqq F(\psi^{\ti{}}_{x})$, where $\psi_{x}^{\ti{}} \coloneqq \psi(x - y)$, 
		\item (As a distribution): $(F \ast \psi)(\varphi) \coloneqq F(\psi^{\ti{}} \ast \varphi)$, for all $\varphi \in \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, with $\psi^{\ti{}} \equiv \psi^{\ti{}}_{0}$. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{definition} 

To see where these definitions could come from, assume first that $F$ is a locally integrable function. Then one has that 
\begin{equation*}
	(F \ast \psi)(x) \coloneqq \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(x - y)\psi(y)\;dy = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(y)\psi(x - y)\;dy. 
\end{equation*}
This leads naturally to the first definition. On the other hand, for any $\varphi \in \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, if $F$ is a locally integrable function, then 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		(F \ast \psi)(\varphi) &= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(y)\psi(x - y)\varphi(x)\;dydx \\
		&= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(y)\psi(x - y)\varphi(x)\;dxdy \qquad (\text{by Fubini's Theorem}) \\
		&= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(y)(\psi^{\ti{}}\ast \varphi)(y)\;dy  \\
		&= F(\psi^{\ti{}} \ast \varphi). 
	\end{split} 
\end{align*}
This leads naturally to the second definition. 

\begin{prop}[Equivalence of Definitions of Convolution]
	Suppose $F$ is a distribution and $\psi \in \mathcal{D}$. Then \vspace{-1.75em}
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt]
		\item The two definitions of $F \ast \psi$ given above coincide. 
		\item The distribution $F \ast \psi$ is a $C^{\infty}$ function. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}

We can also talk about distributions converging in $\mathcal{D}^{\ast}$. 

\begin{definition}[Weak Convergence of Distributions]
	A sequence of distributions $\{F_{n}\} \subset \mathcal{D}^{\ast}$ converges to a distribution $F$ in the \textbf{weak sense} (or in the \textbf{sense of distributions}) if $F_{n}(\varphi) \to F(\varphi)$ for all $\varphi \in \mathcal{D}$.
\end{definition} 

In particular, the following result follows. 
\begin{prop}[Approximating Distibutions with Smooth Functions]
	Suppose $F$ is a distribution on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$. Then there exists a sequence $\{f_{n}\}$, with $f_{n} \in C^{\infty}$, and $f_{n} \to F$ in the weak sense. 
\end{prop}

However, the space of test functions $C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ could potentially be too restrictive at points. Thus we generalize instead to the Schwartz space, $\mathcal{S} = \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ of \textbf{Schwartz functions}, which are smooth functions that rapidly decrease at infinity with all of their derivatives. I.e., we define 
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) \coloneqq \{\varphi \in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}): \sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^{d}}|x^{\beta}(\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\varphi)(x)| < \infty, \quad \forall |\alpha|, |\beta| \leq N \text{ and } \forall N \in \mathbb{N}\}. 
\end{equation*}
We denote, 
\begin{equation*}
	\norm{\varphi}_{N} = \sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^{d}, |\alpha|,|\beta| \leq N}|x^{\beta}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varphi)(x)|, \qquad \forall N \in \mathbb{N}; 
\end{equation*}
we say that $\varphi_{k} \to \varphi$ in $\mathcal{S}$ iff $\norm{\varphi_{k} - \varphi}_{N} \to 0$ for every $N \geq 1$. Accordingly, we make the following definition. 

\begin{definition}[Tempered Distributions]
	$F$ is said to be a \textbf{tempered distribution} if it is a linear functional on $\mathcal{S}$ which is continuous in the sense that $F(\varphi_{k}) \to F(\varphi)$ whenever $\varphi_{k} \to \varphi$ in $\mathcal{S}$. By $\mathcal{S}^{\ast}$, we shall denote the space of all tempered distributions on a domain. 
\end{definition}

Tempered distributions satisfy the following bounding criterion. 

\begin{prop}[Bounding for Distributions]
	Suppose $F$ is a tempered distribution. Then there is a positive integer $N$ and a constant $c > 0$, so that 
	\begin{equation*}
		|F(\varphi)| \leq c\norm{\varphi}_{N}, \qquad \forall \varphi \in \mathcal{S}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{prop} 
\begin{proof}
Assume that this bound is not satisfied. Then for every positive integer $n$, there exists $\psi_{n} \in \mathcal{S}$ with $\norm{\psi_{n}}_{n} = 1$, and $|F(\psi_{n})| \geq n$. For each $n$, set $\varphi_{n}= \psi_{n}/n^{1/2}$. This means $\norm{\varphi_{n}}_{N} \leq \norm{\varphi_{n}}_{n}$ for all $n \geq N$. 
In particular, $\norm{\varphi_{n}}_{N} \leq n^{-1/2} \to 0$ as $n \to \infty$. On the other hand, $|F(\varphi_{n})| \geq n^{1/2} \to \infty$, which contradicts the continuity of $F$. 
\end{proof} 

Now we recall that the Fourier transform of Schwartz functions are Schwartz. As a result, it follows that the Fourier transform maps the class of tempered distributions onto itself, so that $\mathcal{S}^{\ast}$ is closed under the Fourier transform. But we have not yet described what it means to take the Fourier transform of a distribution. 

Recall that for all $\varphi, \psi \in \mathcal{S}$, one has 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{\varphi}(x)\psi(x)\;dx &= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(y)\psi(x)e^{-2\pi iy \cdot x}\;dy\right)\;dx \\
		&= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(y)\psi(x)e^{-2\pi i y \cdot x}\;dx\right)\;dy \qquad (\text{by Fubini's Theorem}) \\
		&= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(y)\hat{\psi}(y)\;dy \eqqcolon \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\hat{\psi}(x)\;dx. 
	\end{split} 
\end{align*}
Thus, one seeks to generalize this to distributions as follows: 
\begin{definition}[Fourier Transform for Tempered Distributions]
	Let $F \in \mathcal{S}^{\ast}$ be a tempered distribution. Then the \textbf{Fourier distribution} of $F$, denoted by $F^{\wedge}$, is defined by 
	\begin{equation*}
		F^{\wedge}(\varphi) = F(\varphi^{\wedge}), \qquad \forall \varphi \in \mathcal{S}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition} 
In particular, the Fourier transform of tempered distributions satisfy the same properties as the Fourier transform for functions. 

\subsubsection{Important Examples of Distributions} 
\begin{example}[Principle Value]
	Consider the function $f(x)= 1/x$ for $x$ real and nonzero. Since $f$ is not integrable in any neighborhood of the origin, it follows that $f(x)$ does not represent a distribution on $\mathbb{R}$. However, consider instead the linear functional, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\varphi \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) \mapsto \lim_{\epsilon \to 0}\int_{|x|\geq \epsilon}\varphi(x)\;\frac{dx}{x}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	First, we must check that the limit actually exists. We may assume $\epsilon \leq 1$. Then we write 
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_{|x|\geq \epsilon}\varphi(x)\;\frac{dx}{x} = \int_{1 \geq |x| \geq \epsilon} \varphi(x)\frac{dx}{x} + \int_{|x| > 1}\varphi(x)\;\frac{dx}{x}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Since $\varphi$ is Schwartz, clearly the rightmost integral converges. On the other hand, since 
	\begin{equation*}
		A\int_{1 \geq |x| \geq \epsilon}\frac{dx}{x} = 0, 
	\end{equation*} 
	for any constant $A$ since $1/x$ is an odd function, we can write the first integral on the right side as 
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_{1\geq |x| \geq \epsilon}\varphi(x)\;\frac{dx}{x}= \int_{1 \geq |x|\geq \epsilon}\frac{\varphi(x) - \varphi(0)}{x}\;dx. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Then 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split} 
			\abs{\int_{1 \geq |x| \geq \epsilon}\frac{\varphi(x) - \varphi(0)}{x}\;dx} &\leq \int_{1 \geq |x| \geq \epsilon}\frac{|\varphi(x) -\varphi(0)|}{|x|}\;dx \leq c\int_{1\geq |x| \geq \epsilon} \frac{|x|}{|x|}\;dx < \infty, 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*}
	where $c = \sup|\varphi'(x)|$. Thus the limit converges; we denote the limit by 
	\begin{equation*}
		\operatorname{pv}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi(x)\;\frac{dx}{x}} \coloneqq \lim_{\epsilon \to 0}\int_{|x|\geq \epsilon}\varphi(x)\;\frac{dx}{x}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	In particular, one has that the principal value is bounded above by (some constant multiple of) $\norm{\varphi}_{1}$. Therefore, the above linear functional is a tempered distribution, which we shall henceforth denote by $\operatorname{pv}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)$. 
\end{example} 

\begin{example}[Principal Value and Hilbert Transform]
	Recall that we also defined the Hilbert transform in the form (\ref{eq:HilbertTransform2}); namely, 
	\begin{equation*}
		H(f) = \lim_{\epsilon \to 0}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{|y| \geq \epsilon}f(x  - y)\;\frac{dy}{y}  \eqqcolon \frac{1}{\pi}\operatorname{pv}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \ast f, \qquad \forall f \in\mathcal{S}. 
	\end{equation*} 
\end{example} 

\paragraph{Fundamental Solutions} 
Perhaps in these notes, the most striking application of distribution theory is to the notion of \textit{fundamental solutions} to partial differential equations. We begin by presenting a few key ideas of PDEs in this subsection. We start by defining \textbf{fundamental solutions}. 

\begin{definition}[Fundamental Solutions]
	Suppose $L$ is a the partial differential operator 
	\begin{equation*}
		L = \sum_{|\alpha| \leq m}a_{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\alpha} \qquad \text{ on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$}, 
	\end{equation*}
	with $a_{\alpha}$ complex constants. A \textbf{fundamental solution} of $L$ is a distribution $F$ so that $L(F) = \delta$, where $\delta$ is the Dirac delta function. Note that a fundamental solution is not necessarily unique since we can always add a solution to the homogeneous problem $Lu = 0$ to $F$. 
\end{definition} 
The following calculations show why using the fundamental solution is a ``good'' idea. Let $T: \mathcal{D} \to C^{\infty}$ be the operator defined by $T: f \mapsto F \ast f$, where $F$ is the fundamental solution to $f$. Then we claim that $LT = TL = 1$. Indeed, since $\partial_{x}^{\alpha}(F \ast f) = (\partial_{x}^{\alpha}F) \ast f= F \ast (\partial_{x}^{\alpha}F)$ and $\delta \ast f = f$, we have 
\begin{equation*}
	LTf = \sum_{|\alpha| \leq m}a_{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}(F \ast f) = \sum_{|\alpha| \leq m}F \ast a_{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}f = F \ast Lf = TLf. 
\end{equation*}
Thus the fundamental solution can be thought of as an ``inverse'' to a partial differential operator. 

Now, we are interested in actually obtaining the fundamental solution to various PDE explicitly. Thus we start with the following definition. 

\begin{definition}[Characteristic Polynomial]
	Let $L$ be the partial differential operator, 
	\begin{equation*}
		L = \sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}a_{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Then the \textbf{characteristic polynomial} of the operator $L$ is the polynomial, 
	\begin{equation*}
		P(\xi) = \sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}a_{\alpha}(2\pi i \xi)^{\alpha}. 
	\end{equation*} 
\end{definition} 
The significance is as follows: we know that if a fundamental solution $F$ exists for $L$, then $LF = \delta$. On the other hand, for any $f \in \mathcal{S}$, one has that $(Lf)^{\hat{}} = P \cdot \hat{f}$. This would imply that $1 = (LF)^{\hat{}} = P \cdot \hat{F}$. Thus it would be desirable to just proclaim the fundamental solution $F$ to be given by $\hat{F}(\xi) = 1/P(\xi)$, or 
\begin{equation*}
	F = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{P(\xi)}e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\;d\xi. 
\end{equation*}
However, the issues with this definition is that we must account for the zeros of $P$ (and the difficulty with defining $1/P$ as a distribution arising from the zeros). There are a few cases where this definition follows naturally. Consider, for example, the case of Laplace's equation. 

\begin{theorem}{Fundamental Solution to Laplace's Equation ($d\geq 3$)}
	For $d \geq 3$, the locally integrable function $F$ defined by $F(x) = C_{d}|x|^{-d + 2}$ is a fundamental solution for the operator $\Delta$, with $C_{d} = - \frac{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2} - 1)}{4\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}$. 
\end{theorem} 

\begin{theorem}[Fundamental Solution to Laplace's Equation ($d = 2$)]
	When $d = 2$, the locally integrable function $\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(|x|)$ is a fundamental solution of $\Delta$. 
\end{theorem} 


\subsubsection{Calder{\'o}n-Zygmund Distributions}

In this subsubsection, we cover some rudimentary principles of \textit{Calder{\'o}n-Zygmund theory}. For motivation, consider Poisson's equation $\Delta \phi = F$ under sufficient regularity assumptions on $\phi$ and $F$. Suppose that we wish to estimate the partial derivatives of $\phi$ (e.g., assume we wish to estimate the second derivatives of $\phi$). Taking the Fourier transform of the Poisson equation, we obtain 
\begin{equation*}
	-4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\hat{\phi} = \hat{F} \iff \hat{\phi} = -\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}}\hat{F}. 
\end{equation*}
Then to study the $L^{p}$ norms of $\partial_{ij}^{2}\phi$ for $1 < p < \infty$, it suffices to study $\mathcal{F}^{-1}(-4\pi^{2}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\hat{\phi})$, which we can write as
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(-4\pi^{2}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\hat{\phi}\right) = \mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\frac{\xi_{i}\xi_{j}}{|\xi|^{2}}\hat{F}\right). 
\end{equation*} 
We note that the kernel, $\hat{K} = (\xi_{i}\xi_{j})^{-1}/|\xi|^{2}$ is locally integrable, smooth away from the origin, is bounded, tempered, and satisfies the bound $|\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\hat{K}| \leq c'_{\alpha}|\xi|^{-\alpha}$ for all multiindices. Thus if $K_{ij} = \mathcal{F}^{-1}(\hat{K})$, by the convolution theorem, we have 
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\frac{\xi_{i}\xi_{j}}{|\xi|^{2}}\hat{F}\right) = K \ast F. 
\end{equation*} 
Therefore, if we can show that the operator $T$ defined by $TF = K \ast F$, initially just defined on $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, then we will have shown that $T$ extends to a bounded operator on all of $L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ (for all $1 < p < \infty$), from which we will have deduced the corresponding $L^{p}$ estimates on the second partial derivatives of $\phi$. 

The theory developed in this subsubsection will identify the specific hypotheses on $K$ that enables us to make such estimates. Therefore, the material in this subsubsection is an extension of our discussion of the Hilbert transform.  We begin with the following regularity assumption that we will be using throughout this subsubsection. 

\begin{definition}[Regularity Assumption]
	Let $K$ be a distribution on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$. $K$ is said to be \textbf{regular} in the Calder{\'o}n-Zygmund sense if there exists a function $k$ that is smooth away from the origin, $K = k$ away from the origin, and $|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}k(x)| \leq C_{\alpha}|x|^{-d-\alpha}$ for all $\alpha$ (in particular, when $\alpha = 0$, one has that $K$ is a tempered distribution). 
\end{definition} 

Moreover, we require the following definitions. 

\begin{definition}[$C^{(n)}$-Normalized Bump Functions]
	Fix an integer $n$. Then $\varphi$ is said to be a \textbf{$C^{(n)}$-normalized bump function} if $\varphi$ is supported in the unit ball $B_{1}(0)$, and for all multiindices $|\alpha|\leq n$, $\sup_{x}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varphi(x)|\leq 1$. 
\end{definition} 
\begin{definition}[Cancellation Condition]
	For any $C^{(n)}$-normalized bump function $\varphi$, define $\varphi_{r}(x) = \varphi(rx)$ for all $r > 0$. Then $K$ is said to satisfy the \textbf{cancellation condition} in the sense of Calder{\'o}n-Zygmund if 
	\begin{equation}
		\sup_{0 < r}|K(\varphi_{r})| \leq A, 
	\end{equation}
	for all such functions $\varphi$. 
\end{definition} 
Then we define \textit{Calder{\'on}-Zygmund distributions} as the following. 
\begin{definition}[Calder\'on-Zygmund Distribution]
	A distribution $K$ on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ is said to be a \textbf{Calder\'on-Zygmund distribution} if it is regular and satisfies the cancellation condition outlined above. 
\end{definition} 

\begin{prop}[Equivalent Formulations of Calder\'on-Zygmund Distributions]
	A distribution $K$ is a Calder\'on-Zygmund distribution on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ if and only if it is a tempered distribution such that $\hat{K} \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus \{0\}) \cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus \{0\})$, and $|\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\hat{K}(\xi)| \leq c'_{\alpha}|\xi|^{-\alpha}$. 
\end{prop} 

Then one immediately obtains the following result. 
\begin{theorem}[Calder\'on-Zygmund Decomposition Lemma]
	Roughly speaking, every integrable function can be split into a ``good'' and a ``bad'' term. I.e., for very $f \in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ and $\alpha > 0$, we can find an open set $E_{\alpha}$ and a decomposition $f = g + b$ so that: \vspace{-0.25cm} 
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\alph{*})]
		\item $\displaystyle m(E_{\alpha}) \leq \frac{c}{\alpha}\norm{f}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$. 
		\item $|g(x)| \leq c\alpha$, for all $x$. 
		\item $E_{\alpha}$ is a union $\bigcup Q_{k}$ of cubes $Q_{k}$ whose interiors are disjoint. Moreover $b = \sum_{k}b_{k}$, with each function $\operatorname{supp}(b_{k}) \subseteq Q_{k}$ and 
		\begin{equation*}
			\int |b_{k}(x)|\;dx \leq c\alpha m(k) \qquad \text{and} \qquad \int_{Q_{k}}b_{k}(x)\;dx = 0. 
		\end{equation*}
		I.e., $b$ is supported in $E_{\alpha}$, so that $g(x) = f(x)$ if $x \notin E_{\alpha}$. Moreover, each $b_{k}$ is of the form $c\alpha m(Q_{k})\frak{a}_{k}$, where $\frak{a}_{k}$ is a 1-atom. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem} 
I.e., the theorem tells us that every integrable function can be exressed as the sum of a good function $g$, which is bounded, and a bad function $b = \sum b_{k}$, where each $b_{k}$ is supported in a small cube so that the support of the bad function $b$ is contained in a measurable set $E_{\alpha}$ which is bounded in size. 
\begin{theorem}[Calder\'on-Zygmund Distributions and $L^p$ Estimates]
	Let $T$ be the linear operator on $L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, initially only defined on $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, defined by $Tf = f \ast K$, where $K$ is a Calder\'on-Zygmund distribution. Then $T$ extends to a bounded operator on $L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ for $1 < p < \infty$; i.e., for $1 < p < \infty$, there exists $A_{p} = A_{p}(p)$ such that for all $f \in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{Tf}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} \leq A_{p}\norm{f}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{theorem} 

Let us consider some examples. 

\begin{example}[Hilbert Transform]
	Recall the following definition of the Hilbert transform, 
	\begin{equation*}
		H(f)(x) \coloneqq \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-i\hat{f}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}(\xi)e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\;d\xi = \mathcal{F}^{-1}(-i\operatorname{sign}(\xi)\hat{f}(\xi)). 
	\end{equation*} 
	Consider the distribution $K$ such that $\hat{K} = -i\operatorname{sign}(\xi)$. It follows that $\hat{K} \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus \{0\}) \cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus \{0\})$. To show that $K$ is a tempered distribution, it suffices to show that $\hat{K}$ is a tempered distribution. Suppose $\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n = 1}^{\infty}, \varphi \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ such that $\varphi_{n} \to \varphi$ in $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Then
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			\abs{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{K}(\xi)\varphi(\xi) - \hat{K}(\xi)\varphi_{n}(\xi)\;d\xi} &\leq \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\hat{K}(\xi)||\varphi(\xi) - \varphi_{n}(\xi)|\;d\xi = \norm{\varphi - \varphi_{n}}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{n \to \infty} 0. 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*} 
	Thus, $\hat{K}(\varphi_{n}) \to \hat{K}(\varphi)$ so that $\hat{K}$ (and therefore, $K$) is tempered. This means that $K$ is a Calder\'on-Sygmund distribution. Therefore, by the convolution theorem, it follows that for all $f \in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ ($1 < p < \infty$), 
	\begin{equation*}
		H(f)(x) \coloneqq (f \ast K)(x). 
	\end{equation*}
	Thus by the preceding theorem, we conclude that the Hilbert transform is a bounded operator on $L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ for all $1 < p < \infty$, which was precisely the conclusion of Theorem \ref{thm:RieszThmHilbertTrans}. 
\end{example} 
\begin{example}[Estimates for Poisson's Equation]
	Consider again the Poisson equation, $\Delta \phi = F$, where $\phi$ and $F$ are assumed to be sufficiently regular and contained in the appropriate function spaces (e.g., assume $F \in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ for some $1 < p < \infty$). As we saw above, one has that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\hat{\phi} = -\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}}\hat{F} \qquad \text{and} \qquad \mathcal{F}^{-1}(-4\pi^{2}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\hat{\phi}) = \mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\frac{\xi_{i}\xi_{j}}{|\xi|^{2}}\hat{F}\right),
	\end{equation*}
	for all $1 \leq i, j \leq d$. Consider the distribution $K_{ij}$ on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ so that $\hat{K}_{ij} = \frac{\xi_{i}\xi_{j}}{|\xi|^{2}}$ is locally integrable. Since for any $1 \leq i, j \leq d$, $|\xi_{i}|\leq |\xi|$, it follows that $\hat{K}_{ij} \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus \{0\}) \cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus \{0\})$. It is also straightforward to check that the derivatives of $\hat{K}_{ij}$ satisfy the differential inequality stated in the definition of Calder\'on-Zygmund distributions. Thus it remains to be seen that $\hat{K}_{ij}$ is tempered. Let $\{\varphi_{n}\}, \varphi \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ so that $\varphi_{n} \to \varphi$ in $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Then we compute 
	\begin{equation*}
		\abs{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\xi_{i}\xi_{j}}{|\xi|^{2}}(\varphi - \varphi_{n})\;d\xi} \leq \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\abs{\frac{\xi_{i}\xi_{j}}{|\xi|^{2}}}|\varphi - \varphi_{n}|\;d\xi \leq \norm{\hat{K}_{ij}}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\norm{\varphi - \varphi_{n}}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} \xrightarrow{n \to \infty} 0. 
	\end{equation*}
	Thus this establishes that $\hat{K}_{ij}$ (and therefore, $K_{ij}$) is tempered; $K_{ij}$ is a Calder\'on-Zygmund distribution. From the theorem above, it follows that for any $1 < p < \infty$ and for any $1 \leq i, j \leq d$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{\partial_{ij}^{2}\phi}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} \leq A_{p}\norm{F}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	This gives us a $W^{2,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ regularity estimate for the Poisson equation. We can use this result to establish $W^{k + 2,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ regularity estimates for the Poisson equation. Start by assuming $F \in W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ for some fixed $k \geq 0$. Let $\alpha$ be a multiindex with $|\alpha| = k + 2$. Then if we let $\beta = \alpha - e_{i} - e_{j}$ (so that $|\beta| = k$), one has that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\phi}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} = \norm{\partial_{ij}^{2}(\partial_{x}^{\beta}\phi)}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} \leq A_{p}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\beta}F}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}. 
	\end{equation*}
	Then, by definition of the $W^{k + 2, p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ norm, one has that 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
		\norm{\phi}_{W^{k + 2,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} = \left(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k + 2}\norm{\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\phi}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\right)^{1/p}&\leq \left(\sum_{|\alpha| < 2}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\phi}^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} + \sum_{0 \leq |\beta|\leq k}C_{p}^{p}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\beta}F}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\right)^{1/p}  \\
		&\leq \left(\sum_{|\alpha| < 2}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\phi}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\right)^{1/p} + C_{p}\left(\sum_{0\leq |\beta|\leq k}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\beta}F}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\right)^{1/p} \\
		&= \norm{\phi}_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} + C_{p} \norm{F}_{W^{k, p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}. 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*} 
	Thus, we obtain the $W^{k + 2, p}$ regularity estimate, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{\phi}_{W^{k + 2, p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} \leq \norm{\phi}_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} + C_{p} \norm{F}_{W^{k, p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{example} 

\newpage 
\subsection{Oscillatory Integrals in Fourier Analysis}
In this subsection, we consider \textit{oscillatory integrals} and their applications in Fourier analysis. As we will see, the idea of oscillatory integrals are indispensable for PDEs, in particular, for dispersion equations. For example, consider the Klein-Gordon equation $\Box \phi - \phi = 0$, with compactly supported smooth initial data $(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}}$, on $\mathbb{R}^{1 + d}$. Then one can show that the solution $\phi$ satisfies the dispersion estimate, 
\begin{equation*}
	\sup_{x}|\phi|(t, x) \lesssim_{(\varphi_{0}, \varphi_{1})}\frac{1}{\left(1 + t\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}. 
\end{equation*} 
Likewise, for the linear wave equation, $\Box \phi = 0$ on $\mathbb{R}^{1 + d}$, one has the dispersion rate 
\begin{equation*}
	\sup_{x}|\phi|(t, x) \lesssim_{(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1})} \frac{1}{(1 + t)^{\frac{d - 1}{2}}}. 
\end{equation*}
Proving these estimates requires us to study the asymptotics of oscillatory integrals. Thus, we turn to the theory of oscillatory integrals in this subsection. 

\subsubsection{Oscillatory Integrals}

We begin with the following definition.

\begin{definition}[Oscillatory Integrals]
	By \textbf{oscillatory integrals}, we mean integrals of the form 
	\begin{equation*}
		I(\lambda) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\lambda \Phi(x)}\psi(x)\;dx. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition} 
The types of questions we are interested in studying is the behavior of $I(\lambda)$ for large $\lambda$. Here, we assume that both the parameter $\lambda$ and the \textbf{phase} $\Phi$ are real-valued, but in general, $\psi$ may be complex-valued. The main idea behind attaining decay estimates for these oscillatory integrals is the idea of \textbf{stationary phase}. Essentially, if the gradient of the phase is nonvanishing, then the integral is rapidly decreasing in $\lambda$ (and thus negligible). Therefore, the only meaningful contributions are from the critical points of $\Phi$ (i.e., when the gradient vanishes). We will see this procedure in detail over the course of this section. But we begin with the following opening estimate. 

\begin{prop}[First Estimate for Oscillatory Integrals]
	Let $I(\lambda)$ be the oscillatory integral from above. Suppose $|\nabla \Phi(x)| \geq c > 0$ for all $x$ in the support of $\psi$. Then for every $N \geq 0$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		|I(\lambda)| \leq C_{N}\lambda^{-N}, \qquad \text{whenever $\lambda > 0$}. 
	\end{equation*} 
\end{prop} 
\begin{proof}
	We start by considering the vector field 
	\begin{equation*}
		L = \frac{1}{i\lambda}\sum_{k = 1}^{d}a_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}} = \frac{1}{i\lambda}(a \cdot \nabla), 
	\end{equation*}
	where $a = (a_{1}, \ldots, a_{n}) = \nabla \Phi/|\nabla \Phi|^{2}$. This is because we then observe that 
	\begin{equation*}
		L(e^{i\lambda \Phi(x)})= \frac{a}{i\lambda}\cdot \nabla(e^{i\lambda \Phi(x)}) = \frac{a}{i\lambda}\cdot \left(i\lambda e^{i\lambda \Phi(x)}\nabla \Phi\right) = e^{i\lambda \Phi(x)}. 
	\end{equation*}
	Thus for any positive integer $N$, $L^{N}(e^{i\lambda \Phi}) = e^{i\lambda \Phi}$. We then compute the transpose $L^{\top}$ of $L$ to be 
	\begin{equation*}
