		L^{\top}(f) = -\frac{1}{i\lambda}\sum_{k = 1}^{d}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}(a_{k}f) = -\frac{1}{i\lambda}\nabla \cdot (af). 
	\end{equation*}
	Since we assume that $|\nabla \Phi(x)|\geq c > 0$ for all $x$ in the support of $\psi$, the $a_j$ and all of their partial derivatives are each bounded on the support of $\psi$. Thus, we compute, 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			|I(\lambda)| &\leq \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\abs{L^{N}(e^{i\lambda \Phi})\psi}\;dx  = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|e^{i\lambda \Phi}||(L^{\top})^{N}(\psi)|\;dx = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|(L^{\top})^{N}(\psi)|\;dx 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*}
	Thus by smoothness and compact support of $\psi$, we conclude that $|I(\lambda)| \leq c_{N}\lambda^{-N}$, where the prefactor $C$ follows from bounds on the derivatives of $a$ and $\psi$. 
\end{proof} 

In dimension one, we can actually get more precise estimates at the cost of simpler hypotheses. 

\begin{prop}[Oscillatory Estimate in One Dimension]
	Let $I_{1}(\lambda)$ be the following integral, 
	\begin{equation*}
		I_{1}(\lambda) = \int_{a}^{b}e^{i\lambda \Phi(x)}\;dx, 
	\end{equation*} 
	where $a$, $b$ are any real numbers, $\Phi \in C^{2}$, and $\Phi'(x)$ is monotonic (either increasing or decreasing) with $|\Phi'(x)| \geq 1$ on $[a, b]$. Then 
	\begin{equation*}
		|I_{1}(\lambda)| \leq c\lambda^{-1},
	\end{equation*} 
	for all $\lambda > 0$, with $c = 3$. 
\end{prop} 
\begin{proof}
	We use an operator $L$ similar to the operator used in the proof above. Without loss of generality, we may assume that $\Phi'(0) > 0$ on $[a, b]$ since the case for $\Phi' < 0$ follows by taking the complex conjugates. Then in this case, $L = (-i/\lambda) (1/\Phi'(x))\frac{d}{dx}$, and likewise, $L^{\top}(f) = \frac{i}{\lambda}\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{f}{\Phi'}\right)$. Then, we compute 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			I_{1}(\lambda) &= \int_{a}^{b}L(e^{i\lambda \Phi})\;dx = \int_{a}^{b}e^{i\lambda \Phi}L^{t}(1)\;dx + \left[e^{i\lambda \phi}\frac{1}{i\lambda \Phi'}\right]_{a}^{b}, 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*}
	where the boundary terms appear since the amplitude does not identically vanish at the endpoints. Focus first on the boundary terms. Since $|\Phi'| \geq 1$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\abs{\left[e^{i\lambda\Phi}\frac{1}{i\lambda \Phi'}\right]_{a}^{b}} \leq \frac{1}{\lambda |\Phi'(b)|} + \frac{1}{\lambda |\Phi'(a)|} \leq \frac{2}{\lambda}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	On the other hand, the first term can be majorized as follows: 
	\begin{equation*}
		\abs{\int_{a}^{b}L^{\top}(1)\;dx} \leq \frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{a}^{b}\abs{\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{1}{\Phi'}\right)}\;dx = \frac{1}{\lambda}\abs{\int_{a}^{b}\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{1}{\Phi'}\right)\;dx} = \frac{1}{\lambda} \cdot \abs{\frac{1}{\Phi'(b)} - \frac{1}{\Phi'(a)}}, 
	\end{equation*} 
	where the first equality follows from the fact that $\Phi'$ is monotonic and continuous on the interval $[a, b]$ and $|\Phi'(x)| \geq 1$ so that the derivative does not change signs on the interval. Thus this concludes the proof. 
\end{proof} 

We have a similar result when $\Phi$ has a \textit{nondegenerate critical point} at $x_{0}$. 
\begin{prop}[van der Corput's Estimate]
	Let $I_{1}(\lambda)$ be the oscillatory integral defined above. Assume $\Phi \in C^{2}([a,b])$, with nondegenerate critical point $x_{0}$, and $|\Phi''(x)| \geq 1$. Then for all $\lambda > 0$ and with $c' = 8$, the following estimate applies 
	\begin{equation*}
		|I_{1}(\lambda)| \leq c'\lambda^{-1/2}. 
	\end{equation*} 
\end{prop} 
\begin{corollary}
	Under the same hypotheses as in van der Corput's estimate, one has the estimate 
	\begin{equation*}
		\abs{\int_{a}^{b}e^{i\lambda \Phi(x)}\psi(x)\;dx} \leq c_{\psi}\lambda^{-1/2}, 
	\end{equation*} 
	where 
	\begin{equation*}
		c_{\psi} = 8\left(\int_{a}^{b}|\psi'(x)|\;dx + |\psi(b)|\right). 
	\end{equation*}
\end{corollary} 
While there are no direct analogs of the above strict estimates for $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ ($d > 1$), we have the following generalization. 
\begin{prop}[Generalization of Decay in Higher Dimensions]
	Suppose $\Phi$ and $\psi$ are smooth, $\psi$ has compact support, and $\det\{\nabla^{2}\Phi\} \neq 0$ on $\operatorname{supp}(\Psi)$. Then 
	\begin{equation*}
		I(\lambda) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\lambda \Phi(x)}\psi(x)\;dx = \mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-d/2}), \qquad \text{as } \lambda \to \infty. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{prop} 

\paragraph{Fourier Transform of Surface-Carried Measures}


\newpage 
\part{Nonlinear Wave Equations}
\section{Linear Wave Equation}\label{sec:lwe}

\subsection{Introduction} 
Through these notes, our goal is to study the properties of the nonlinear wave equation, which can either be a ``simple'' scalar equation or a system of equations \cite{Luk}: 
\begin{equation}
	\frac{1}{\sqrt{-\det(g)}}\partial_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{-\det{g}}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\phi\right) = F(\phi, \partial \phi) 
	\label{eq:nlw}, 
\end{equation}
where $g$ is a Lorentzian metric on $I \times \mathbb{R}^{n}$, and $\phi: I \times \mathbb{R}^{n} \to \mathbb{R}$ is an unknown function of some regularity. For example, if $n = 1$, and $g$ is the flat Minkowski metric, $\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)$, then the nonlinear wave equation is merely 
\begin{equation*}
	\partial_{\mu}(\partial^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\phi) = F(\phi, \partial \phi) \iff \partial_{t}^{2} - (\partial_{x}\phi)^{2} = F(\phi, \partial \phi). 
\end{equation*}
Note that we denote the coordinate of $I$ by $t$ (to designate the \textit{time} coordinate), and the coordinates of $\mathbb{R}^{n}$ by $x = (x^{1}, \ldots, x^{n})$. An equation of the form (\ref{eq:nlw}) is said to be \vspace{-1.2em}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\roman{*})]
	\item \textbf{linear}: $g$ is independent of $\phi$ and $F$ is a linear function of both of its arguments;
	\item \textbf{semilinear}: $g$ is independent of $\phi$  and $F$ is a nonlinear function; 
	\item \textbf{quasilinear}: $g$ is independent of $\phi$ and/or $\partial \phi$. 
\end{enumerate}
Before we build up to the nonlinear wave equation, we will first examine the linear wave equation. Generally speaking, we are interested in the following properties: \vspace{-1.2em}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\roman{*})]
	\item \textbf{Existence and Uniqueness}: Does a solution to (\ref{eq:nlw}) exist, and if so, is it the only solution? 
	\item \textbf{Dispersion}: What physical properties do solutions to (\ref{eq:nlw}) have? 
	\item \textbf{Well-Posedness}: If we consider the \textit{Cauchy wave problem} consisting of the equation (\ref{eq:nlw}) along with initial data $(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)$ on the hypersurface $\{t = 0\}$, where $\phi, \partial_{t}\phi$, does there exist a unique solution to the problem that satisfy the initial conditions and is continuously dependent on the initial data?
\end{enumerate}

We will gradually work through each of these results, sometimes developing new techniques and machinery. We first begin by developing some general analytic results, and idea of energy estimates. Then we will apply these to the study of linear wave equations. This will then give a foundation for the study of nonlinear wave equations. 




\newpage 
\subsection{Energy Estimates}

In this section, we introduce the notion of the energy of a solution to the wave equation. We begin by considering the homogeneous linear Cauchy problem, 
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{alignedat}{1}
		&\Box \phi = 0, \\
		&(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}), 
	\end{alignedat}
	\right.
	\label{eq:clwp}
\end{equation}
where we initially assume $\phi_{0}, \phi_{1} \in C_{c}^{\infty} \subset \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, and $\Box$ denotes the \textbf{wave operator}. As of now, we do not yet have an existence (or uniqueness) result for this Cauchy wave problem. Thus we simply assume that such a unique (sufficiently regular) solution exists for now. Then we define the \textbf{energy} of a solution to be the following $L^{2}$-type norm,
\begin{definition}[Energy]
	For $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ and $\phi$ the solution to the Cauchy problem (\ref{eq:clwp}), the \textbf{energy} of $\phi$ at the spacetime point $(t, x)$ is defined to be the $L^{2}$-type norm, 
	\begin{equation}
		E(t) = \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left((\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \sum_{1}^{n}(\partial_{j}\phi)^{2}\right)(t, x)\;dx. 
		\label{eq:energy_lwp}
	\end{equation}
	\label{def:energy}
\end{definition}
Intuitively, the definition for the energy is as we expected: consider an oscillating 1-dimensional string, and let $\phi(t, x)$ denote the amplitude of the wave at a point $x$ at time $t$. Then the quantity $(1/2)(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}$ resembles the kinetic energy of an infinitesimal point particle at the spacetime location $(t, x)$, while the quantity $\sum_{1}^{n}(\partial_{j}\phi)^{2}$ resembles the (spring) potential energy at $(t, x)$. Thus under these observations, $E(t)$ resembles the total energy of the system at some time $t$. Since the Cauchy wave problem (\ref{eq:clwp}) has no source term (i.e., is homogeneous), one expects the following result \cite{Luk}: 
\begin{prop}[Conservation of Energy]
	Let $\phi$ be a (sufficiently regular) solution to (\ref{eq:clwp}). Define $E(t)$ as given in Definition \ref{def:energy}. Then $E$ is constant in time. 
	\label{prop:cons_energy}
\end{prop} 

\begin{remark}
		We will give two proofs for this result; one relies on straightforward differentiation of $E(t)$ and follows the steps outlined in \cite{Luk}, while the other introduces the concept of conservation laws and follows the steps outlined in \cite{Lawrie}. 
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}[Proof \# 1]
	Let $\phi$ be a sufficiently regular solution to (2), and define the energy $E(t)$ of $\phi$ to be the function, 
	\begin{equation*}
		E(t) = \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left((\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \sum_{1}^{n}(\partial_{j}\phi)^{2}\right)(t, x)\;dx, 
	\end{equation*}
	where we assume that the initial data $\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}$ are both smooth with compact support. As we will see in a later section, $\phi \in C^{\infty}(I, \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$. Thus by the Dominated Convergence Theorem allows us to pass a time derivative inside the integral sign. We then obtain, 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			\frac{d}{dt}E(t) &= \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{tt}\phi) + \sum_{1}^{n}\partial_{t}(\partial_{j}\phi)^{2}\;dx \\
			&= \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi)\cdot \left(\sum_{1}^{n}\partial_{j}^{2}\phi\right) + \sum_{1}^{n}\partial_{t}\phi \cdot (\partial_{j}\phi)^{2}\;dx  \\
			&= \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi) \cdot \left(\sum_{1}^{n}\partial_{j}^{2}\phi\right) + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{j}(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{j}\phi)\;dx  \\
			&= \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi) \cdot \left(\sum_{1}^{n}\partial_{j}^{2}\phi\right) + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{1}^{n}\left[(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{j}\phi)\bigg|_{|x| = \infty} - \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{j}^{2} \phi)\;dx\right] \\
			&= \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi) \cdot \left(\sum_{1}^{n}\partial_{j}^{2}\phi\right)\;dx - \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi) \cdot \left(\sum_{1}^{n}\partial_{j}^{2}\phi\right)\;dx = 0, 
		\end{split}
	\end{align*}
	where the boundary term is identically zero since $\phi$ is Schwartz. Thus, the energy is conserved. 
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof \# 2]
	We start by claiming that solutions to (\ref{eq:clwp}) are the stationary points of a variational integral. Define the linear functional $\mathcal{L}: C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \to \mathbb{R}$ by 
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal{L}(\phi) = \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} - \sum_{1}^{n}(\partial_{j}\phi)^{2}\;dtdx; 
	\end{equation*}
	to obtain this functional, we can use the Euler-Lagrange equations for a physical system. Now let $\psi \in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^{n})$. We recall that $\braket{\mathcal{L}'(\phi), \psi}$, where $\mathcal{L'}(\phi)$ is the Fr\'echet derivative is defined as the directional derivative along $\psi$; i.e., 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			\braket{\mathcal{L}'(\phi), \psi} &= \frac{d}{d\epsilon}\mathcal{L}(\phi + \epsilon \psi)\bigg|_{\epsilon = 0} \\
			&= \frac{d}{d\epsilon}\bigg|_{\epsilon = 0} \left(\mathcal{L}(\phi) + \epsilon \int_{\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^n} (-\nabla \phi \cdot \nabla \psi + (\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{t}\psi))\;dtdx + \mathcal{O}(\epsilon^2)\right) \\
			&= \int_{\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}-(\nabla \phi) \cdot (\nabla \psi) + (\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{t}\psi)\;dtdx. 
		\end{split}
	\end{align*} 
	Since $\psi$ is smooth with compact support, and $\phi$ is Schwartz, using Fubini's theorem and integration by parts, and recognizing that the boundary terms are identically zero, one obtains 
	\begin{equation*}
		\braket{\mathcal{L}'(\phi), \psi} = -\int_{\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}(\Box \phi) \psi\;dtdx = 0. 
	\end{equation*}
	Thus since $\psi \in C_{c}^{\infty}$ was arbitrary, we conclude that $\phi$ is stationary for $\mathcal{L}$ iff $\Box \phi = 0$. Now, we recall N\"other's theorem, which states that if $\mathcal{L}$ is invariant with respect to a 1-parameter family of diffeomorphisms, then there exists a conservation law for an extreme value of $\mathcal{L}$. We note that $\mathcal{L}$ is time-translation invariant: if $G_{\epsilon}: \phi(t, x) \mapsto \phi(t + \epsilon, x)$, then one observes that $\mathcal{L}(\phi) = \mathcal{L}(G_{\epsilon}\phi)$. Therefore, since the vector field $\partial_{t}$ generates the flow corresponding to time translations, one has 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			0 &= \phi_{t}\Box \phi \\
			&= \phi_{tt}\phi_{t}- \Delta \phi \phi_{t} \\
			&= \partial_{t}\left(\frac{(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}}{2} - \frac{(\nabla \phi)^{2}}{2}\right) - \operatorname{div}(\nabla \phi \phi_{t}). 
		\end{split}
	\end{align*}
	Then, integrating over $\mathbb{R}^{n}$ yields, 
	\begin{equation*}
		0 = \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} - (\nabla \phi)^{2}\;dx\right) - \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\operatorname{div}(\nabla \phi \phi_{t})\;dx. 
	\end{equation*}
	The last term vanishes by using integration by parts and recalling that $\phi$ is Schwartz. Thus, one has that the energy is independent of time. 
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
	As the second proof shows, because of the time translation invariance, to show that the energy is conserved, it suffices to integrate the quantity $(\partial_{t}\phi)(\Box\phi)$ by parts. 
\end{remark}

Likewise, we have the following conservation law in Sobolev norms \cite{Holzegel2021}. 

\begin{corollary}[Sobolev Conservation Law I]
	Given initial data $(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, and assuming the existence of a solution $\phi \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}; \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ to the Cauchy wave problem (\ref{eq:clwp}), for all $s \in \mathbb{R}$, 
	\begin{equation}
		\norm{\partial_{t}\phi}_{H^{s - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} + \norm{\nabla \phi}_{H^{s - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} = \norm{\phi_{0}}_{H^{s - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} + \norm{\nabla \phi_{1}}_{H^{s - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}. 
	\end{equation}   
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
	Assume that $\phi \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}; \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ is a solution to (\ref{eq:clwp}) with initial data $(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$. If $\hat{\empspace}$ denotes the Fourier transform of $\empspace$, then one has the equation
	\begin{equation*}
		(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + 4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}(\hat{\phi})^{2} = (\hat{\phi}_{0})^{2} + 4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}(\hat{\phi}_{1})^{2}.
	\end{equation*} 
	Note, this equation becomes clearer once we establish a representation formula for $\phi$ in a later section. Multiplying through by $(1 + |\xi|^{2})^{s - 1}$ (for arbitrary $s \in \mathbb{R}$), and integrating, yields the desired conservation law. 
\end{proof}

One immediate advantage of having conservation laws is that if the Cauchy problem (\ref{eq:clwp}) has a solution, then these laws immediately imply uniqueness. 

\begin{corollary}[Uniqueness I]
	Given initial data $(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright|_{\{t = 0\}}= (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, if we have two solutions $\phi^{(1)}$ and $\phi^{(2)}$ to the linear wave equation (\ref{eq:clwp}) with the given data such that both of them satisfy $(\phi^{(i)}, \partial_{t}\phi^{(i)}) \in L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ for $i = 1, 2$, then $\phi^{(1)} = \phi^{(2)}$ almost everywhere in $[0,T] \times \mathbb{R}^{n}$. 
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The immediate difficulty with trying to use conservation of energy to prove uniqueness of solutions under the regularity assumptions made above is that we do not have enough regularity in the time variable to justify passing the time derivative inside the integral when differentiating $E(t)$. Thus we must use a mollification argument. First, let us assume there exist two solutions $\phi^{(1)}, \phi^{(2)} \in L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ to the linear wave equation, where both solution satisfy the initial data $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1})$. Define $\phi = \phi^{(1)} - \phi^{(2)}$, which solves the linear wave equation for initial data that are identically zero. For now, we provide a heuristic argument, with a rigorous statement to be filled in afterwards. The idea is that we can consider mollifying $\phi$ with temporal mollifiers $\varphi_{\epsilon}$ for each $\epsilon > 0$. Then for each $\epsilon > 0$, one has that $\phi_{\epsilon} = \phi \ast \varphi_{\epsilon}$ is smooth and the energy conservation applies. Thus passing to the limit $\epsilon \to 0$ would enable us to conclude that $\phi$ is identically zero, yielding uniqueness. 
\end{proof} 

To extract more useful information, we can localize the energy identity, thus obtaining the following result. 

\begin{prop}[Localized Energy Identity]
	Define, for $0 \leq t \leq R$, 
	\begin{equation}
		E(t;x, R) \coloneqq \frac{1}{2}\int_{B(x, R - t)}\left((\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \sum_{1}^{n}(\partial_{j}\phi)^{2}\right)(t, y)\;dy. 
		\label{eq:local_e_id}
	\end{equation}
	Then $E(t;x, R)$ is a nonincreasing function of $t$. 
\end{prop} 

\begin{remark}
	This result tells us that the energy at time $t$ is less than or equal to the energy at time $s > t$ in the backwards light cone with vertex at $x$. 
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}
	As suggested in the second proof of Proposition \ref{prop:cons_energy}, we will start with the expression $(\partial_{t}\phi)\Box\phi = 0$ and integrate by parts in the region to the future of $\{t_{1}\} \times B(x, R - t_{1})$ and to the past of $(\{t_{2}\} \times B(x, R - t_{2})) \cup \left(\bigcup_{s \in [t_{1}, t_{2}]}(\{s\} \times \partial B(x, R - s))\right)$. The idea behind this choice is that the boundary terms conveniently give us $E(t_{1}; x, R)$ and $E(t_{2}; x, R)$. The boundary term on $\bigcup_{s \in [t_{1}, t_{2}]}(\{s\} \times \partial B(x, R - s))$ then has a definite sign, which then allows us to compare the final and initial energy. Indeed, one has the following computations, 
	\begin{multline*}
			0 = \int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{B(x, R - t)}\left((\partial_{t}\phi)\left(-\partial_{t}^{2}\phi + \sum_{1}^{n}\partial_{j}^{2}\phi\right)\right)(t,y)\;dydt 
			\\ = -\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}} \int_{B(x, R - t)}\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{1}^{n}(\partial_{j}\phi)^{2}\right)\;dydt
			 + \sum_{1}^{n}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{B(x, R- t)}\partial_{j}((\partial_{t}\phi) \cdot (\partial_{j}\phi))\;dydt \\
			= -\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{B(x, R - t)}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+ \Delta \phi \right]\;dt
			 - \frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\partial B(x, R - t)}\left((\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \sum_{1}^{n}(\partial_{j}\phi)^{2}\right)
			\\ + \int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\partial B(x, R - t)}(\partial_{t}\phi) \cdot \sum_{1}^{n}(\partial_{j}\phi) \cdot \frac{(y- x)_{j}}{|y - x|}\;d\sigma(y)dt \\
			= -E(t_{2}; x, R) + E(t_{1}; x, R) 
			- \frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\partial B(x, R - t)}\left((\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \sum_{1}^{n}(\partial_{j}\phi)^{2} - 2(\partial_{t}\phi) \cdot \sum_{1}^{n}\frac{(y -x)_{j}}{|y - x|} (\partial_{j}\phi)\right)\;d\sigma(y)dt. 
	\end{multline*}
	where the second equality follows from Theorem C.4.6 of \cite{evans10}. Now we estimate the final term of the last equation. Cauchy-Schwarz tells us the following (CS arises in the second line), 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			2(\partial_{t}\phi) \cdot \sum_{1}^{n}\frac{(y - x)_{j}}{|y - x|}(\partial_{j}\phi)&\leq (\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \left(\sum_{1}^{n}\frac{(y - x)_{j}}{|y - x|}(\partial_{j}\phi)\right)^{2}\\
			&\leq (\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \left(\sum_{1}^{n}\frac{(y - x)_{j}}{|y -x|}\right)^{2} \cdot \left(\sum_{1}^{n}(\partial_{j}\phi)^{2}\right) \\
			&= (\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \sum_{1}^{n}(\partial_{j}\phi)^{2}. 
		\end{split}
	\end{align*}
	Thus this implies that the final term is negative. So then, one must have $-E(t_{2}; x, R) + E(t_{1}; x, R) \geq 0$, whence $E(t_{2}; x, R) \leq E(t_{1}; x, R)$. 
	\end{proof}
	
While we are at it, we also consider the following linear, non-constant coefficient wave equation, 
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{alignedat}{1}
		&\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi) = F, 	\\
		&(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}), 
	\end{alignedat}
	\right.
	\label{eq:clwp_ncc} 
\end{equation}
where $a$ is assumed to be a symmetric $(n + 1)\times (n + 1)$ matrix on $I \times \mathbb{R}^{n}$ satisfying 
\begin{equation*}
	\sum_{\alpha,\beta}|a^{\alpha\beta} - m^{\alpha\beta}| < \frac{1}{10}. 
\end{equation*}
While the energy is not conserved, in general, due to the source term $F$, the growth of the energy is controlled in the following sense. 

\begin{theorem}[Growth Bound on Energy]
	Assume $\phi$ is a solution to (\ref{eq:clwp_ncc}) of some sufficient regularity; let $E(t)$ be the energy of $\phi$ in the sense of Proposition (\ref{prop:cons_energy}). Then there exists a positive constant $C = C(n) > 0$ such that the following energy estimate holds 
	\begin{equation}
		\norm{E^{1/2}}_{C([0,T])} =  \leq C\left(\norm{(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1})}_{\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} + \int_{0}^{T}\norm{F}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right)\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{T}\norm{\partial a}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right), 
		\label{eq:clwp_nc_ebound}
	\end{equation} 
	\label{thm:clwp_nc_ebound}
\end{theorem}
where by $\partial \empspace$, we denote $(\partial_{t} \empspace)^{2} + \Delta \empspace$. 

To prove this result, we first require \textbf{Gr\"onwall's lemma}. 

\begin{lemma}[Gr\"onwall's Lemma]
	Let $f: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ be a nonnegative continuous function and $g: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ be a nonnegative integrable function such that 
	\begin{equation*}
		f(t) \leq A + \int_{0}^{t}f(s)g(s)\;ds
	\end{equation*}
	holds for some $A \geq 0$, and every $t \in [0,T]$. Then 
	\begin{equation*}
		f(t) \leq A \exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\;ds\right)
	\end{equation*}
	for every $t \in [0,T]$. 
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	$ $\newline \vspace{-0.65cm}
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\arabic{*})]
		\item (Standard Proof): The proof follows by showing that the result holds for any $A > 0$, and then passing to the limit $A \to 0$. First, we compute 
		\begin{equation*}
			\frac{d}{dt}\left(A + \int_{0}^{t}f(s)g(s)\;ds\right) = f(t)g(t) \leq g(t)\left(A + \int_{0}^{t}f(s)g(s)\;ds\right). 
		\end{equation*}
		In particular, this implies that 
		\begin{equation*}
			\frac{d}{dr}\log\left[A + \int_{0}^{t}f(s)g(s)\;ds\right] \leq g(r), 
		\end{equation*}
		so that integrating, 
		\begin{equation*}
			\log\left[A + \int_{0}^{t}f(s)g(s)\;ds\right] \leq \int_{0}^{t}g(r)\;dr + \log{A}. 
		\end{equation*}
		Thus, we conclude that 
		\begin{equation*}
			f(t) \leq A + \int_{0}^{t}f(s)g(s)\;ds \leq A\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}g(r)\;dr\right). 
		\end{equation*}
		\item (Bootstrap Method): Now we will prove the same result again, using the \textbf{bootstrap} method. For each $\epsilon > 0$, consider the following condition: 
		\begin{equation*}
			f(t) \leq (1 + \epsilon)A\exp\left((1 + \epsilon)\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\;ds\right), 
			\tag{$\star$}
		\end{equation*}
		and define the subset $B \subseteq [0,T]$ by 
		\begin{equation*}
			B \coloneqq \{t \in [0,T]: (\star) \text{ holds for all $s \in [0,t]$}\}. 
		\end{equation*}
		It follows immediately that $B$ is nonempty as it at least contains the point $0 \in B$. Likewise, by continuity of $f$, it follows that $B$ is closed. By continuity of $f$, if we can show that $f$ satisfies a \textit{better} estimate for $t \in B$, then it must follow that $f$ satisfies the above bound in some small neighborhood of $t$, which would show that $B$ is open in $[0,T]$. Indeed, let $t \in B$. Then combining the hypothesis, one has that 
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split}
				f(t) &\leq A + \int_{0}^{t}g(s)\;ds \leq A + (1 + \epsilon)A\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\exp\left((1 + \epsilon)\int_{0}^{s}g(r)\;dr\right)\;ds \\
				&\leq A\left(1 + \left(\exp\left((1 + \epsilon)\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\;ds\right)-1\right)\right) \qquad (\text{by integration by parts}) \\
				&= A\exp\left((1 + \epsilon)\int_{0}^{t}g(s)\;ds\right). 
			\end{split}
		\end{align*}
		Thus, this shows that $B$ must be open. Since $[0,T]$ is connected, one must have $B = [0,T]$. Hence the modified condition holds for all $t \in [0,T]$ and $\epsilon > 0$ so that passing to the limit $\epsilon \to 0$ yields the desired estimate. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

Now we will use Gr\"onwall's lemma to prove the energy estimate from above. 

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{thm:clwp_nc_ebound}]
	For the sake of this proof, we will be assuming that $\phi$ is compactly supported so that we can ignore the spatial boundary terms in the integration. The fundamental idea behind the techniques in this proof is to bound the energy of the solution on the time interval $[0,T]$ by looking at the total energy at some fixed time, and then summing these contributions. In other words, we wish to integrate the first term of the quantity, $\partial_{t}\phi(\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi) - F)$ by parts. Before doing so, we make the following observations. 
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\arabic{*})]
		\item Suppose first that $\alpha = \beta = 0$. Then one has that 
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split}
				\partial_{t}\phi(\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi)) &= (\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{t}(a^{tt}\partial_{t}\phi)) \\
				&= (\partial_{t}\phi)((\partial_{t}a^{tt})(\partial_{t}\phi)) + a^{tt}(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{tt}\phi) \\
				&= (\partial_{t}a^{tt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \frac{1}{2}a^{tt}\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}, 
			\end{split}
		\end{align*}
		so that 
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split}
				\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{t}(a^{tt}\partial_{t}\phi))\;dxdt &= \int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}} (\partial_{t}a^{tt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \frac{1}{2}a^{tt}\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}\;dx dt \\
				&= \frac{1}{2}\int_{\{T\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{tt}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}\;dx - \frac{1}{2}\int_{\{0\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{tt}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}\;dx \\
				&\qquad + \frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}a^{tt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}\;dxdt, 
			\end{split}
		\end{align*}
		where the second line follows from Fubini/Tonelli, and the fundamental theorem of calculus. 
		
		\item Now assume that one of the indices is $t$, while the other index runs from $j = 1, \ldots, n$. Then one has that 
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split}
				\partial_{t}\phi(\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi)) &= (\partial_{t}\phi) \cdot \left[\partial_{j}(a^{jt}\partial_{t}\phi) + \partial_{t}(a^{tj}\partial_{j}\phi)\right] \\
				&= (\partial_{t}\phi) \cdot \left[(\partial_{j}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi) + a^{jt}\partial_{j}(\partial_{t}\phi) + \partial_{t}(a^{jt})(\partial_{j}\phi) + a^{jt}\partial_{j}(\partial_{t}\phi)\right] \\
				&= (\partial_{j}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + a^{jt}\partial_{j}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + (\partial_{t}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{j}\phi). 
			\end{split}
		\end{align*}
		Observe that $a^{jt} = a^{tj}$ by symmetry of $a$. We first consider the integral of the middle term. Integration by parts tells us that 
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split}
				\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{jt}\partial_{j}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}\;dx dt &= \int_{0}^{T} \left[a^{jt}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}|_{\text{bdr.}} - \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{j}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}\;dx\right]\;dt \\
				&= -\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{j}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}\;dxdt, 
			\end{split}
		\end{align*}
		where the boundary terms vanish because of our assumption that $\phi$ is compactly supported. Thus in fact, 
		\begin{equation*}
			\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{t}\phi(\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi))\;dxdt = \int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{j}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} - (\partial_{j}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + (\partial_{t}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{j}\phi)\;dx dt. 
		\end{equation*}
		\item Now assume that both indices run over $i, j = 1, \ldots, n$. Then one has that  
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split}
				\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{t}\phi (\partial_{i}(a^{ij}\partial_{j}\phi))\;dxdt &= (\partial_{t}\phi)(a^{ij}\partial_{j}\phi)\bigg|_{\text{bdr.}} - \int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{t}\partial_{i}\phi a^{ij}\partial_{j}\phi\;dx dt \\
				&= -\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{ij}\partial_{t}(\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi)\;dxdt  \\
				&= -\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{T\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi \partial_{j}\phi\;dx + \frac{1}{2}\int_{\{0\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi \;dx  \\ &\qquad\quad + \frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n}(\partial_{t}a^{ij}(\partial_{i}\phi)(\partial_{j}\phi)\;dxdt, 
			\end{split}
		\end{align*}
		where the final line follows from the fact that 
		\begin{equation*}
			\partial_{t}(a^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi) - (\partial_{t}a^{ij})(\partial_{i}\phi)(\partial_{j}\phi) = a^{ij}\partial_{t}(\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi), 
		\end{equation*}
		we used the fundamental theorem of calculus after using the above fact, and the boundary terms in the first line vanish since $\phi$ has compact support. 
	\end{enumerate}
	Now we obtain the following result: 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi)) &= \int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi)F \\
			\implies 
			\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{T\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{tt}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}\;dx -\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{T\}\times \mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\;dx &= \frac{1}{2}\int_{\{0\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{tt}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}\;dx  -\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{0\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\;dx \\
			&\hspace{5pt}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi)F - \frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}a^{tt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} - (\partial_{j}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} \\
			&\qquad\qquad\hspace{2pt}+(\partial_{j}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} - (\partial_{t}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{j}\phi)\;dxdt
		\end{split}
	\end{align*}
	Now, one has that 
	\begin{multline*}
		\abs{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{t}\phi)F - \frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}a^{tt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} - (\partial_{j}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+(\partial_{j}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} - (\partial_{t}a^{jt})(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{j}\phi)\;dxdt} \\
		\leq 
		C'\int_{0}^{T}\norm{\partial_{t}\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\norm{F}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) + \norm{\partial a}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\norm{\partial \phi}^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt, 
	\end{multline*}
	where we used H\"older's inequality to obtain the first term, and assumed $\partial a \in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$. 
	On the other hand, by our hypothesis on $a^{\alpha\beta}$, there exists some constant $K > 0$ large enough so that (taking $C = \min\{C',K\}$)\todo{Explain how?},
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{\partial \phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(T) \leq C\abs{\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{T\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{tt}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}\;dx - \frac{1}{2}\int_{\{T\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}}a^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\;dx}. 
	\end{equation*}
	Thus this implies that 
	\begin{multline*}
		\norm{\partial \phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(T) \leq C\norm{\partial \phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}(0) + C\int_{0}^{T}\norm{\partial_{t}\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\norm{F}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) + \norm{\partial a}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\norm{\partial \phi}^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt. 
	\end{multline*}
	Since one can repeat this argument for any smaller interval $[0, T']$, where $0 < T' < T$, one obtains 
	\begin{multline*}
		\sup_{t \in [0,T]}\norm{\partial \phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \leq C\norm{\partial \phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}(0) + C\int_{0}^{T}\norm{\partial_{t}\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\norm{F}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) + \norm{\partial a}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\norm{\partial \phi}^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt. 
	\end{multline*}
	For the first term inside the integral, one has that 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			\int_{0}^{T}\norm{\partial_{t}\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\norm{F}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt &\leq \sup_{t \in [0,T]}\norm{\partial_{t}\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} \cdot \left(\int_{0}^{T}\norm{F}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right)\\
			&\leq \delta\norm{\partial_{t}\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2} + \frac{1}{4\delta}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\norm{F}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right)^{2}. 
		\end{split}
	\end{align*}
	where the second line follows from Young's inequality with $\epsilon$\footnote{For any $a, b \geq 0$ and any $\epsilon > 0$, $$ab \leq \frac{\epsilon^{p}a^{p}}{p} + \frac{1}{e^{q}q}b^{q}$$, where $p$, $q$ are conjugate exponents.}. If we fix $\delta > 0$ small enough so that the first term can be combined with the left side of the above inequality (and absorbing $\delta$ into the constant $C$), one has that 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			\sup_{t \in [0,T]}\norm{\partial \phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \leq C\norm{\partial \phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}(0) + C\left(\int_{0}^{T}\norm{F}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}(t)\;dt\right)^{2} + C\int_{0}^{T}\norm{\partial a}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\norm{\partial \phi}^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt.
		\end{split}
	\end{align*}
	Thus using Gr\"onwall's lemma concludes the proof. 
\end{proof}

We can, of course, say more. Using the above estimate, we can control the size of the higher-order derivatives of $\phi$ via the following result. 

\begin{prop}[Higher Order Energy Estimate I]
	Assume $\phi$ is a smooth solution to the Cauchy wave problem \ref{eq:clwp_ncc}, and let $k$ be a positive integer. Then there exists some constant $C = C(n,k) > 0$, the following energy estimates holds: 
	\begin{multline}
		\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\norm{\phi}_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) + \sup_{t \in [0,T]}\norm{\partial_{t}\phi}_{H^{k -1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \\ \leq C(1 + T)\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{T}\norm{\partial a}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right)\left(\norm{f}_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} 
		+ \norm{g}_{H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} + \int_{0}^{T}\norm{F}_{H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right.\\ + \left. C\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{|\alpha| + |\beta| \leq k - 1}\norm{\partial\partial_{x}^{\alpha}a\partial\partial_{x}^{\beta}\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) + \sum_{|\alpha| + |\beta| \leq k- 1}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}a\partial\partial_{x}^{\beta}\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right). 
		\label{eq:cor49}
	\end{multline}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
	\textcolor{red}{[!! Complete Later !!]}
\end{proof}


\newpage 
\subsection{Solutions to the Linear Wave Equation (Constant Coefficients)} 
In the previous subsection, we outlined various simple energy estimates solutions to the (non-)constant coefficient linear wave equations would satisfy if they existed, and also used these estimates to establish uniqueness results. But we have not yet shown that such solutions exist, nor what their physical properties. We dedicate this subsection to answering these questions. 


Consider again the Cauchy wave problem (\ref{eq:clwp}), 
\begin{equation*}
	\left\{
	\begin{alignedat}{1}
		&\Box \phi = 0, \\
		&(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}), 
	\end{alignedat}
	\right.
\end{equation*}
where we initially assume $\phi_{0}, \phi_{1} \in C_{c}^{\infty} \subset \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, and $\Box$ denotes the \textbf{wave operator}. Then one has the following result. 

\begin{theorem}[Existence and Uniqueness for (\ref{eq:clwp})]
	Given initial data $(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \subset \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, there exists a unique solution $\phi \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}; \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ that solves (\ref{eq:clwp}).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
 Assuming that $\phi$ is an appropriate space of functions, taking the Fourier transformation of the wave equation yields, 
\begin{equation*}
	\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\phi} + 4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\hat{\phi} = 0 \implies \partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\phi} = -4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\hat{\phi}, 
\end{equation*} 
which is a second-order ordinary differential equation. Thus solving this system, one obtains the general solution 
\begin{equation*}
	\hat{\phi}(t, \xi) = A\cos(2\pi t|\xi|) + B\sin(2\pi t \xi). 
\end{equation*}
Thus matching the initial conditions, it follows that 
\begin{equation*}
	\hat{\phi}(t, \xi) = \hat{\phi}_{0}\cos(2\pi t \xi) + \frac{\hat{\phi}_{1}}{2\pi  \xi}\sin(2\pi t \xi). 
\end{equation*}
But to complete the proof of existence, it remains to be seen that we can compute the inverse Fourier transform of $\hat{\phi}$. It suffices to show, at least, that $\hat{\phi}(t, \empspace) \in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$. Thus, we compute, 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\hat{\phi}(t, \xi)|\;d\xi &\leq \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\hat{\phi}_{0}(\xi)|\;d\xi + \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)|}{2\pi |\xi|}|\sin(2\pi t |\xi|)|\;d\xi. 
	\end{split}
\end{align*}
Since $\hat{\phi}_{0}(\xi)$ is Schwartz, it is integrable, and thus the first term is finite. $\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)$ is also Schwartz, and thus bounded above; i.e., there exists some $M > 0$ such that for all $\xi$, $|\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)| \leq M$. We note that $\sin(2\pi t \xi)/\xi \to t$ as $\xi \to 0$. This means there exists some $\epsilon > 0$ such that for all $\xi \in B_{\epsilon}(0)$, $|\sin(2\pi t \xi)/\xi - t| \lesssim_{t} 1$. This means that 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\hat{\phi}(t, \xi)|\;d\xi &\leq \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\hat{\phi}_{0}(\xi)|\;d\xi + \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)|}{2\pi |\xi|}|\sin(2\pi t |\xi|)|\;d\xi \\
		&\lesssim \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\hat{\phi}_{0}|\;d\xi + \int_{B_{\epsilon}(0)}M\;d\xi + \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{\epsilon}(0)}|\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)|\;d\xi < \infty.  
	\end{split} 
\end{align*}
Therefore, it follows that $\hat{\phi}(t, \empspace) \in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$. The Fourier Inversion Theorem then tells us that 
\begin{equation*}
	\phi(t, x) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\hat{\phi}(t, \xi)e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\;d\xi 
\end{equation*}
The final piece of the proof of existence is that $\phi(t, x)$ is a solution to (\ref{eq:clwp}). We first compute, 
\begin{equation*}
	\phi(0,x) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\hat{\phi}(0, \xi)e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\;d\xi = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\hat{\phi}_{0}e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\;d\xi = \phi_{0}(x). 	
\end{equation*}
Then using integrability and the dominated convergence theorem, one obtains: 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		\partial_{t}\phi(t, x) &= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}(t, \xi)e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\;d\xi, \\
		\partial_{t}^{2}\phi(t, x) &= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\phi}(t, \xi)e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\;d\xi, \\
		\Delta \phi(t, x) &= -\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\hat{\phi}(t, \xi)e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\;d\xi,  
	\end{split}
\end{align*}
so that 
\begin{equation*}
	\partial_{t}\phi(0,x) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\;d\xi = \phi_{1}(x), 
\end{equation*}
and 
\begin{equation*}
	\Box \phi = \partial_{t}^{2}\phi(t,x) - \Delta \phi(t, x) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\phi}(t, \xi) + 4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\hat{\phi}(t, \xi)\;d\xi = 0. 
\end{equation*}
Thus, an integrable solution exists for (\ref{eq:clwp}) under the hypothesis assumed above. In fact, since $\hat{\phi}(t, x) \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, one actually has that $\phi(t, \cdot) \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$. To prove uniqueness, assume $\phi^{(1)}$ and $\phi^{(2)}$ are two solutions to (\ref{eq:clwp}). Linearity of $\Box$ then implies that $\phi \coloneqq \phi^{(1)} - \phi^{(2)}$ also solves the linear wave equation with zero initial data. Thus, if $E(t) = \norm{\partial \phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}$ denotes the energy of $\phi$, then $E(0) = 0$. Thus conservation of energy implies that $E(t) = 0$ for all $t \geq 0$, whence $\phi \equiv 0$. Therefore, $\phi^{(1)} \equiv \phi^{(2)}$. 
\end{proof}

In particular, one does not completely require the initial data to smooth with compact support. We have the following existence and uniqueness result. 

\begin{theorem}[Existence and Uniqueness for (\ref{eq:clwp})]
	Given initial data $(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, there exists a unique solution $\phi \in L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ that solves (\ref{eq:clwp}). 
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
	The following proof is due to \cite{Holzegel2021}. \textcolor{red}{[!! Complete Later !!]}
\end{proof}


\subsubsection{Fundamental Solutions}

Now we will present another approach to solving (\ref{eq:clwp}). In particular, we consider instead the \textit{fundamental solution} using distributions (see Section \ref{subsec:distributions} for a review of distributions). We immediately have the following definition. 

\begin{definition}[Forward Fundamental Solution]
	The distribution $E_{+}$ is said to be a \textbf{forward fundamental solution} to the linear wave equation on $\mathbb{R}^{n + 1}$ if \vspace{-1.25em}
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt]
		\item $\Box E_{+} = \delta_{0}$ (where $\delta_{0}$ is defined as $\delta_{0}(\varphi) = \varphi(t = 0, x =0)$), 
		\item $\operatorname{supp}(E_{+}) \subseteq \{(t, x) \in \mathbb{R}^{1 + n} \mid 0 \leq |x| \leq t\}$. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{definition} 
In other words, the forward fundamental solution to the linear wave equation is the solution to linear wave equation on the \textit{forward light cone}. Immediately, one has the following uniqueness result. 

\begin{prop}[Uniqueness of Forward Fundamental Solution]
	A forward fundamental solution on $\mathbb{R}^{n + 1}$, if it exists, is unique. 
\end{prop} 
\begin{proof}
	Let $E_{1}, E_{2}$ be two forward fundamental solutions. Then one has, 
	\begin{equation*}
		E_{1} = E_{1} \ast \delta_{0}  = E_{1} \ast (\Box E_{2}) = E_{1} \ast (\Box \delta_{0} \ast E_{2}) = (\Box \delta_{0} \ast E_{1}) \ast E_{2} = \Box E_{1} \ast E_{2} = \delta_{0} \ast E_{2} = E_{2}, 
	\end{equation*}
	where we use the support properties of $E_{1}$ and $E_{2}$ to justify well-definedness of the convolution of the distributions and to justify swapping the wave operator.  
\end{proof} 

Now, we can use the forward fundamental solution to craft a solution to (\ref{eq:clwp}). 

\begin{prop}[Forward Fundamental Solutions and Representation Formulas]
	Let $E_{+}$ be a forward fundamental solution to the linear wave equation on $\mathbb{R}^{1 + n}$. Then if $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, the unique solution to (\ref{eq:clwp}) is given by, 
	\begin{equation*}
			\phi(t, x) = -E_{+} \ast (\phi_{1}\delta_{\{t = 0\}}) - (\partial_{t}E_{+}) \ast (\phi_{0}\delta_{\{t = 0\}}). 
	\end{equation*}
	More generally, for $\Box \phi = F$, where $F \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, $\phi$ is given for $t \geq 0$ by the following formula 
	\begin{equation*}
		\phi(t, x) = -E_{+} \ast (\phi_{1}\delta_{\{t = 0\}}) - (\partial_{t}E_{+}) \ast (\phi_{0}\delta_{\{t = 0\}}) + (F\chi_{\{t\geq 0\}}) \ast E_{+}, 
	\end{equation*}
	where 
	\begin{equation*}
		\chi_{\{t \geq 0\}}(t, x) \coloneqq 
		\begin{cases}
			1, & t \geq 0 \\
			0, & t < 0
		\end{cases}
	\end{equation*}
	is the characteristic function for the set $\{t \geq 0\}$. 
\end{prop} 

\begin{prop}[Unique Fundamental Solution]
	The unique forward fundamental solution is given by 
	\begin{equation}
		E_{+}(t, x) = -\frac{\pi^{\frac{1 - n}{2}}}{2}\chi_{\{t \geq 0\}}\chi_{+}^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}(t^{2} - |x|^{2}), 
		\ref{eq:fund_solution} 
	\end{equation}
	where 
	\begin{equation*}
		\chi_{+}^{a} \coloneqq \frac{x^{a}\chi_{\{x \geq 0\}}}{\Gamma(a + 1)}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{prop} 

From the fundamental solution, one has the following result. 

\begin{corollary}[Propagation of Waves]
	Solutions to the wave equation $\Box \phi = F$ propagate in time; i.e., $\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{supp}(\phi)(t) > 0$.  
	\label{cor:wave_prop}
\end{corollary} 


Now we can derive some of the explicit representation formulas given in the Appendix. 

\begin{prop}[d'Alembert's Formula]
	Let $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ be initial data to (\ref{eq:clwp}) in $\mathbb{R}^{1 + 1}$. Then the unique solution is given by 
	\begin{equation*}
		\phi(t, x) = \frac{1}{2}\left(\phi_{0}(x + t) - \phi_{0}(x - t)\right) + \frac{1}{2}\int_{x - t}^{x + t}\phi_{1}(y)\;dy. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{prop} 

\begin{proof}
	First, we have 
	\begin{equation*}
		\chi_{+}^{0}(t^{2} - |x|^{2}) = \chi_{\{t^{2} - |x|^{2} \geq 0\}}, 
	\end{equation*}
	so that 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			(\chi_{\{t \geq 0\}}\chi_{+}^{0}(t^{2} - |x|^{2})) \ast (\phi_{1}\delta_{\{t = 0\}}) &= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{\{t \geq 0\}} \chi_{\{t^{2} - |x - y|^{2}\}}\phi_{1}(y)\;dy = \int_{x - t}^{x + t}\phi_{1}(y)\;dy. \\
			(\partial_{t}(\chi_{\{t \geq 0\}}\chi_{+}^{0}(t^{2} - |x|^{2}))) \ast (\phi_{0}\delta_{t = 0}) &= \partial_{t}\int_{x - t}^{x + t}\phi_{0}(y)\;dy = \phi_{0}(x + t) - \phi_{0}(x - t). 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*}
	Thus using the formula for the forward fundamental solution, we conclude that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\phi(t, x) = \frac{1}{2}(\phi_{0}(x + t) - \phi_{0}(x - t)) + \frac{1}{2}\int_{x - t}^{x + t}\phi_{1}(y)\;dy. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{proof} 

The support of the forward fundamental solution also yields the following results. 

\begin{corollary}[Finite Speed of Propagation]
	Suppose $\phi  \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n + 1})$ is a solution to the linear wave equation with data $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1})$. If $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) = (0,0)$ in $\{y \in \mathbb{R}^{n}: |x - y| \leq t\}$, then $\phi(t, x) = 0$. 
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}[Strong Huygens Principle]
	Let $n \geq 3$ be an odd integer. Suppose $\phi \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n + 1})$ is a solution to the linear wave equation with data $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1})$. If $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) = (0,0)$ in $\{y \in \mathbb{R}^{n}: |x - y| = t\}$, then $\phi(t, x) =0$. 
\end{corollary} 
\begin{proof}
	The proof just follows by looking at the forward fundamental solution, 
	\begin{equation*}
		E_{+}(t, x) = -\frac{\pi^{\frac{1-n}{2}}}{2}\chi_{\{t \geq 0\}}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{3 - n}{2})}\frac{1}{(t^{2} - |x|^{2})^{\frac{n- 1}{2}}}\chi_{\{|x| \leq t\}}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Consider $n \geq 3$ odd. Then $1/\Gamma((3 -n)/2)$ is identically zero since $3 - n$ is a nonpositive integer, and the $\Gamma$ function has poles at these values. Since for $|x| < t$, $1/(t^{2} - |x|^{2})^{(n - 1)/2}$ is bounded, it follows that the forward fundamental solution is identically zero for all $|x| < t$. On the other hand, one has that on $|x| = t$, the $1/(t^{2} - |x|^{2})^{(n - 1)/2}$ is unbounded, while $1/\Gamma((3 - n)/2)$ is zero. Thus the support focuses on the spherical shell $|x| = t$. On the other hand, for $n \geq 2$ even, $\Gamma((3 - n)/2)$ is bounded as is $1/(t^{2} - |x|^{2})^{(n - 1)/2}$ for $|x| < t$. Thus the forward fundamental solution is not identically zero in the interior of the balls. 
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Decay Estimates}

Corollary \ref{cor:wave_prop} shows that as $t$ increases, the support of the unique solution to the (non)homogeneous wave equation increases (e.g., consider ripples on the surface of a lake). For $n \geq 2$, the measure of the support increases in time, while for $n =1$, the measure remains constant. However, we showed that the energy of a solution to (\ref{eq:clwp}) is conserved. This must imply that the amplitude of the wave $|\phi(t, x)|$ decays as $t \to \infty$. We have the following decay estimates. 

\begin{prop}[Pointwise Decay Rate]
	Fix any $R > 0$. For $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in C_{c}^{\infty} \times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ and $\phi$ the unique solution to the linear Cauchy wave problem (\ref{eq:clwp}), 
	\begin{equation}
		\sup_{|x| \leq R}|\phi(t, x)|\lesssim_{(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}, R)} \frac{1}{(1 + t)^{n - 1}}. 
		\label{eq:decay_clwp_1}
	\end{equation}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}
	The fundamental ideas behind the proof are as follows: we wish to consider the expression 
	\begin{equation*}
		\phi(t, x) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{2\pi i (t|\xi| + x \cdot \xi)}\left(\frac{\hat{\phi}_{0}}{2} + \frac{\hat{\phi}_{1}}{2\pi i |\xi|}\right) + e^{-2\pi i t|\xi|}\left(\frac{\hat{\phi}_{0}}{2} - \frac{\hat{\phi}_{1}}{2\pi i |\xi|}\right)\;d\xi, 
	\end{equation*}
	and then bound each of the terms in the integral. For sake of demonstrating the main ideas, we shall assume $n = 3$. The proof follows trivially for all $|t| \leq 1$: let 
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{matrix}
			\displaystyle I_{1} = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{2\pi i (t|\xi| + x \cdot \xi)}\frac{\hat{\phi}_{0}}{2}\;d\xi, & & \displaystyle I_{2} = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-2\pi i (t|\xi| + x \cdot \xi)}\frac{\hat{\phi}_{0}}{2}\;d\xi. \\ && \\
			\displaystyle I_{3}  = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{2\pi i (t|\xi| + x \cdot \xi)}\frac{\hat{\phi}_{1}}{2\pi i |\xi|}\;d\xi && \displaystyle I_{4} = -\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-2\pi i(t|\xi| + x \cdot \xi)}\frac{\hat{\phi}_{1}}{2\pi i |\xi|}\;d\xi. 
		\end{matrix}
	\end{equation*}
	Since $\hat{\phi}_{0}$ is Schwartz, and thus integrable, there exist $M_{1}, M_{2} > 0$ such that for all $|t|\leq 1$, $|I_{i}| \leq M_{i}$ for $i = 1, 2$. Now for $I_{3}$ (and analogously for $I_{4}$), we can write 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			|I_{3}| &\leq \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{|\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)|}{2\pi |\xi|}\;d\xi = \int_{B_{1}(0)}\frac{|\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)|}{2\pi |\xi|}\;d\xi + \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{1}(0)}\frac{|\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)|}{2\pi |\xi|}\;d\xi \\
			&\leq \int_{B_{1}(0)}\frac{K_{1}}{2\pi |\xi|}\;d\xi + \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{1}(0)}\frac{|\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)|}{2\pi |\xi|}\;d\xi\\
			&= \int_{B_{1}(0)}\frac{K_{1}}{2\pi |\xi|}\;d\xi + \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{1}(0)}\frac{|\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)|}{2\pi |\xi|}\;d\xi < \infty, 
		\end{split}
	\end{align*}
	where the second line follows from the fact that since $\hat{\phi}_{1} \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, it is continuous on the compact set $\overline{B}_{1}(0)$ and thus bounded above by some positive constant $K_{1}$, and the final line follows from the fact that $1/|\xi|$ is integrable over $B_{1}(0)$ for all $n \geq 2$, and the second term in the final line is controlled above by H\"older's inequality; $I_{4}$ is controlled in the same way. Thus, one has that for every $x \in \mathbb{R}^{n}$, and all $|t| \leq 1$, $|\phi(t, x)| \leq M$ for some constant $M$. Taking $C > 0$ large enough so that $C \geq M \cdot 2^{n - 1}$, one has that
	\begin{equation*}
		\sup_{|x| \leq R}|\phi(t, x)| \leq \frac{M}{(1 + t)^{2}}; 
	\end{equation*} 
	note that since $M_{1}, M_{2}, K_{1}, K_{2}$ are dependent on $\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}$, the proof concludes. So now we focus on the case with $t > 1$. Now introduce polar coordinates on $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ as follows: let $(|\xi|, \xi_{\theta}, \xi_{\phi})$ be the polar coordinates on $\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\xi}$ with $\xi_{1} = |\xi|\sin{\xi_{\theta}}\cos{\xi_{\phi}}$, $\xi_{2} = |\xi|\sin{\xi_{\theta}}\sin{\xi_{\phi}}$, and $\xi_{3} = |\xi|\cos{\xi_{\theta}}$. The corresponding volume form is, 
	\begin{equation*}
		d\xi = |\xi|^{2}\sin{\xi_{\theta}}\;d|\xi|d\xi_{\theta}d\xi_{\phi}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Furthermore, one has that for any $N \in \mathbb{N}_{0}$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\left(\frac{1}{2\pi i t}\frac{\partial}{\partial |\xi|}\right)^{N}e^{2\pi i t|\xi|} = e^{2\pi i t|\xi|}. 
	\end{equation*}
	Now using integration by parts, 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			I &= \int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^\pi\int_0^\infty e^{2\pi i t|\xi|} e^{2\pi i (x \cdot \xi)} \frac{\hat{\phi}_1(\xi)}{2\pi i}|\xi|\sin(\xi_\theta)\;d|\xi|d\xi_\theta d\xi_\varphi \\
			&= \left[ \frac{1}{(2\pi i)^2 t}\int_0^{\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}e^{2\pi i t |\xi|}e^{2\pi i (x \cdot \xi)}\hat{\phi}_1(\xi) |\xi| \sin(\xi_\theta)\;d\xi_\varphi d\xi_\theta \right]^{R \to \infty}_{|\xi| = 0} \\
			&\qquad - \int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^\pi\int_0^\infty \frac{1}{2\pi i t} e^{2\pi i t|\xi|} \frac{\partial}{\partial |\xi|}\left(e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\frac{\hat{\phi}_1(\xi)}{2\pi i}|\xi|\right)\sin(\xi_\theta)\;d|\xi| d\xi_\theta d\xi_\varphi
		\end{split}
	\end{align*}
	We claim that the first term is identically zero. To prove this, we first note that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\lim_{|\xi|\to \infty}\abs{^{2\pi i t |\xi|}e^{2\pi i (x \cdot \xi)}\hat{\phi}_1(\xi) |\xi| \sin(\xi_\theta)} = \lim_{|\xi| \to \infty}|\hat{\phi}_1(\xi)||\xi| = 0, 
	\end{equation*}
	since $\hat{\phi}_1(\xi) \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^3)$. On the other hand, the integrand vanishes identically at $|\xi| = 0$. Now, we see that one application of integration by parts gave us a power of $t$. To get another power of $t$, we will use integration by parts on the second term. This time, after dealing with a boundary term at $|\xi| = 0$, we get 
	\begin{equation*}
		I = -\int_{\mathbb{R}^3}e^{2\pi i t|\xi|}\frac{1}{8\pi^3it^2}\left(\dotsm\right)\sin(\xi_\theta)\;d|\xi|d\xi_\theta d\theta_\varphi + \frac{1}{2\pi^2 i t^2}\hat{\phi}_1(\xi = 0). 
	\end{equation*}
	The boundary term does not allow us to use integration by parts again. Thus bounding each of the term and using the fact that $\hat{\phi}_1 \in \mathcal{S}$, one obtains 
	\begin{equation*}
		|I| \leq \frac{C(1 + R^2)}{t^2}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{proof}

Note that this decay bound is not uniform for all $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$.  We can actually get a uniform decay bound for all $x \in \mathbb{R}^{n}$, but at the cost of the strength. 

\begin{theorem}[Uniform Decay Rate]
	For $(\phi_0, \phi_1) \in C_c^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^n) \times C_c^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^n)$, and $\phi$ the unique solution to the homogeneous initial value problem, there exists $C = C(\phi_0, \phi_1) > 0$ so that
	\begin{equation*}
		\sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^n}|\phi(t,x)| \leq \frac{C}{(1 + t)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}
	\end{equation*}
	for $t\geq 0$. 
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
	As before, we only present the proof for $n = 3$ and for the term 
	\begin{equation*}
		I = \left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{2\pi i (t |\xi| + x \cdot \xi)}\frac{\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)}{2\pi i |\xi|}\;d\xi\right).
	\end{equation*}
	It is always possible to find a coordinate system in which $x = (0,0,\alpha)$ for some $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$; thus, we will work in these coordinates. Let $\rho = \sqrt{\xi_{1}^{2} + \xi_{2}^{2}}$, and let us use the coordinate system $(\xi_{3}, \rho, \xi_{\theta})$, where $\xi_{\theta}$ is given by $\rho\cos(\xi_{\theta}) = \xi_{1}$. First note that  
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{\partial |\xi|}{\partial \rho} = \frac{\partial}{\partial \rho}\sqrt{\rho^{2} + \xi_{3}^{2}} = \frac{\rho}{|\xi|}. 
	\end{equation*}
	Moreover, $\partial_{\rho}e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi} = 0$. Thus, since for any nonnegative integer $N$,  
	\begin{equation*}
		\left(\frac{|\xi|}{2\pi i \rho t}\frac{\partial}{\partial \rho}e^{2\pi i t|\xi|}\right)^{N} = e^{2\pi i t|\xi|}, 
	\end{equation*}
	integration by parts gets us, 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			I &= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{|\xi|}{2\pi i \rho t}\frac{\partial}{\partial \rho}(e^{2\pi i t|\xi|})e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi} \frac{\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)}{2\pi i |\xi|}\;\rho d\rho d\xi_{\theta}d\xi_{3} = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{1}{2\pi i t}\frac{\partial}{\partial \rho}(e^{2\pi i t|\xi|})e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi} \frac{\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)}{2\pi i}\;d\rho d\xi_{\theta}d\xi_{3} \\
			&= -\int \frac{1}{4\pi^{2}t}e^{2\pi i(t|\xi| + x \cdot \xi)}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)\bigg|_{\rho = 0}^{\infty}\;d\xi_{\theta}d\xi_{3} + \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}t}e^{2\pi i (t |\xi| + x \cdot \xi)}\frac{\partial}{\partial \rho}(\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi))\;d\rho d\xi_{\theta}d\xi_{3} \\
			&= \int \frac{1}{2\pi t}e^{2\pi i (t|\xi_{3}| + \alpha \xi_{3})}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\rho = 0, \xi = \xi_{3})\;d\xi_{3} + \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}t}e^{2\pi i (t|\xi| + x \cdot \xi)}\frac{\partial}{\partial \rho}(\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi))\;d\rho d\xi_{\theta}d\xi_{3}. 	
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*}
	Since $\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)$ and $\partial_{\rho}\hat{\phi}_{1}(\xi)$ are Schwartz, it follows that 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			|I| &\leq \frac{\norm{\hat{\phi}_{1}}_{L^{1}}}{2\pi t} + \frac{\norm{\partial_{\rho}\hat{\phi}_{1}}_{L^{1}}}{4\pi^{2} t} \eqqcolon \frac{M}{t}. 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*}	
	Now we split into two cases. First assume $t \in [0,1]$. Then $(1 + t)^{-1} \geq 2^{-1}$ so that $2/(1 + t) \geq 1$. Directly bounding $|I(t,x)|$, one has that $|I(t, x)| \leq M_{0}$ for some $M_{0} > 0$, and all $t \in [0,1]$, $x \in \mathbb{R}^{3}$. Thus in this case, $|I| \leq M_{0} \leq \frac{2M_{0}}{1 + t}$. Now for the $t > 1$ case, $2t \geq 1 + t$ so that $2/(1 + t) \geq \frac{1}{t}$. Therefore, $|I| \leq \frac{2M}{1 + t}$. Take $C = \max\{2M_{0}, 2M\}$ so that $|I| \leq C/(1 + t)$ for all $t \in [0,1]$ and $x \in \mathbb{R}^{3}$. The other terms of $\phi(t, x)$ can be controlled the same way. 
\end{proof}

The key result is the following: for all $n \geq 2$, solutions to (\ref{eq:clwp}) undergo \textit{geometric dispersion}, while no dispersion occurs for $n = 1$. Even more strikedly, we notice that the decay rate inside a bounded ball is \textit{faster} than the globally uniform decay rate. Computationally, this is because we were able to undertake a second integration by parts for the bounded ball case, with each integration by parts adding a power of $t$ (in the case of $n =3$). We can try to understand why this would make sense intuitively. Looking at the fundamental solution, (\ref{eq:fund_solution}), we notice that the decay is primarily ``saturated'' along the wavefront. Consider first the case of $n = 3$. Fix $R > 0$ large enough, and thus fix the ball $B_{R}(0)$ of radius $R$ around the origin. Suppose that at $t = 0$, a light is flashed at the origin. The emitted light waves propagate in a spherical shell at the speed of light ($c = 1$). Because of conservation of energy, we know that the signal weakens as the wavefront approaches $\partial B_{R}(0)$. The moment the wavefront passes $\partial B_{R}(0)$ 


\subsection{Solutions to the Linear Wave Equation (Variable Coefficients)} \label{subsec:sol_lin_varco}
In this subsection, we continue studying the (non)homogeneous linear wave equation with \textit{variable} coefficients \cite{Luk}. Consider again the equation (\ref{eq:clwp_ncc}), 
\begin{equation*}
	\left\{
	\begin{alignedat}{1}
		&\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi) = F \\
		&(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}), 
	\end{alignedat}
	\right.
\end{equation*}
where $k \in \mathbb{N}$, $\phi: I \times \mathbb{R}^{n} \to \mathbb{R}$, and $a^{\alpha\beta}$ is assumed to be a symmetric matrix such that\footnote{to ensure hyperbolicity; \textcolor{red}{[!! Complete Later !!]}}. 
\begin{equation*}
	\sum_{\alpha,\beta}|a^{\alpha\beta} - m^{\alpha\beta}| < \frac{1}{10}. 
\end{equation*}

Furthermore, we shall assume that $a$ and all its derivatives (of all orders) are bounded in $[0,T] \times \mathbb{R}^{n}$, and that $F: [0,T] \times \mathbb{R}^{n} \to \mathbb{R}^{n} \in L^{1}([0,T];H^{k-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ . As in the case of the linear wave equation with constant coefficients, we wish to proceed with an existence and uniqueness result. But this requires us to first present the following result. 

\begin{lemma}[Bound on Sobolev Norms]
	Let $L$ denote the partial differential operator $L\phi = \partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi)$. Let $L^{\ast}$ be defined by $L^{\ast}\psi = \partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\psi)$ be the (formal) adjoint of $L$.\todo{Explain?} Suppose $\psi \in C_{c}^{\infty}((-\infty, T) \times \mathbb{R}^{n})$. Then for every $m \in \mathbb{Z}$, there exists $C = C(m, T, a, b) > 0$ such that 
	\begin{equation}
		\norm{\psi}_{H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \leq C \cdot \int_{t}^{T}\norm{L^{\ast}\psi}_{H^{m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(s)\;ds. 
		\label{eq:sobNorm_ncc}
	\end{equation}  
\end{lemma} 
\begin{proof}
	\textcolor{red}{[!! Complete Later !!]}
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Existence of Solutions to the Linear Equation]
	Let $k \in \mathbb{N}$. Given $F \in L^{1}([0,T]; H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$, there exists a solution 
	\begin{equation*}
		(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi) \in L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
	\end{equation*}
	solving (\ref{eq:clwp_ncc}). 
\end{theorem} 
\begin{proof}
	Unlike for the linear case with constant coefficients where we explicitly derived a representation formula, the proof of this result requires a bit of functional analysis (in particular the \textit{Hahn-Banach theorem}). The proof will be carried out over two steps. \vspace{-0.25cm}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item First assume that $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) = (0,0)$. Let $L$ be the differential operator $\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha \beta}\partial_{\beta} \bullet)$, and let $L^{\ast}: C_{c}^{\infty}((-\infty, T) \times \mathbb{R}^{n}) \to C_{c}^{\infty}((-\infty, T) \times \mathbb{R}^{n})$ be the formal adjoint of $L$\footnote{In the sense that for every $\phi, \psi \in C_{c}^{\infty}((-\infty, T) \times \mathbb{R}^{n})$, $\braket{L\phi, \psi} = \braket{\phi, L^{\ast}\psi}$.}. For every $\psi \in C_{c}^{\infty}((-\infty, T) \times \mathbb{R}^{n})$, define a map 
		\begin{equation*}
			\Phi: L^{\ast}\psi \in L^{\infty}(C_{c}^{\infty}((-\infty, T) \times \mathbb{R}^{n})) \mapsto \int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\psi F\;dxdt \eqqcolon \braket{F, \psi}. 
		\end{equation*} 
		We claim that this functional $\Phi$ is bounded. Indeed, one has 
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split}
				|\Phi(L^{\ast}\psi)| &= \abs{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}} \psi F\;dxdt} \leq \int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\psi F|\;dxdt  \\
				&\leq \int_{0}^{T}\norm{F}_{H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\norm{\psi}_{H^{1 - k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} \;dt \\
				&\leq C\cdot \left(\int_{0}^{T}\norm{F}_{H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\;dt\right) \cdot \left(\sup_{t \in [0,T]}\norm{\psi}_{H^{1 - k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\right) \\
				&\leq C' \cdot \int_{0}^{T}\norm{L^{\ast}\psi}_{H^{-k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(s)\;ds, 
			\end{split} 
		\end{align*}
		where (1) the second line follows from the generalized H\"older's inequality\footnote{$\norm{uv}_{L^{1}} \leq \norm{u}_{W^{(-s,p)}}\norm{v}_{W^{(-s, q)}}$, where $p, q$ are conjugate exponents}, (2) the final line follows from incorporating $\int_{0}^{T}\norm{F}_{H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\;dt$ into $C$, and using the previous lemma. It is also straightforward to see that the $\Phi$ is linear. Thus, by the Hahn-Banach theorem, there exists $\phi \in (L^{1}((-\infty, T); H^{-k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})))^{\ast} = L^{\infty}((-\infty, T); H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ with $\phi = 0$ for $t < 0$ that extends $\Phi$: $\braket{F, \psi} = \braket{\phi, L^{\ast}\psi}$ for every $\psi \in C_{c}^{\infty}((-\infty, T) \times \mathbb{R}^{n})$. Thus $\phi$ is a \textit{weak solution} (see e.g., Section \ref{subsec:distributions}) to the Cauchy wave problem with trivial data. 
		
		
		\item Now turn to the general case with $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1})\in H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, and let $u$ be a function in $[0,T] \times \mathbb{R}^{n}$ such that 
		\begin{equation*}
			(u, \partial_{t}u)\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}). 
		\end{equation*} 
		Then the system $L\eta = F - Lu$ has trivial initial data. Then the work in (1) gives us a weak solution $\eta$ to the problem. Setting $\phi = \eta + u$ gives the desired solution\footnote{\textcolor{red}{Complete proof that $(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)$ belong to the appropriate spaces}}.  
	\end{enumerate} 
\end{proof} 
\begin{remark}
	It is important to note that the above theorem only gives the existence of weak solutions. If the initial data is sufficiently regular, we can upgrade to a classical solution via the \textit{Sobolev Embedding Theorem}. 
\end{remark} 
\begin{theorem}[Sobolev Embedding Theorem]
	For every $s > \frac{n}{2}$, and for all $\phi \in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{\phi}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} \lesssim_{(n, s)} \norm{\phi}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	I.e., for every $s > n/2$, $H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \hookrightarrow C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$. 
\end{theorem} 

\subsection{More on Linear Hyperbolic Systems} 

Here, we consider additional properties of the linear wave equation not studied above, and also consider the more general class of \textit{hyperbolic systems} \cite{Rauch2012}. As we shall see, this leads naturally to the notion of \textit{propagation}. Some of these results are presented, perhaps with less exposition, in the Appendix. 

\paragraph{Method of Characteristics} 

We start with a simple setting: consider the homogeneous linear wave equation in $\mathbb{R}^{1 + 1}$, given by 
\begin{equation*}
	\partial_{t}\phi + c(t, x)\;\partial_{x}\phi  = 0, 
\end{equation*}
where $c(t, x)$ is assumed to be a smooth real-valued function. We also consider the ordinary differential equation, $\dot{x}(t)= c(t, x(t))$. For a smooth function $\phi$, one has that along the integral curves $(1, x(t))$, 
\begin{equation*}
	\frac{d}{dt}\phi(t, x(t)) = (\partial_{t}\phi + c\;\partial_{x}\phi)|_{(t, x(t))}. 
\end{equation*}
Thus it follows that the solutions of the homogeneous 1D linear wave equation are precisely those solutions $\phi$ that are constant along the integral curves $(t, x(t))$. We call these curves the \textbf{characteristic curves} of the partial differential operator $L = \partial_{t} + c(t, x)\partial_{x}$. 

The \textbf{method of characteristics} then boils down to considering a differential equation in one spatial dimension, reducing the equation to an ODE on integral curves, and constructing an overall solution from the solutions on each integral curve (e.g., see Chapter 1 of \cite{Folland} for an in-depth coverage of the subtleties and generalizations of the method of characteristics). It then follows that certain existence and uniqueness theorems follow easily from this method. E.g., 

\begin{prop}[Existence and Uniqueness for 1D Wave Equation] If $c \in \mathbb{R}$ is constant, then $\phi \in C^{\infty}([0,T] \times \mathbb{R})$ satisfies 
\begin{equation*}
	\partial_{t}\phi + c\partial_{x}\phi = 0 
\end{equation*}
if and only if there is an $f \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})$ such that $\phi = f(x - ct)$. The function $f$ is \textit{uniquely determined}. 
\end{prop} 
\begin{proof}
	By the method of characteristics, for constant $c$, the characteristics along which $u$ is constant are the lines $(t, x + ct)$. Thus, $u(t, x) = u(0,x - ct)$. If one sets $f(x) = u(0, x)$, then the forward direction is satisfied. Conversely, if there exists $f \in C^{\infty}$ such that $\phi(t, x) = f(x - ct)$, then 
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_{t}\phi = -cf'(x - ct), \qquad c\partial_{x}\phi = cf'(x - ct). 
	\end{equation*} 
	Thus, $\partial_{t}\phi + c\partial_{x}\phi = 0$. 
\end{proof} 

I.e., the Cauchy problem above is uniquely solvable by a $C^{\infty}$ function, and the solutions are \textit{propagating} waves translating rigidly with velocity $c$. We may also consider the same differential equation, but where $c(t, x)$ is \textit{not} bounded. 

\begin{example}[Nonuniqueness for Unbounded Speed]
	Consider the differential equation, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_{t}\phi + c(t, x)\;\partial_{x}\phi = 0, 
	\end{equation*}
	where $c(t, x) = x^{2}$ is not bounded. Fix a point $(0,x_{0})$ in spacetime. The characteristic through this point is the solution of the differential equation, 
	\begin{equation*}
		x' = x^{2}, \qquad x(0) = x_{0}, 
	\end{equation*}
	whence 
	\begin{equation*}
		x(t) = \frac{x_{0}}{1 - x_{0}t}. 
	\end{equation*}
	Inverting this, one obtains 
	\begin{equation*}
		x_{0} = \frac{x}{1 + xt}. 
	\end{equation*}
	Thus for each point $(t, x)$ with $t \geq 0$, there is a unique characteristic tracing back to $t = 0$. Therefore, given initial data $\phi(0,x) = g(x)$, the solution $\phi(t, x)$ is uniquely determined in $t \geq 0$ by requiring $t \geq 0$ by requiring $\phi$ to be constant on the characteristics. By plotting the integral curves, we observe the following: for $|x_{0}| \geq 1$, the corresponding integral curves all diverge before $t = 1$, for $x_{0} = 1$, the integral curve passing through $(0,x_{0})$ diverges at $t = 1$, and for $x_{0} \in (0,1)$, the integral curves blow up at finite time $t = 1/x_{0} > 1$. The backward characteristic through $(0,-1)$ diverges to $-\infty$ at $t = -1$. These observations have the following consequences. 
	
	Since information about the data on $[1, \infty)$ blows up before $t = 1$, the data for $|x| \geq 1$ does not influence the solution in $t \geq 1$. Consequently, there has been a loss of information so that the initial values do not uniquely determine a solution in $t < 0$. 
\end{example} 

Thus, going forward, we shall make the following hypotheses: 

\begin{quote}
	\textbf{Hypotheses 3.1} \textit{Suppose that for all $T > 0$, $\partial_{t, x}^{\alpha}c \in L^{\infty}([0, T] \times \mathbb{R})$, and that the coefficient $d(t, x)$ satisfies analogous bounds.}
\end{quote}

One can also generalize to \textit{systems} of hyperbolic equations. For instance, the method of characteristics applies to systems of the form 
\begin{equation*}
	u_{t} + \operatorname{diag}(c_{1}(t, x), \dotsm, c_{N}(t,x))u = 0, 
\end{equation*}
where $u = (u_{1}(t, x), \dotsm, u_{N}(t, x))^{\top}$, and each $u_{j}$ is constant on characteristics with speed $c_{j}(t, x)$ that satisfy Hypotheses 3.1. More generally, the method of characteristics extends to systems given by the partial differential operator, 
\begin{equation*}
	L = \partial_{t} + A(t, x)\partial_{x} + B(t, x), 
\end{equation*}
where $A, B$ are smooth matrix-valued functions such that for all $T \in \mathbb{R}$ and for all multiindices $\alpha$, $\partial^{\alpha}_{t, x}A, \partial^{\alpha}_{t, x}B \in L^{\infty}([-T, T] \times \mathbb{R})$ when the following hypothesis is satisfied, 
\begin{quote}
	\textbf{Hypothesis 3.2} There is  smooth matrix-valued function $M(t, x)$ so that for all $T \in \mathbb{R}$, and all multiindices $\alpha$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_{t, x}^{\alpha}(M), \partial_{t, x}^{\alpha}(M^{-1}) \in L^{\infty}([0,T] \times \mathbb{R}), 
	\end{equation*}
	and $MAM^{-1}$ is diagonal and real. 
\end{quote} 

Here, real diagonalizability of $A$ just ensures that the linear operator remains hyperbolic-type, and the bounds on $M$ and $A$ just ensure that the characteristic speeds do not diverge (thus preventing the loss of information and/or uniqueness of solutions). 

\begin{definition}[Strictly Hyperbolic Systems]
	If for each $(t, x)$, the matrix $A$ has $N$ distinct real eigenvalues $c_{1}(t, x) < c_{2}(t, x) < \dotsm < c_{N}(t, x)$, the system is said to be \textbf{strictly hyperbolic}. To ensure that the estimates on $M$ and $M^{-1}$ are uniform as $|x|\to \infty$, we also make the additional assumption 
	\begin{equation*}
		\inf_{(t,x)\in [0,T] \times \mathbb{R}}\min_{2 \leq j \leq N}\left(c_{j}(t,  x) - c_{j - 1}(t, x)\right) > 0. 
	\end{equation*}
	\todo{Explain?}
	
	A strictly hyperbolic system satisfies Hypothesis 3.2. 
\end{definition} 

In particular, if the hypothesis holds, then since one has that $A = MDM^{-1}$ with $D = \operatorname{diag}(c_{1}, \ldots, c_{N})$, we can write 
\begin{equation*}
	L = \partial_{t} + MDM^{-1}\partial_{x} + B(x, t)
\end{equation*} 

\textcolor{red}{[!! Complete Later !!]}

\subsubsection{Energy Estimates} 

Here, we return again to energy estimates for \textit{systems} of hyperbolic partial differential equations. As in the previous section, we will initially only consider first-order hyperbolic PDE. 

\paragraph{Constant Coefficient Systems} 

We start by considering the constant coefficient systems, 
\begin{equation*}
	\partial_{t}\phi + \sum_{j = 1}^{d}A_{j}\partial_{x^{j}}\phi = f, 
\end{equation*}
where $\phi = (\phi_{1}, \ldots, \phi_{d})^{\top}$, and each $A_{j}$ is a matrix-valued function. One has the following definition, 
\begin{definition}[Symmetric Hyperbolic Operator (Constant Coefficient)]
	A constant coefficient operator 
	\begin{equation*}
		L = \partial_{t} + \sum_{j = 1}^{d}A_{j}\partial_{x^{j}} 
	\end{equation*}
	on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ is said to be \textbf{symmetric hyperbolic} if each $A_{j}$ is hermitian symmetric. 
\end{definition} 
Requiring that the $A_{j}$ be hermitian \textit{symmetric} is very useful for computing energy estimates. We recall that if $A$ is symmetric, then the matrix-valued operator $\exp(-itA)$ is \textit{unitary}. So now, computing the Fourier transform of the PDE $L\phi = 0$, one obtains, 
\begin{equation*}
	\hat{\phi}(t,\xi) = \exp\left(-it\sum_{j = 1}^{d}A_{j}\xi_{j}\right)\hat{\phi}(0,\xi). 
\end{equation*}
But then, using the unitariness of the the exponential operator, we recover that for all $t \geq 0$, 
\begin{equation*}
	|\hat{\phi}(t,\xi)|^{2} = |\hat{\phi}(0,\xi)|^{2}. 
\end{equation*}
In particular, integrating over $d\xi$ and using Plancherel's theorem, one easily obtains conservation of the $L^2$-norm: $\norm{\phi(t)}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} = \norm{\phi(0)}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$. Likewise, under appropriate assumptions on $\phi$, one could also obtain conservation of the Sobolev norms. 

As we saw before, these types of energy estimates segue easily into a discussion of (local) well-posedness, existence, and unique. Thus as an analogue to the existence and uniqueness results in the above subsections, we have the following result. 

\begin{prop}[Existence and Uniqueness for Constant Coefficient Symmetric Hyperbolic PDE]
Suppose that $L = \partial_{t} + \sum_{j= 1}^{d}A_{j}\partial_{j}$ is a constant coefficient symmetric hyperbolic operator. For $g \in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, there exists a unique solution $\phi \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}; H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))$ to the initial value problem 
\begin{equation}
	L\phi = 0, \qquad \phi|_{\{t = 0\}} = g. 
\end{equation}
This solution is given by $\wh{\phi}(t, \xi) = e^{-2\pi it\sum A_{j}\xi_{j}}\hat{g}$\footnote{Note the $2\pi$ constant; \cite{Rauch2012} uses a slightly different convention for the Fourier transform.}. Furthermore, for all $t, s$, $\norm{\phi(t)}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} = \norm{\phi(0)}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}$. 
\end{prop} 

\begin{proof}
	We first prove existence of a solution: consider the function $\hat{\phi}(t, \xi) = \exp\left(-2\pi it\sum A_{j}\xi_{j}\right)\hat{g}$. Then one notes that 
	\begin{equation*}
		|\hat{\phi}(t, \xi)|^{2} = |\hat{g}|^{2} \implies \norm{\hat{\phi}(t)}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2} = \norm{\hat{g}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2} = \norm{g}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2} < \infty, 
	\end{equation*}
	where the last equality follows from Plancherel's theorem, and the final inequality follows from the fact that $g \in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) \subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Thus the inverse Fourier transform of $\hat{\phi}(t, \xi)$ is well-defined and also $L^{2}$. In particular, one has 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			\phi(t, x) &= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{2\pi i (x \cdot \xi - t\sum A_{j}\xi_{j})}\hat{g}(\xi)\;d\xi. 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*}
	It is immediately clear that $\phi(t, \empspace) \in C^{\infty}$. Now we must show that $\phi(\empspace, x) \in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Let $s \in \mathbb{R}$ be an arbitrary fixed real number. Then, 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			|(1 + |\xi|^{2})^{s/2}\hat{\phi}(t, \xi)|^{2} &= (1 + |\xi|^{2})^{s} \cdot |\hat{g}|^{2}. 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*}
	But since we assumed that $g \in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, it follows that $(1 + |\xi|^{2})^{s/2}\hat{g}(\xi) \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$. Thus, it follows that $\phi(\empspace, x) \in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ for all $s \in \mathbb{R}$. Hence, $\phi \in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}; H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))$. To show that $\phi$ actually solves the PDE, it suffices to show that $\hat{\phi}$ solves the ODE obtained by taking the Fourier transform of the PDE. But we've already shown this to be the case. Therefore, the proof of existence is complete. Moreover, the above equality also establishes conservatinon the Sobolev norms for all $t, s$. All that remains is to prove uniqueness. Assume that $\phi_{1}, \phi_{2}$ are two solutions to the initial value problem. Then their difference $\phi = \phi_{1} - \phi_{2}$ also solves the PDE $L\phi = 0$, but with the initial value identically zero. Applying the above results (in particular, the Sobolev norm conservation), it follows that $\phi$ is identically zero. Thus the proof concludes. 
\end{proof} 

\paragraph{Variable Coefficient Systems} Now we shall consider variable coefficient systems, analagous to the variable coefficient equations studied in Section \ref{subsec:sol_lin_varco}. We start with the following definition.
