\begin{definition}[Symmetric Hyperbolic Systems]
	In $\mathbb{R}^{1 + d}$ introduce the coordinates $y = (y_{0}, \ldots, y_{d})$, where $y_{0} = t$, and for each $1 \leq j \leq d$, $y_{j} = x_{j}$. Then the partial differential operator, 
	\begin{equation}
		L(y,\partial) = \sum_{\mu = 0}^{d}A_{\mu}(y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{\mu}} + B(y) 
		\label{eq:symHypOp}
	\end{equation}
	is said to be \textbf{symmetric hyperbolic} if the following hold: \vspace{-0.2cm} 
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep = -2pt]
		\item The coefficient matrices $A_{\mu}$ and $B$ have uniformly bounded derivatives: for all $\alpha$, 
		\begin{equation*}
			\sup_{y \in \mathbb{R}^{1 + d}}\norm{\partial^{\alpha}_{y}(A_{\mu}(y), B(y))} < \infty; 
		\end{equation*} 
		\item $A_{\mu}$ are hermitian symmetric; 
		\item $A_{0}$ is strictly positive in the sense that there exists $c > 0$ so that for all $y$, $A_{0}(y) \geq cI$. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{definition} 

It follows that this system satisfies an $L^{2}$ estimate of the following form. 

\begin{prop}{$L^{2}$ Estimates}
	For every $s \in \mathbb{R}$, there is a constant $C(s, L)$ so that for all $t \geq 0$ and all $u \in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}; H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})) \cap C(\mathbb{R}; H^{s + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{u(t)}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} \leq Ce^{Ct}\norm{u(0)}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})} + \int_{0}^{t}Ce^{C(t - \sigma)} \norm{(Lu)(\sigma)}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\;d\sigma, 
	\end{equation*} 
	where $L$ is a symmetric hyperbolic system. 
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
	Let us consider a few cases. \vspace{-0.4cm} 
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item First assume that $A_{0} = I$, and define 
		\begin{equation*}
			G(t) = \sum_{j}A_{j}(t, x)\;\partial_{j} + B(t, x), 
		\end{equation*} 
		and define its formal adjoint differential operator $G^{\ast}$ by 
		\begin{equation*}
			G^{\ast}(t) = -\sum_{j}A_{j}^{\ast}(t, x)\partial_{j} + B(t, x)^{\ast} - \sum_{j}(\partial_{j}A_{j}^{\ast})  
		\end{equation*} 
		so that for all $\phi, \psi \in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, $\braket{G(t)\phi, \psi} = \braket{\phi, G^{\ast}(t)\psi}$. In particular, one has 
		\begin{equation*}
			G(t) + G^{\ast}(t) = B(t,x) + B^{\ast}(t, x) - \sum_{j = 1}^{d}\partial_{j}A_{j}, 
		\end{equation*} 
		where we used the fact that since the $A_{j}$ are hermitian symmetric, $A_{j}^{\ast} = A_{j}$. Therefore, $G(t) + G^{\ast}(t)$ is multiplication by a uniformly bounded matrix, which means that as an operator on $L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, $\norm{G(t) + G^{\ast}(t)} \leq 2C$ for some $C > 0$. Note that this means that $G(t)$ is \textbf{nearly anti-self-adjoint}. If we assume that $u \in C(\mathbb{R}; H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))$, $u_{t} \in C(\mathbb{R}; L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))$, and $f \coloneqq Lu$, then it is straightforward to see that $u'(t) + G(t)u(t) = f(t)$. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{proof} 


\newpage 

\section{Local Theory for Nonlinear Wave Equations}
\subsection{Quasilinear Equations} 
In this subsection, we are most interested in the following quasilinear wave equations for $\phi: I \times \mathbb{R}^{n} \to \mathbb{R}$: 
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{alignedat}{1}
		&\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi)\partial_{\beta}\phi) = F(\phi, \partial \phi) \\
		&(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}), 
	\end{alignedat} 
	\right. 
	\label{eq:qlwe}
\end{equation}
where $k \in \mathbb{N}$ is specified later, $a^{\alpha\beta}$ is symmetric and satisfies, 
\begin{equation}
	\sum_{\alpha,\beta}|a^{\alpha\beta} - m^{\alpha\beta}| \leq \frac{1}{10}, \qquad a(0) =0, \qquad F(0,0) = 0, 
	\tag{$\ast$}
	\label{eq:assumptions-aF}
\end{equation} 
and $a^{\alpha\beta}: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $F: \mathbb{R}\times \mathbb{R}^{n} \to \mathbb{R}$ are smooth functions. As we will see, unlike the linear wave equation, (\ref{eq:qlwe}) is only \textit{locally} (in time) well-posed. We will first state the main result of this section, then prove a couple of technical results that will then enable us to prove the theorem. 

\begin{theorem}[Local Existence Theorem]
	Fix $a$ and $F$ satisfying assumptions (\ref{eq:assumptions-aF}). Then the Cauchy problem (\ref{eq:qlwe}) is locally well-posed in the following sense. \vspace{-0.2cm} 
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item (\textsc{Existence and Uniqueness of Local-in-time Solutions}) There exists 
		\begin{equation*}
			T = T(\norm{\phi_{0}}_{H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}, \norm{\phi_{1}}_{H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}) > 0 
		\end{equation*}
		such that there exists a \textbf{classical} solution $\phi$ to (\ref{eq:qlwe}) with 
		\begin{equation*}
			(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi) \in L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})). 
		\end{equation*} 
		Moreover, this solution is unique in the function space above.
		\item (\textsc{Continuous Dependence on Initial Data}) Let $\phi_{0}^{(i)}, \phi_{1}^{(i)}$ be sequences of functions such that $\phi_{0}^{(i)} \to \phi_{0}$ in $H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ and $\phi_{1}^{(i)} \to \phi_{1}$ in $H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ as $i \to \infty$. Then taking $T > 0$ sufficiently small, we have 
		\begin{equation*}
			\norm{(\phi^{(i)} - \phi, \partial_{t}(\phi^{(i)} - \phi))}_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{s - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} \to 0
		\end{equation*} 
		as $i \to \infty$ for every $1 \leq s < n + 2$. 
	\end{enumerate}
	\label{thm:nlwlwp}
\end{theorem} 
Before we can prove this theorem, we need the following estimates. 
\begin{lemma}
	There exists $B = B(A, n, F) > 0$ such that for $t \in [0,T]$, we have 
	\begin{equation*}
		\sum_{0 \leq |\alpha|\leq n + 1}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}F(\phi^{(i - 1)}, \partial \phi^{(i - 1)})}_{L^{2}}(t) \leq B. 
	\end{equation*} 
	\label{lemm:bound_F}
\end{lemma} 
\begin{proof}
	To establish this theorem, we need to first expand the partial derivatives, and use the chain rule. First, since $F$ was assumed to be smooth, by the Extreme Value Theorem, one has that for any $A > 0$, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\sum_{|\gamma|\leq N}\sup_{|x|, \norm{p}\leq A}|\partial^{\gamma}_{x,p}(F)|(x, p) \leq C_{A, N}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Thus expanding the partial derivatives using the chain rule, one gets 
	\begin{multline*} 
			\sum_{0 \leq |\alpha| \leq n + 1}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}F(\phi^{(i - 1)}, \partial\phi^{(i - 1)})}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \\
			\leq C\left(\sum_{0 \leq |\alpha|\leq n + 1}\norm{\partial\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\phi^{(i - 1)}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) + \sum_{0 \leq |\alpha_{1}| + |\alpha_{2}| \leq n + 1}\norm{\partial\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}\phi^{(i-1)}\partial\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\phi^{(i -1)}}_{L^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})(t)\right. \\
			\left. + \text{cubic terms} + \dotsm + \text{terms of order $n + 1$} \right). 
	\end{multline*} 
	Thus we need to bound each of the terms on the right side of the estimate above. Consider first the quadratic term. It follows that either $|\alpha_{1}| \leq \frac{n + 1}{2}$ or $|\alpha_{2}| \leq \frac{n + 1}{2}$. So without loss of generality, assume that $|\alpha_{1}| \leq \frac{n + 1}{2}$. Then since $\frac{n + 1}{2} + \frac{n}{2} \leq n + 1$, the Sobolev embedding theorem tells us that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\sum_{0 \leq |\alpha_{1}|\leq \frac{n + 1}{2}}\norm{\partial\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}\phi^{(i - 1)}}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} \leq C \cdot \sum_{0 \leq |\alpha|\leq n + 1}\norm{\partial\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\phi^{(i - 1)}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}. 
	\end{equation*}
	Thus, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\sum_{0 \leq |\alpha_{1}| + |\alpha_{2}| \leq n + 1}\norm{\partial\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}\phi^{(i - 1)}\partial \partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\phi^{(i - 1)}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \leq C \cdot \sum_{0 \leq |\alpha| \leq n + 1}\norm{\partial\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\phi^{(i - 1)}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}(t). 
	\end{equation*}
	Thus if we treat the higher-order terms in the above expansion analogously, we derive the estimate, 
	\begin{multline*}
		\sum_{0 \leq |\alpha| \leq n + 1}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}F(\phi^{(i - 1)}, \partial\phi^{(i - 1)})}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \\
		C\left(1 + \norm{\phi^{(i - 1)}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} + \sum_{0 \leq |\alpha| \leq n + 1}\norm{\partial\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\phi^{(i - 1)}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\right)^{n + 1} \leq B. 
	\end{multline*} 
\end{proof} 

\begin{lemma}[Bound on the Two Remaining Quantities] There exists some $B = B(A, n, F) > 0$ such that 
\begin{multline*}
	\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{|\gamma| + |\sigma| \leq n + 1}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\norm{\partial\partial^{\gamma}_{x}\left(a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)})\right)\partial\partial_{x}^{\sigma}\phi^{(i)}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt \\ 
	+ \int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\substack{|\gamma| + |\sigma| \leq n + 1 \\ |\sigma|\leq n}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\norm{ \partial_{x}^{\gamma}\left(a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)})\right)\partial\partial\partial^{\sigma}_{x}\phi^{(i)} }_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt \leq B.
	\end{multline*}
\end{lemma} 
\begin{lemma}[Bound on Derivatives of $a$]
	\begin{equation*}
		\sum_{|\gamma| = 1}\norm{\partial_{x}\left(a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)})\right)}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} \leq B. 
	\end{equation*}  
\end{lemma} 
\begin{proof}
	The proof of this theorem follows in two main steps; in the first step, we prove the existence of a solution. In the second, we prove continuous dependence on the initial data, and thus uniqueness of solutions. It is not immediately clear how to construct a solution to this general nonlinear system. But we recall from the previous section that we have a global-in-time existence and uniqueness solution for nonconstant coefficient linear wave equations. Thus if we can find a way to link the quasilinear system above to linear systems, then we can work from there. Luckily, we already have a tool that assists in this approach (namely, \textit{Picard iteration}\footnote{Recall that in ODE theory, Picard iteration is as follows: suppose one has the differential equation $y'(t) = f(t, y(t))$, where $y(t)$ is the unknown function, and $y(t_{0}) = y_{0}$. If we simply integrate both sides, then we can extract $y(t) =y_{0} + \int_{t_{0}}^{t}f(s, y(s))\;ds$. However, the integral depends explicitly on the unknown function. Thus we start with an initial ansatz $y_{0}$, and then use the above equation to produce a better estimate for the solution. We then take this estimate and use the above equation to obtain a better estimate. We do this iteratively, obtaining better solutions (under specific conditions).}).  \vspace{-0.25cm} 
	
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\arabic{*})]
		\item By density arguments, it suffices to assume that the initial data are Schwarz functions. Let $\phi^{(1)} \in C^{\infty}$ be an initial guess, and for $i \geq 2$, define $\phi^{(i)}$ as the unique smooth solution to the \textit{linear} system 
		\begin{equation*}
			\left\{
			\begin{alignedat}{1}
			&\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)})\partial_{\beta}\phi^{(i)}) = F(\phi^{(i - 1)}, \partial \phi^{(i - 1)}) \\
			&(\phi^{(i)}, \partial_{t}\phi^{(i)})\upharpoonright_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}).
			\end{alignedat} 
			\right. 
		\end{equation*} 
		To prove existence for the quasilinear system, it suffices to show that for $T > 0$ (sufficiently small, and depending only on the initial data), the sequence is uniformly bounded in the space $L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$, and is Cauchy in some larger function space. Thus we tackle each step at a time. 
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item (\textsc{Uniformly Bounded}) What we wish to show is that for all $i \geq 1$, there exists a $C = C(n,a, F_{0}, \phi_{0}, \phi_{1})> 0$ such that 
			\begin{equation*}
				\norm{(\phi^{(i)}, \partial_{t}^{(i)}\phi)}_{L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} \leq C. 
			\end{equation*}
			To show uniform boundedness, it suffices to show that the estimate holding for some $i -1$ implies the result holds for $i$. Thus for some $i \geq 1$, we will assume there exists some $A > 0$ such that 
			\begin{equation*}
				\norm{(\phi^{(i - 1)}, \partial_{t}\phi^{(i - 1)})}_{L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} \leq A. 
			\end{equation*}
			The principle of bootstrap arguments tells us that we can also assume that 
			\begin{equation*}
				\norm{(\phi^{(i)}, \partial_{t}\phi^{(i)})}_{L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} \leq 4A, 
			\end{equation*}
			as long as the bound can be proved (which our theorem says is possible). Now we note that the bounds that we require look a lot like the estimates in (\ref{eq:cor49}), 
			\begin{multline*}
				\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\norm{(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)}_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{k -1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \\ \leq C(1 + T)\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{T}\norm{\partial a}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right)\left(\norm{f}_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} 
				+ \norm{g}_{H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} + \int_{0}^{T}\norm{F}_{H^{k - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right.\\ + \left. C\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{|\alpha| + |\beta| \leq k - 1}\norm{\partial\partial_{x}^{\alpha}a\partial\partial_{x}^{\beta}\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) + \sum_{|\alpha| + |\beta| \leq k- 1}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}a\partial\partial_{x}^{\beta}\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right). 
			\end{multline*}
			With these estimates, we can finally use our energy estimate (\ref{eq:cor49}): 
			\begin{multline*}
				\norm{(\phi^{(i)}, \partial_{t}\phi^{(i)})}_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} \\
				\leq C\left(\norm{(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1})}_{H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n} \times H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} + BT + CBT^{2}\right)\exp(CBT)
			\end{multline*} 
			Let us assume wlog that $C \geq 2$. While the actual constant $B$ can be very large, we can choose sufficiently small $T > 0$ (depending on $A$), so that $(BT + CBT^{2})\exp(CBT) \leq \norm{(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1})}_{H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{n+ 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}$ and $\exp(CBT) \leq 2$. Thus with $T$ chosen as above, we get the bound 
			\begin{equation*}
				\norm{(\phi^{(i)}, \partial_{t}\phi^{(i)})}_{L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} \leq 4C_{0}\norm{(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1})}_{H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}. 
			\end{equation*} 
			Let $A = 4C_{0}\norm{(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1})}_{H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}$. Thus we have shown that 
			\begin{equation*}
				\norm{(\phi^{(i-1)}, \partial_{t}\phi^{(i-1)})}_{L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} \leq A
			\end{equation*} 
			implies 
			\begin{equation*}
				\norm{(\phi^{(i)}, \partial_{t}\phi^{(i)})}_{L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} \leq A, 
			\end{equation*} 
			provided that $T = T(A)$ is sufficiently small. Thus this portion of the proof concludes. 
			\item Now we will show that the sequence is Cauchy in $L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$. For every $i \geq 3$, we consider the difference $\phi^{(i)} - \phi^{(i - 1)}$:
			\begin{align*}
				\begin{split}
					\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i -1)})\partial_{\beta} (\phi^{(i)} - \phi^{(i - 1)})) &= \partial_{\alpha}\left( a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)})\partial_{\beta}\phi^{(i)} - a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)})\partial_{\beta}\phi^{(i - 1)} \right) \\
					&= F(\phi^{(i - 1)}, \partial\phi^{(i - 1)}) - \partial_{\alpha}\left(a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)})\partial_{\beta}\phi^{(i - 1)}\right) \\
					&= F(\phi^{(i - 1)}, \partial\phi^{(i - 1)}) - \partial_{\alpha}((a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)}) + a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i -2)}) - a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 2)}))\partial_{\beta}\phi^{(i - 1)}) \\
					&= F(\phi^{(i)}, \partial \phi^{(i - 1)}) - F(\phi^{(i - 2)}, \partial\phi^{(i - 2)}) \\
					&\qquad  - \partial_{\alpha}((a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)}) - a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 2)}))\partial_{\beta}\phi^{(i - 1)}). 
				\end{split} 
			\end{align*}
			We know that $a$ is continuously differentiable, and thus, satisfies the bound, 
			\begin{equation*}
				\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\sum_{|\gamma|\leq N}\sup_{|x|\leq A}|\partial_{x}^{\gamma}(a^{\alpha\beta})|(x)\leq C_{A,N}. 
			\end{equation*} 
			Since we also know that 
			\begin{equation*}
				\norm{(\phi^{(i)}, \partial_{t}\phi^{(i)})}_{L^{\infty}([0, T];H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} \leq C,
			\end{equation*}
			the Mean Value Theorem tells us that there exists some $C > 0$ (depending on the initial data, but neither on $i$ nor on $T$) such that 
			\begin{equation*}
				\abs{\partial_{\alpha}((a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)}) - a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 2)}))\partial_{\beta}\phi^{(i - 1)})} \leq C|\partial(\phi^{(i-1)} - \phi^{(i - 2)})|, 
			\end{equation*} 
			which implies 
			\begin{equation*}
				\norm{\partial_{\alpha}((a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)}) - a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 2)}))\partial_{\beta}\phi^{(i - 1)})}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \leq C\|\partial(\phi^{(i-1)} - \phi^{(i - 2)})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t), 
			\end{equation*} 
			for all $t \in [0, T]$. Likewise, one has that 
			\begin{equation*}
				\norm{F(\phi^{(i -1)}, \partial\phi^{(i - 1)}) - F(\phi^{(i - 2)}, \partial \phi^{(i - 2)})}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \leq C\|\partial(\phi^{(i-1)} - \phi^{(i - 2)})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t), 
			\end{equation*} 
			and $\norm{\partial (a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)}))}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} \leq C$. Thus applying (\ref{eq:cor49}), 
			we get 
			\begin{align*}
				&\sup_{t \in [0,T]} \|(\phi^{(i)} - \phi^{(i-1)}, \partial_t \phi^{(i)} - \partial_t \phi^{(i-1)})\|_{H^1(\mathbb{R}^n) \times L^2(\mathbb{R}^n)}(t) \\
				\le{} &CT \sup_{t \in [0,T]} \|(\phi^{(i-1)} - \phi^{(i-2)}, \partial_t \phi^{(i-1)} - \partial_t \phi^{(i-2)})\|_{H^1(\mathbb{R}^n) \times L^2(\mathbb{R}^n)}(t) \leq C_{1}.
			\end{align*}
			Thus if we choose $T > 0$ sufficiently small, then 
			\begin{equation*}
				\sup_{t \in [0, T]}\norm{\phi^{(i)} - \phi^{(i - 1)}}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \leq \frac{1}{2}\sup_{t \in [0, T]}\norm{\phi^{(i)} - \phi^{(i - 2)}}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t), 
			\end{equation*}
			whence 
			\begin{equation*}
				\sup_{t \in [0, T]}\norm{\phi^{(i)} - \phi^{(i -1)}}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \leq 2^{2 - i}C_{1}, 
			\end{equation*} 
			so that the Cauchy property follows immediately. 
			\item Now we will prove existence (uniqueness will follow from the proof of the second part of the theorem). Since we showed that the sequence is Cauchy in $L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$, there exists a limit $(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi) \in L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$. But by the uniform bound in the other function space, we conclude that $\phi, \partial_{t}\phi$ are both contained in the desired regularity class. 
			\end{enumerate}
		\item Now we will show a proof of continuous dependence on the initial data. Pick $i \in \mathbb{N}$ sufficiently large, and consider the difference $\phi^{(i)} - \phi$. Then we compute 
		\begin{multline*}
				\partial_{\alpha}(a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i)} - \phi)) \\
				= -\partial_{\alpha}((a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i)}) - a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi))\partial_{\beta}\phi) + F(\phi^{(i)}, \partial \phi^{(i)}) - F(\phi, \partial \phi). 
		\end{multline*}
		Applying (\ref{eq:cor49}), one obtains 
		\begin{multline*}
			\sup_{s \in [0,T]}\norm{(\phi^{(i)} - \phi, \partial_{t}\phi^{(i)} - \partial_{t}\phi)}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(s) \\
			\leq 
			C\left(\norm{(\phi_{0}^{(i)} - \phi_{0},\phi_{1}^{(i)} - \phi_{1})}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} + \int_{0}^{t}\norm{ \partial_{\alpha}((a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi^{(i - 1)}) - a^{\alpha\beta}(\phi))\partial_{\beta}\phi) }_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right. \\
			\left.+ \int_{0}^{t}\norm{F(\phi^{(i)}, \partial \phi^{(i)}) - F(\phi,\partial\phi)}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt\right) 
		\end{multline*} 
		Then the proof of (1) gives the following estimate, 
		\begin{multline*}
			\sup_{s \in [0,t]}\norm{ (\phi^{(i)} - \phi,\partial_{t}\phi^{(i)} - \partial_{t}\phi) }_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(s) \\
			\leq C\left( \norm{(\phi_{0}^{(i)} - \phi_{0}, \phi_{1}^{(i)} - \phi_{1})}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n} \times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} + \int_{0}^{t}\norm{(\phi^{(i)} - \phi, \partial_{t}\phi^{(i)} - \partial_{t}\phi)}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\;dt \right). 
		\end{multline*} 
		Thus by Gr\"onwall's inequality, one has that for some $C = C(T) > 0$, 
		\begin{equation*}
			\sup_{t \in [0,T]}\norm{ (\phi^{(i)} - \phi_{0}, \phi_{1}^{(i)} - \phi_{1}) }_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \leq C\norm{(\phi^{(i)}_{0} - \phi_{0}, \phi_{1}^{(i)} - \phi_{1})}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n} \times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))} \xrightarrow{i \to \infty} 0. 
		\end{equation*}
		Then to get the general estimate for $1 \leq s < n + 2$, we can simply use the bound 
		\begin{equation*}
			\norm{\eta}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} \leq C \norm{\eta}_{H^{s_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{\theta_{1}}\norm{\eta}_{H^{s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{\theta_{2}}
		\end{equation*} 
		for some $C = C(s_{1}, s_{2}, s, n) > 0$, where $0 \leq s_{1} \leq s \leq s_{2}$, $\theta_{1} + \theta_{2} = 1$, and $\theta_{1}s_{1} + \theta_{2}s_{2} = s$ (proven in the homework). 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}
We note the following difference between the existence and uniqueness results we have already seen in the linear case, and Theorem (\ref{thm:nlwlwp}): in the previous results, the solutions existed on time intervals $[0,T]$ for any arbitrary $T > 0$. However, the above theorem only gives existence of a \textit{classical} solution on some finite interval $[0,T]$, where $T$ is explicitly determined by the initial data. 

For physical theories, we often desire \textit{smooth} solutions. However, the unique solution constructed above is contained in $L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ for $T > 0$ sufficiently small. But in some cases, we can also \textit{lose} derivatives and regularity. Thus we desire some type of safeguard against the loss of regularity. The following result establishes this \textit{persistence of regularity}. 

\begin{theorem}[Persistence of Regularity]
	Given initial data $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ to (\ref{eq:qlwe}). Furthermore, let 
	\begin{equation}
		T_{\ast} \coloneqq \sup\{T > 0: \exists ! \text{ solution } \in L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\}, 
		\label{eq:maximal_time}
	\end{equation}
	be the \textbf{maximal time of existence}. \vspace{-0.25cm} 
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep = -2pt,label = (\alph{*})]
		\item If $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$ with $m > n +2$, then the solution $(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi) \in L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$ for every $T < T_{\ast}$. 
		\item If $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in \bigcap_{m = 1}^{\infty}(H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{m - 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))$, then the solution is smooth in $[0, T_{\ast}) \times \mathbb{R}^{n}$. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem} 

However, we are interested in what happens as we approach the maximal time of existence. To study this, we first have the following technical lemma. 

\begin{lemma}[Schauder Estimates I]
	Let $s \geq 0$, and $F: \mathbb{R}^{m} \to \mathbb{R}$ be a smooth function with $F(0) =0$. Then if $\phi: \mathbb{R}^{n} \to \mathbb{R}^{m}$ belongs to the space $\phi \in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}; \mathbb{R}^{m}) \cap H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}; \mathbb{R}^{m})$, there exists a constant $C = C(F, \norm{\phi}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}; \mathbb{R}^{m})}, s, n, m) > 0$ such that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{F(\phi)}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} \leq C\norm{\phi}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{lemma} 
\begin{lemma}[Schauder Estimates II]
	For $s > 0$, there exists $C = C(n, s) > 0$ such that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{f \cdot (\partial_{x_{i}}g)}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} \leq C(\norm{f}_{H^{s + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\norm{g}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})} + \norm{g}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\norm{f}_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}). 
	\end{equation*}
\end{lemma} 

\begin{theorem}[Breakdown Criteria]
	Given initial data $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$ to (\ref{eq:qlwe}), let $T_{\ast}$ be the maximal time of existence. If $T_{\ast} < \infty$, then all of the following hold: \vspace{-0.25cm}
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=-2pt,label = (\roman{*})]
		\item $$\liminf_{t \to T_{\ast}}\norm{(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)}_{H^{n + 2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) \times H^{n + 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \to \infty,$$
		\item $$\limsup_{t \to T_{\ast}}\left(\sum_{|a| \leq \lfloor \frac{n + 2}{2}\rfloor}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\phi}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) + \sum_{|\alpha| \leq \lfloor \frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\norm{\partial_{t}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\phi}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t)\right) \to 0$$. 
		\item $$\limsup_{t \to T_{\ast}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 1}\norm{\partial^{\alpha}\phi}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(t) \to \infty$$. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\subsubsection{Global Regularity for Subcritical Equations} 
In this subsubsection, we study the following nonlinear equation 
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{alignedat}{1}
		&\Box\phi = |\phi|^{2}\phi, \\
		&(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)\upharpoonright|_{\{t = 0\}} = (\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}), 
	\end{alignedat} 
	\right. 
	\label{eq:global_reg_subcritical}
\end{equation}
where $\phi: I \times \mathbb{R}^{3} \to \mathbb{R}$, and $I \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ is an interval. While the energy function, (\ref{eq:energy_lwp}), is not conserved for solutions $\phi$ to the above system, we have the following conserved quantity. 
\begin{prop}
	As long as the solution to (\ref{eq:global_reg_subcritical}) remains sufficiently regular, the following quantity is independent of time, 
	\begin{equation}
		E(t) \coloneqq \int_{\mathbb{R}^3}\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{1}^{3}(\partial_{i}\phi)^{2} + \frac{1}{4}|\phi|^{4}\;dx. 
		\label{eq:conserved_energy_subcritical}
	\end{equation} 
\end{prop} 
\begin{proof}
	As long as the solution remains \textit{sufficiently} regular, differentiating yields, 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			\frac{d}{dt}E(t) &= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{tt}^{2}\phi)\;dx + \sum_{1}^{3}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\partial_{i}(\partial_{t}\phi)(\partial_{i}\phi)\;dx  + \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\phi^{3}\;dx  \\
			&= \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\partial_{t}\phi)\cdot \left(\sum_{1}^{3}\partial^{2}_{ii}\phi - \phi^{3}\right)\;dx - \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\partial_{t}\phi) \cdot \sum_{1}^{3}(\partial_{i}^{2}\phi) + \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\phi^{3}\;dx = 0, 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*} 
	where the second term in the second line follows from using integration by parts. 
\end{proof} 
It is important to remember that just because we have a conserved quantity, the solution $\phi$ need \textit{not} be globally regular. But we have the following result. 

\begin{prop}[Control over Solutions]
	There exists a constant $C > 0$ such that for every $\phi: \mathbb{R}^{3} \to \mathbb{R}$, we have $$\norm{\phi}_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})} \leq C\norm{\phi}_{\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.$$  
\end{prop} 
\begin{proof}
	This is a consequence of the Sobolev embedding theorems. 
\end{proof} 
\begin{theorem}[Global-in-Time Solutions]
	Assume $(\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}) \in (H^{5}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) \times H^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap (C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) \times C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))$. Then (\ref{eq:global_reg_subcritical}) has a global-in-time solution. 
\end{theorem}
\begin{remark}
	Notice that the local well-posedness result for nonlinear wave equations only guaranteed us local-in-time solutions to the general nonlinear wave equation, with no additional information about whether a global-in-time solution exists. The above theorem states that for the specific nonlinear equation (\ref{eq:global_reg_subcritical}), we, in fact, have a global-in-time solution. 
\end{remark} 
\begin{proof}
	By the local existence theorem for nonlinear wave equations, we know that we have a smooth local-in-time solution to (\ref{eq:global_reg_subcritical}). Thus it suffices for us to show that this solution is global-in-time. Therefore, we must show that for every $T > 0$, the following bound is satisfied. 
	\begin{equation*}
		\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2}\norm{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\phi}_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times \mathbb{R}^{3})} +  \sum_{|\alpha|\leq 1}\norm{\partial_{t}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\phi}_{L^{\infty}([0,T] \times \mathbb{R}^{3})} \leq C(T). 
	\end{equation*}
	But by the Sobolev embedding theorem ($H^{s_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) \subset H^{s_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})$ for all $s_{1}\geq s_{2}$), it suffices to prove that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)}_{L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{3})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))} \leq C(T). 
	\end{equation*}
	We start by showing that, at minimum, 
	$$\norm{(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)}_{L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))} \leq C(T).$$ 
	We start by computing, 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			&\norm{(\phi, \partial_{t}\phi)}_{L^{\infty}([0,T]; H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})) \times L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))} \\
			&\leq C(T) \cdot \left(\norm{(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) \times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})} + \int_{0}^{T}\norm{\phi \cdot \phi \cdot \partial\phi}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\;dt\right) \\
			&\leq C(T)\cdot \left(\norm{\phi_{0}, \phi_{1}}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) \times H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})} + \int_{0}^{T}\norm{\phi}_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}(t)\norm{\phi}_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}(t)\norm{\partial\phi}_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}(t)\;dt\right) \\
			&\leq C(T)\cdot \left(\norm{(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})} + \int_{0}^{T}(E(t))^{2}\norm{(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) \times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}(t)\;dt\right). 
		\end{split}
	\end{align*} 
	Since $E(t)$ is \textit{a priori} controlled by the conservation law, we can just use Gr\"onwall's inequality to establish the bound on the $H^{2}$ norms. Thus, one also has that $(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)$ is bounded in $L^{\infty}([0,T] \times \mathbb{R}^{3})$. Likewise, we can also establish the appropriate bound on the $H^{4}$ norm, thus establishing the proof. 
\end{proof} 
\textcolor{red}{[!! Try to think about why it \textbf{suffices} to show the bounds that were shown above !!]}

\paragraph{Scaling and Criticality} In this section, we introduce the notion of \textit{scaling}, and \textit{criticality}. First suppose $\phi$ is a solution to (\ref{eq:global_reg_subcritical}). Then for $\lambda > 0$, we claim that $\phi_{\lambda}(t, x) = \lambda \phi(\lambda t, \lambda x)$ is also a solution: 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		\partial_{t}^{2}\phi_{\lambda} &= \lambda^{3}(\partial_{t}^{2}\phi)(\lambda t, \lambda x). \\
		\partial_{x}^{2}\phi_{\lambda} &= \lambda^{3}(\partial_{x}^{2}\phi)(\lambda t, \lambda x). \\
		|\phi_{\lambda}|^{2}\phi_{\lambda} &= \phi_{\lambda}^{3} = \lambda^{3}(|\phi|^{2}\phi)(\lambda t,\lambda x). 
	\end{split} 
\end{align*} 
Thus since $\phi$ is a solution to (\ref{eq:global_reg_subcritical}), we have that $\phi_{\lambda}$ is also a solution. To get a sense of criticality, let us consider the $\dot{H}^{1}$ seminorm of $\phi_{\lambda}$. We compute, 
\begin{equation*}
	\norm{\phi_{\lambda}}_{\dot{H}^{1}}(t) = \left(\int |\nabla \phi_{\lambda}|^{2}(t,x)\;dx\right)^{1/2}  = \left(\int \lambda^{4}|(\nabla \phi)|^{2}(\lambda t,\lambda x)\;dx\right)^{1/2} = \left(\int \lambda|\nabla \phi|^{2}(\lambda t, \ti{x})\;d\ti{x}\right)^{1/2} = \lambda^{1/2}\norm{\phi}_{\dot{H}^{1}}(\lambda t). 
\end{equation*}
Thus in the limit $\lambda \to \infty$, we notice that the $\dot{H}^{1}$ norm also tends to infinity. Thus it becomes clear that conservation laws because much more important at the smaller scales. Thus we have the following classification of equations. An equation is said to be \vspace{-0.25cm}
\begin{itemize}[itemsep =-2pt]
	\item \textbf{subcritical} if the ``conservation law becomes more useful at smaller scales'', 
	\item \textbf{supercritical} if the opposite is true, 
	\item \textbf{critical} if the conservation law scales in the same way as the scaling of the equation. 
\end{itemize}
The classification is useful since generally speaking, \vspace{-0.25cm}
\begin{itemize}[itemsep =-2pt]
	\item most subcritical equations have a global regular solution, 
	\item critical equations vary a lot in terms of having global regular solutions/finite-time blow up, 
	\item very little is known for supercritical equations. 
\end{itemize}
For instance, for the given equation, 
\begin{equation}
	\Box\phi = |\phi|^{p - 1}\phi. 
	\label{eq:mod_crit_eq}
\end{equation}
in $\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^{3}$, one observes that the $\dot{H}^{1}$ norm is always controlled by the conservation law, while the equation itself is invariant under the the scaling 
\begin{equation*}
	\phi_{\lambda}(t, x) = \lambda^{\frac{2}{p - 1}}\phi(\lambda t,\lambda x). 
\end{equation*} 
Then, we have the following. 
\begin{prop}
	For the equation (\ref{eq:mod_crit_eq}) in $\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^{3}$, the equation is \textbf{subcritical} if $p < 5$, \textbf{critical} if $p = 5$, and supercritical if $p > 5$. 
\end{prop} 
\begin{proof}
	A straightforward computation yields, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\norm{\phi_{\lambda}}_{\dot{H^{1}}}(t) = \lambda^{\frac{5 - p}{p - 1}}\norm{\phi}_{\dot{H}^{1}}(\lambda t). 
	\end{equation*}
	Thus for $p < 5$, the exponent of $\lambda$ is positive so that the $\dot{H}^{1}$ norm tends to infinity as $\lambda \to \infty$ (meaning the equation is subcritical), is exactly 0 at $p = 5$ (so that the equation is critical), and is negative for $p > 5$ (so that the equation is supercritical). 
\end{proof} 
\newpage 

\section{An Introduction to Einstein Vacuum Equations in Wave Coordinates} 
In this section, we look at the \textit{Einstein vaccum equations}. In particular, this requires us to cover some initial definitions in geometry. Thus we start with some basic definitions from the theory of Lorentzian manifolds. 

\begin{definition}[Christoffel Symbols]
	Let $(M, g)$ be a Lorentzian manifold, where $g$ is a Lorentzian metric on $M$. Then we define the \textbf{Christoffel symbols} on $M$ to be the smooth functions, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta} = \frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}(g_{\alpha\nu,\beta} + g_{\beta\nu,\alpha} - g_{\alpha\beta,\nu}). 
	\end{equation*}
	Roughly speaking, the Christoffel symbol $\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}$ denotes the $\alpha$th component of the derivative of a local basis vector field $E_{\beta}$ in the direction of $E_{\alpha}$. 
\end{definition} 

\begin{definition}[Levi-Civita Connection]
	Let $(M, g)$ be a Lorentzian manifold, where $g$ is a Lorentzian metric on the smooth manifold $M$. Then there exists a unique connection, $\nabla$, on $M$, called the \textbf{Levi-Civita connection} on $M$ defined by 
	\begin{equation*}
		\nabla_{X^{\alpha}\partial_{x^{\alpha}}}(Y^{\beta}\partial_{x^{\beta}})= X^{\alpha}\partial_{x^{\alpha}}Y^{\beta}\partial_{x^{\beta}} + \Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}X^{\alpha}Y^{\beta}\partial_{x^{\mu}},
	\end{equation*}
	that is \textbf{compatible} with $g$ (in the sense that $\nabla g = 0$) and is \textbf{torsion-free} (in the sense that $\nabla_{X}Y - \nabla_{Y}X -[X,Y] = 0$). 
\end{definition} 

\begin{definition}[Riemann Curvature Tensor]
	Let $(M, g)$ be a Lorentzian manifold, where $g$ i a Lorentzian metric on $M$. Then we define the \textbf{Riemann curvature tensor} on $M$ to be the $(1,3)$-tensor field given by 
	\begin{equation*}
		{R^{\gamma}}_{\alpha\mu\nu}\partial_{\gamma} = \nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\partial_{\alpha}- \nabla_{\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}. 
	\end{equation*}
	We can also think of the Riemann curvature tensor as a $(0,4)$-tensor field obtained by lowering an index:
	\begin{equation*}
		R_{\gamma\alpha\mu\nu} = g_{\gamma\eta}{R^{\eta}}_{\alpha\mu\nu}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition} 
The Riemann curvature tensor has the following properties (of which we omit the proofs). 
\begin{prop}[Properties of the Riemann Curvature Tensor]
	Let $(M, g)$ be a Lorentzian manifold, and let $R$ denote the Riemann curvature tensor on $M$. Then $R$ satisfies the following properties. \vspace{-0.25cm}
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\roman{*})]
		\item (\textsc{(Anti)Symmetricity}) $R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta} = R_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu} =-R_{\nu\mu\alpha\beta} = -R_{\mu\nu\beta\alpha}$. 
		\item (\textsc{Algebraic Bianchi Identity}) $R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta} + R_{\alpha\mu\nu\beta} + R_{\nu\alpha\mu\beta} = 0$. 
		\item (\textsc{Differential Bianchi Identity}) $\nabla_{\sigma}R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta} + \nabla_{\mu}R_{\nu\sigma\alpha\beta} + \nabla_{\nu}R_{\sigma\mu\alpha\beta} = 0$. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}
We also have the \textit{Ricci curvature tensor}, which is a symmetric $(0,2)$-tensor field. 
\begin{definition}[Ricci Curvature Tensor]
	Let $(M, g)$ be a Lorentzian manifold, and let the \textbf{Ricci curvature tensor}, $\operatorname{Ric}(g)$, on $M$ be the $(0,2)$-tensor field on $M$ obtained by taking the trace over 2 indices of the Riemann curvature tensor as follows:
	\begin{equation*}
		\operatorname{Ric}(g)_{\mu\nu} = g^{\alpha\beta}R_{\mu\alpha\nu \beta}. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition} 

In the homework, we showed that in local coordinates, $\operatorname{Ric}(g)$ can be written in the form, 
\begin{equation*}
	\operatorname{Ric}(g)_{\mu\nu} = -\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha \beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}g_{\mu\nu} - \frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\mu\nu} g_{\alpha\beta} + \frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha \nu}g_{\beta \mu} + \frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\beta\mu}g_{\alpha\nu} + F_{\mu\nu}(g, \partial g), 
\end{equation*}
where $F_{\mu\nu}(g, \partial g)$ is a function of $g$ and its derivatives. In particular, if only the first term remains, then the PDE (i.e., the Einstein vacuum equations) corresponding to $\operatorname{Ric}(g)_{\mu\nu} = 0$ becomes the wave equation so that the local existence theorem for quasilinear systems can be applied. Indeed, one has the following result: under certain (i.e., \textit{wave coordinate} conditions), the Einstein vacuum equations turn into a system of nonlinear wave equations. In particular, the Ricci curvature tensor can be expressed as the sum of a quasilinear wave operator and two terms that consist of the first derivatives of the metric. We formalize this result now. 
\begin{definition}[Wave Coordinate Condition]
	Let $(M, g)$ be a Lorentzian manifold. A local coordinate system $(x^{\mu})$ is said to satisfy the \textbf{wave coordinate condition} on $M$ if the following is true, 
	\begin{equation*}
	0 = \Box_{g}x^{\alpha} \coloneqq \frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\mu}(g^{\mu\nu}\sqrt{-g}\partial_{\nu}x^{\alpha}). 
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition} 
\begin{prop}
	Let $(M, g)$ be a Lorentzian manifold, and let $\operatorname{Ric}(g)$ be the Ricci curvature tensor on $M$. Then 
	\begin{equation*}
		\operatorname{Ric}(g)_{\mu\nu} = \ti{\operatorname{Ric}}(g)_{\mu\nu} + \frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\lambda_{\nu} + \frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}\lambda_{\mu}, 
	\end{equation*} 
	where $\ti{Ric}(g)_{\mu\nu}$ is the \textbf{reduced Ricci curvature tensor} defined by 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split} 
			\ti{Ric}(g)_{\mu\nu} &= -\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\beta}g_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta} + \frac{1}{2}g^{\beta\rho}g_{\sigma\rho,\mu}g_{\alpha\nu,\beta} + \frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\sigma}g^{\beta \rho}g_{\sigma\rho,\nu}g_{\beta\mu,\alpha}- \frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\sigma}g^{\beta\rho}g_{\sigma\rho,\mu}g_{\alpha\beta,\nu} + F_{\mu\nu}(g,\partial g). 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*} 
\end{prop}  
\begin{proof}
	Recall the matrix identities, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_{\alpha}g^{\mu\nu} = -g^{\mu\beta}g^{\sigma\nu}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\sigma\beta} \qquad \text{and} \qquad \partial_{\alpha}(\log|\det(g)|) = g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu}. 
	\end{equation*} 
	\textcolor{red}{[!! Complete Later !!]}
\end{proof} 
Thus if the wave condition holds, then the Einstein vacuum equations reduce to a nonlinear system of wave equations, for which we have the general local existence theorem from above. But we must ensure that the wave condition actually holds. We have the following results to that end. 

\begin{lemma}[Ricci Identity]
	Let $(M, g)$ be a Lorentzian manifold, and let $\operatorname{Ric}(g)$ be the Ricci curvature on $M$. Then the following identity holds: 
	\begin{equation*}
		\nabla^{\mu}(\operatorname{Ric}_{\mu\nu} - \frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}R) = 0. 
	\end{equation*} 
\end{lemma} 
\begin{proof}
	From the differential Bianchi identity, we have 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			\nabla^{\mu}R_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta} &= g^{\mu\sigma}\nabla_{\sigma}R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta} = g^{\mu\sigma}\nabla_{\alpha}R_{\mu\nu\beta\sigma} - g^{\mu\sigma}\nabla_{\beta}R_{\mu\nu\sigma\alpha} \\
			&= \nabla_{\alpha}\operatorname{Ric}_{\beta\nu}  - \nabla_{\beta}\operatorname{Ric}_{\alpha\nu}. 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*} 
	Thus taking the trace over $\alpha,\nu$, we get the above identity. 
\end{proof} 
\begin{prop}[Wave Equation]
	Given a Lorentzian metric $g$ such that the reduced Einstein vacuum equations are satisfied, i.e., $\operatorname{Ric}(g) = 0$, $\lambda$ satisfies a wave equation 
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{2}g^{\sigma\mu}\partial_{\sigma\mu}^{2}\lambda_{\nu} + c_{\nu}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\lambda_{\beta} =0, 
	\end{equation*} 
	where $c_{\nu}^{\alpha\beta}$ are smooth functions of $g$ and its derivatives. 
\end{prop} 
From this result, we have the following theorem of Choquet-Bruhat. 

\begin{theorem}[Local Well-Posedness of Einstein Vacuum Equations]
	Given initial data $(\hat{g}_{ij}, \hat{k}_{ij})$ on $\mathbb{R}^{n}$ satisfying the constraint equations, 
	\begin{equation}
		\begin{alignedat}{1}
			\hat{\nabla}_{i}{\hat{k}^{i}}_{j} - \hat{\nabla}_{j}{\hat{k}^{i}}_{j} &= 0, \\
			\hat{R}(\hat{g}) + ({\hat{k}^{i}}_{i})^{2} - {\hat{k}^{i}}_{j}{\hat{k}^{j}}_{i} &= 0,
		\end{alignedat} 
	\end{equation}
	and such that $\sum_{i,j}|\hat{g}_{ij} - \delta_{ij}| \leq \frac{1}{20}$, there exists a metric $g$ in $I \times \mathbb{R}^{n}$ which solves the Einstein vacuum equations such that the induced metric and the induced second fundamental form on $\{0\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n}$ coincide with $\hat{g}_{ij}$ and $\hat{k}_{ij}$, respectively. 
\end{theorem} 

\newpage 

\part{Applications}
In this part of the notes, we transition from the general theory of (non)linear wave equations and hyperbolic PDE to some physical applications. In particular, we will mostly apply the results from the previous part to understanding the mathematical theory of (in)compressible fluids. 
\section{Incompressible Fluids}

In this section, we introduce the mathematical theory of incompressible fluids. \cite{Marchioro_Pulvirenti_1993}.  

\subsection{Introduction to the Euler Equation}

In this subsection, we introduce the \textbf{Euler equations} for ideal, incompressible fluids. In general, we will work in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ unless stated otherwise. We start with a definition of \textit{incompressible displacements}. 

\begin{definition}[Incompressible Displacements]
Let $\Omega \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{3}$ be an open and bounded region with sufficiently regular boundary $\partial \Omega$. Further assume that $\Omega$ contains a fluid. An \textbf{incompressible displacement} of the fluid is a transformation $\sigma: \Omega \to \Omega$ such that \vspace{-0.35cm} 	
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\roman{*})]
	\item $\sigma$ is invertible, and $\sigma(D) = D$; 
	\item $\sigma, \sigma^{-1} \in C^{1}(\Omega)$ (i.e., $\sigma$ is a diffeomorphism on $\Omega$), 
	\item $\sigma$ preserves the Lebesgue measure (in the sense that $m(\sigma(A)) = m(A)$, where $m$ is the $3$-dimensional Lebesgue measure, and $A \subseteq \Omega$). 
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
	Point (iii) makes \textit{incompressibility} explicitly clear to see; intuitively, a subvolume $A$ of fluid in $\Omega$ cannot be compressed or stretched (which could change the Lebesgue measure).
\end{remark} 
In particular, it is clear to see that the set $S$ of all incompressible displacements on $D$ forms a group under function composition as the product. Using this as a guide, we define \textbf{incompressible motion} as follows. 
\begin{definition}[Incompressible Motion]
	Let $\Omega \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{3}$ be an open and bounded region with sufficiently regular boundary $\partial \Omega$. Further assume that $\Omega$ contains a fluid. An \textbf{incompressible motion} is a function $s, t \in \mathbb{R} \mapsto \Phi_{s, t} \in S$ such that \vspace{-0.25cm} 
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\roman{*})]
		\item $\Phi_{s, t}(\Phi_{t,r}(x)) = \Phi_{s, r}(x)$; 
		\item $\Phi_{t, s}(\Phi_{s, t}(x)) = x$; 
		\item $\Phi_{s, t}$ is continuously differentiable in $t$ and $s$. 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}  

\subsubsection{Derivation of the Fluid Equations} 

Using the above results, we derive the PDEs satisfied by systems of \textit{ideal}, incompressible fluids. We begin with the equation for the \textit{density field}. Let $\Omega \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{3}$ be an open and bounded region with sufficiently regular boundary $\partial \Omega$. Further assume that $\Omega$ contains a fluid. Let $\rho = \rho(x, t) \in C^{1}(\Omega)$ denote the \textit{density fluid} of the fluid (the mass of the fluid contained in the element of volume $dx$ at time $t$ is $\rho(x, t)\;dx$). Fix some subregion $V_{0} \subset \Omega$, and let 
\begin{equation*}
	V_{t} \coloneqq \{\Phi_{t}(x): x \in V_{0}\}. 
\end{equation*}
Note, we define $\Phi_{t}(x) \coloneqq \Phi_{t,0}(x)$. Also let $u(\Phi_{t}(x), t) = \frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{t}(x)$ denote the velocity field associated with this incompressible motion. Conservation of mass tells us that 
\begin{equation*}
	\frac{d}{dt}\int_{V_{t}}\rho(x, t)\;dx = 0. 
\end{equation*}
Thus making a ``change of variables'', we have that 
\begin{equation*}
	0 = \frac{d}{dt}\int_{V_{t}}\rho(x, t)\;dx = \frac{d}{dt}\int_{V_{0}}\rho(\Phi_{t}(x), t)J_{t}(x)\;dx = \frac{d}{dt}\int_{V_{0}}\rho(\Phi_{t}(x),t)\;dx, 
\end{equation*} 
where $J_{t}(x)$ is  the Jacobian of the transformation $x \mapsto \Phi_{t}(x)$. Since $\Phi_{t}$ is an incompressible motion, the Jacobian must identically be one. Passing the time derivative inside the integral (which we can do because of the assumed regularity of $\rho$ and boundedness of $V_{0}$), we then obtain 
\begin{equation*}
	0 = \frac{d}{dt}\int_{V_{0}}\rho(\Phi_{t}(x),t)\;dx = \int_{V_{0}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\rho(\Phi_{t}(x),t)\;dx = \int_{V_{0}}\partial_{t}\rho(\Phi_{t}(x),t) + u \cdot \nabla \rho(\Phi_{t}(x),t)\;dx. 
\end{equation*} 
Since $V_{0}$ was arbitrary, we obtain the PDE, $$(\partial_{t} + u \cdot \nabla)\rho(\Phi_{t}(x), t) = 0,$$  which is identically the linear first-order transport equation; we call this equation the \textit{continuity equation}. For the most part, however, we will assume the density to be constant $(\rho \equiv 1)$, so that the above equation is not immediately useful. Instead, we have the more important variation of the \textit{continuity equation}. 

\begin{prop}[Continuity Equation for Incompressible Flows]
	Let $\Omega \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{3}$ be an open and bounded region containing a fluid. Let $V_{0}$ be a fixed subregion in $\Omega$, and let $V_{t} = \{\Phi_{t}(x): x \in V_{0}\}$, where $\Phi_{t} = \Phi_{t,0} \in S$ is an incompressible motion. Let $u(\Phi_{t}(x), t) = \frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{t}(x)$ denote the velocity field for the motion. Then the \textbf{continuity equation} is given by 
	\begin{equation}
		\operatorname{div}(u(x,t)) = \nabla \cdot u(x, t) \qquad (\forall x \in \Omega, t \in \mathbb{R}). 
		\label{eq:conteq_incompressibleflows}
	\end{equation}
\end{prop} 
Hereafter in this subsubsection, we will assume that $u \in C^{1}(\Omega \times \mathbb{R})$ and $u(t, \cdot) \in C(\bar{\Omega})$. So far, we have only given a PDE for $u$, but have not yet assigned any additional constraints. Recall that for PDEs describing physical systems, boundary conditions tell us how the system interacts with the boundaries of its environment. Thus we are interested in giving $u$ a boundary condition based on its interaction with $\partial \Omega$. By the definition of incompressible flow, we assume that no fluid particle can cross $\partial \Omega$. Thus if $v(x)$ denotes the velocity of the boundary $\partial \Omega$ at $x$, we must have $u(x, t) \cdot n = v(x) \cdot n$, where $n$ is the outward unit normal to $\partial \Omega$. Thus once we have the velocity field $u(x, t)$, the trajectories $\Phi_{t}(x)$ can be uniquely determined. 

\subsubsection{Equations of Motion for an Incompressible Fluid} 

In the previous subsubsection, we studied how the density, and more importantly, the velocity fields behaved. In doing so, we wrote the continuity equation, which is a statement about mass conservation. If we assume the density is constant (as we shall), then this equation enables us to uniquely construct the incompressible motions for the fluid. In this subsubsection, we turn to the actual fluid. In particular, we will derive the \textit{Euler equations} for the fluid. We make some simplifying assumptions for our model. 

\begin{definition}[Ideal Fluids]
	Let $\Omega \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{3}$ be an open and bounded region containing a fluid. We assume that the fluid is \textbf{ideal}, or \textbf{perfect}, in the sense that the only interaction between the fluid particles is that induced by our incompressibility condition (namely that two particles may not simultaneously occupy the same position). I.e., we will ignore any interactive forces (e.g., electromagnetic forces) between two distinct fluid particles. 
\end{definition} 

To derive the equations of motion, we will use the \textit{Principle of Stationary Action}. The Lagrangian for the system is the kinetic energy, 
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal{L} = \frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{t}(x)\right)^{2}\;dx. 
\end{equation*} 
Thus, the action is given by 
\begin{equation*}
	A(\Phi; t_{1}, t_{2}) \coloneqq \int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{t}(x)\right)^{2}\;dxdt. 
\end{equation*} 
This defines a functional $\Phi \mapsto A(\Phi; t_{1}, t_{2})$ on the space $M$ of all incompressible motions. To determine a physical law, we will consider variations in this class $M$ as follows: we require that the action be stationary for variations $\Phi \mapsto \Phi + \delta \Phi$ which are compatible with incompressiblity, and to satisfy $\delta \Phi_{t_1}(x) = \delta\Phi_{t_{2}}(x) = 0$ for all $x \in \Omega$. Moreover, we require the variation to satisfy the boundary conditions, $\frac{d}{dt}\Phi^{\epsilon}_{t}(x) \cdot n = 0$, for all $x \in \partial \Omega$ (i.e., the velocity of $\Omega$ is assumed to be zero so that no fluid particle can cross the boundary). By $\Phi^{\epsilon}$, where $\epsilon \in [0,\epsilon_{0}]$, we denote a family of motions tangent to $\partial \Omega$ such that $\Phi^{0} = \Phi$, $\Phi_{t_{1}}^{\epsilon} =\Phi_{t_{1}}$, and $\Phi^{\epsilon}_{t_{2}} = \Phi_{t_{2}}$. We want the action $A$ to be stationary on $\Phi$, which is to say that we want 
\begin{equation*}
	\frac{d}{d\epsilon}A(\Phi^{\epsilon}; t_{1}, t_{2})|_{\epsilon =0} = 0. 
\end{equation*}
Using the definition of $A$, it follows that 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		0 = \frac{d}{d\epsilon}A(\Phi^{\epsilon};t_{1}, t_{2})|_{\epsilon = 0} &= \frac{d}{d\epsilon}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)\right)^{2}\;dxdt\bigg|_{\epsilon = 0} \\
		&= \int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}2\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)\right)\frac{d}{dt}\frac{d}{d\epsilon}\Phi_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)\bigg|_{\epsilon = 0}\;dxdt \\
		\implies 
		\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Omega}\frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{t}(x) \cdot \frac{d}{dt}\gamma_{t}(\Phi_{t}(x))\;dxdt &= 0, 
	\end{split} 
\end{align*} 
where for $\epsilon \in [0,\epsilon_{0}]$, we define 
\begin{equation*}
	\gamma_{t}^{\epsilon}(\Phi_{t}(x)) = \frac{d}{d\epsilon}\Phi_{t}^{\epsilon}(x), 
\end{equation*}
and denote $\gamma_{t} \coloneqq \gamma_{t}^{0}$. In other words, $\gamma_{t}^{\epsilon}$ is the vector field transversal to the motion that generates a flow parametrized by $\epsilon$: $\Phi_{t}(x) \mapsto \Phi_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)$. Then using integration by parts\footnote{\textcolor{red}{[!! Fill in the details later !!]}}, we obtain the equation $D_{t}u = -\nabla p$, where $p: \mathbb{R} \times \Omega  \to \mathbb{R}$ is some function, and $D_{t} = \partial_{t} + (u \cdot \nabla)$ is the \textit{material} or \textit{convective derivative}. Thus we have concluded the derivation of the incompressible Euler equations. 

\begin{prop}[Incompressible Ideal Euler Equations]
	Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^{3}$ be an open and bounded region of $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ containing an incompressible fluid. Let $u$ denote the velocity field of the fluid. Then $u$ satisfies the \textbf{incompressible ideal Euler equations}, 
	\begin{equation}
		\left\{
		\begin{alignedat}{1}
			&\partial_{t}u + (u \cdot \nabla) u = -\nabla p, \\
			&\nabla \cdot u = 0, \\
			&u \cdot n = 0 \qquad (\text{on $\partial \Omega$}).
		\end{alignedat} 
		\right. 
	\end{equation}
\end{prop} 

The equations above are a direct generaliation of the physical equations of motion for incompressible ideal fluids. In particular $D_{t}u$ can be thought of as the ``acceleration'' of the fluid, and the scalar function $p = p(x, t)$ as the \textit{scalar pressure} (thus, the first equation in the system is a direct analog of Newton's law). The second equation is a statement of mass conservation; the third equation is a statement of the boundary conditions for our system of PDEs. 

\subsubsection{Vorticity and Stream Functions}
In the previous subsection, we were looking at physical models that describe the motion of a fluid in a bounded open region $\Omega$ of $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. In this subsubsection, we dive deeper and study more specific properties of fluid motion. We start with the definition of \textit{vorticity}. 

\begin{definition}[Vorticity Field]
	Let $\Omega$ be an open bounded region of $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ containing an incompressible ideal fluid, and let $u = u(t, x)$ be the velocity field of the fluid. Then we define the \textbf{vorticity field} of $u$ as the vector field, 
	\begin{equation}
		\omega(t,x) = \operatorname{curl}{u(t,x)} = \nabla \times u(t, x), 
	\end{equation}
	where $\nabla$ is taken with respect to the space variables only.
\end{definition} 
Physically, the \textit{vorticity} is a measure of how the fluid is rotating. We also define the following. 

\begin{definition}[Deformation Tensor]
	Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^{3}$ be an open and bounded region containing an incompressible ideal fluid, and let $u$ denote the velocity field of the fluid. Let $D$ be the matrix defined as 
	\begin{equation*}
		D = \frac{1}{2}(\nabla u + (\nabla u)^{\perp}). 
	\end{equation*}
	Then $D$ is said to be the \textbf{deformation tensor}. 
\end{definition} 
\begin{prop}[Local Motion of a Fluid]
	Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^{3}$ be an open and bounded region containing an incompressible ideal fluid, and let $u$ denote the velocity field of the fluid. Then 
	\begin{equation}
		u(x + h, t) = u(x, t) + D \cdot h + \frac{1}{2}\omega(x, t) \times h + O(h^{2}), 
		\label{eq:local_motion}
	\end{equation}
	where $D$ is the deformation tensor. 
\end{prop} 
I.e., locally, the velocity field of the fluid consists of three motions: (1) a translation motion, (2) a rotation with angular velocity $\frac{1}{2}\omega$, and (3) a deformation. One can see why $D$ is called the \textbf{deformation tensor} by considering rigid motion, in which case, $D = 0$, and the angular velocity of the fluid is exactly $\Omega = \frac{1}{2}\omega$. 

\paragraph{Constructing Solution to 2D Euler Equations from Vorticity} Suppose that the vorticity $\omega = \omega(x) \in C(\Omega)$ is a given vector field, where $\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^{2}$, and let $u(x)$ denote the velocity field of the fluid. Then $u$ satisfies the system, 
\begin{equation*}
	\left\{
	\begin{alignedat}{1}
		\nabla \times u &= \omega, \\
		\nabla \cdot u&= 0. 
	\end{alignedat} 
	\right. 
\end{equation*} 
Thus we wish to know under what circumstances we can deduce $u$ given $\omega$. In general, even if a solution exists to the above system, it may not necessarily be unique. For instance, consider a solution $u'$, and let $u = u' + \nabla \varphi$, where $\varphi$ is a harmonic function. Then clearly $u$ also satisfies the above system as does $u'$. Thus to force uniqueness, we must at least introduce some boundary conditions. Thus the motivation for the following section is to consider various cases for $\Omega$ and its consequences on solutions to the system above. 

\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\arabic{*})]
	\item (\textbf{$\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^{2}$; $\Omega$ is simply connected and bounded}): Since $\Omega$ is simply connected and bounded, and $u$ is divergence free, there exists\footnote{via the Poincar\'e Lemma} a function $\Psi$, called the \textbf{stream function}, such that $u = \nabla^{\perp}\Psi$, where $\nabla^{\perp} \coloneqq (\partial_{x_{2}}, -\partial_{x_{1}})$. Then we compute, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Delta \Psi = \partial^{2}_{x_{1}}\Psi + \partial^{2}_{x_{2}}\Psi = \partial_{x_{1}}(-u_{2}) + \partial_{x_{2}}(u_{1}) = \partial_{x_{2}}(u_{1}) - \partial_{x_{1}}(u_{2}). 
	\end{equation*} 
	On the other hand, we compute, $\omega = \nabla \times u = \partial_{x_{1}}u_{2} - \partial_{x_{2}}u_{1}$. Thus we obtain the Poisson equation, $\Delta \Psi = -\omega$. Since $u \cdot n = 0$ on $\partial D$, $\Psi$ must be a constant on $\partial \Omega$. Thus without loss of generality, we may assume that $\Psi|_{\partial D} = 0$. Assuming $\omega$ has sufficient regularity, it then follows that there exists a unique solution $\Psi$ satisfying $\Delta \Psi = -\omega$ and $\Psi|_{\partial \Omega} = 0$. Thus this gives us a solution for $u$. Now we establish uniqueness of this solution. Assume $u'$ is another solution to the above system. Then $v = u - u'$ satisfies 
	\begin{equation*}
		\nabla \cdot v = 0 \qquad \text{and} \qquad \nabla \times v = 0. 
	\end{equation*}
	Since $\Omega$ is simply connected, the curl-free condition on $v$ implies that it is the gradient of a smooth function. Thus $v$ satisfies $v = \nabla \varphi$ and $v \cdot n = 0$ on $\partial \Omega$. Taking the divergence of this relation, one then gets the condition $\Delta \varphi = 0$ and $\partial \varphi/\partial n = 0$ on $\partial D$, whence (by integration by parts), $\varphi$ is a constant. Thus $v = 0$, from which $u = u'$. 
	
	\item (\textbf{$\Omega = \mathbb{R}^{2}$}): In this case, we assume that $\Omega = \mathbb{R}^{2}$, which is simply connected, but not bounded. However, the initial analysis for the stream function remains the same; namely, we may introduce a stream function $\Psi$ such that $u = \nabla^{\perp}\Psi$ and $\Delta \Psi = -\omega$, where $\omega$ is the vorticity of the fluid. Unlike in the previous example where we were simply able to exploit boundedness of $\Omega$, integration by parts, and the theory of solutions to the Poisson equation, we must do some extra analysis in the unbounded case of $\mathbb{R}^{2}$. To do so, we will use the method of \textit{Green functions}. 
	
	Let $G = G(x, x')$ be the fundamental solution of the Poisson equation, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Delta_{x}G(x, x') = -\delta(x - x'); 
	\end{equation*}
	an explicit representation of $G$ is given by \cite{evans10}, 
	\begin{equation*}
		G(x, x') = -\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln|x - x'|. 
	\end{equation*} 
	Thus, one has 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Psi(x) = \int G(x,x')\omega(x')\;dx'. 
	\end{equation*} 
	This then implies that 
	\begin{equation*}
		u(x) = \nabla^{\perp}\Psi(x) = \int K(x,x')\omega(x')\;dx', 
	\end{equation*} 
	where we define the velocity kernel, 
	\begin{equation*}
		K(x,x') = -\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{(x - x')^{\perp}}{(x - x')^{2}}. 
	\end{equation*}
	Intuitively, $K(x,x')$ denotes the velocity field (in $x$) generated by a point charge of intensity one fixed in $x'$. Now we are interested in uniqueness of solutions. Consider again $v$, which is the difference of two solutions. Then $v = \nabla \varphi$ for some harmonic function $\varphi$. Suppose we fix the asymptotic behavior $u(x) \to u_{\infty} \in \mathbb{R}^{2}$ as $|x| \to \infty$. Then the unique harmonic function $\nabla \varphi \to u_{\infty}$ has the general form $\varphi = u_{\infty} \cdot x + \text{const.}$ so that the solution to the problem is given by 
	\begin{equation*}
		u(x) = \nabla^{\perp}\Psi(x) + u_{\infty}.
	\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Conservation Laws}

As we did for the theory of linear wave equations, we start by assuming that solutions to the incompressible Euler equations exist, and then showing general properties of these solutions. Likewise, as in the previous part of these notes, we begin with an exposition on the \textit{energy}. We start with the following definition 
\begin{definition}[Energy]
	Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^{2}$ be an open region containing an incompressible ideal fluid, and let $u \in C^{1}(\Omega \times [0,T])$ be the velocity field of the fluid. Then we define the \textbf{energy} of the fluid by 
	\begin{equation}
		E(t) \coloneqq \frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}u^{2}(t)\;dx. 
		\label{eq:energy_incomp_ideal_fluid}
	\end{equation} 
\end{definition} 

Since the fluid is assumed ideal, we have the following energy conservation law. 

\begin{theorem}[Energy Conservation Law]
	Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^{3}$ be a bounded domain, and let $u$ be a solution of the Euler equation with conservative external forces 
	\begin{equation*}
		D_{t}u = -\nabla (p + V), 
	\end{equation*}
	where $V = V(x, t)$ is a known function. Then $\dot{E}(t) =0$. 
\end{theorem} 
\begin{proof}
	Since $\Omega$ is a bounded domain, and $u$ is continuous on $\Omega \times [0,T]$ (so that it is continuous on $\Omega$), one has that $u$ and $\partial_{t}u$ is integrable over $\Omega$. Thus, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\dot{E}(t) = \int_{\Omega} u \cdot \partial_{t}u = -\int_{\Omega}\{u \cdot (u \cdot \nabla)u + u \cdot \nabla P\}, 
	\end{equation*} 
	where $P \coloneqq p + v$. Next, since $\operatorname{div}(Pu) = u \cdot \nabla P + P \nabla \cdot u$, and $u$ was assumed to be divergence-free, 
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_{\Omega}u \cdot \nabla P = \int_{\Omega}\operatorname{div}(Pu) = \int_{\partial \Omega}(Pu) \cdot n. 
	\end{equation*} 
	But since $u \cdot n = 0$ on $\partial \Omega$, it follows that the above integral is zero. Thus it suffices to show that the first term is also zero. We compute, 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split} 
			\int_{\Omega}u \cdot (\nabla \cdot u)u &= \sum_{i}\int_{\Omega}u_{i}u\cdot \nabla u_{i} \\
			&= \sum_{i}\int_{\Omega}\nabla \cdot \left(\frac{1}{2}u_{i}^{2}u\right) - \frac{1}{2}u_{i}^{2}(\nabla \cdot u) \\
			&= \sum_{i} \int_{\partial \Omega} \frac{1}{2}u_{i}^{2}u \cdot n - \sum_{i} \frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega} u_{i}^{2}(\nabla \cdot u) = 0, 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*} 
	where we used the fact that $u$ is divergence-free, $u \cdot n = 0$ on $\partial \Omega$, and the divergence theorem. 
\end{proof} 

Now we introduce a special type of flow, called \textit{stationary flows}. 

\begin{definition}[Stationary Flows]
	Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^{3}$ be a bounded domain containing a fluid, and let $u$ be a solution to the Euler equations in $\Omega$. Then $u$ is said to be a \textbf{stationary} or \textbf{steady flow} if $u = u(x)$ is independent of time. 
\end{definition} 

For such flows, we have the following theorems of Bernoulli. 

\begin{theorem}[Bernoulli I]
	In an ideal fluid in stationary motion under the action of a conservative force with potential $V$ independent of time, the quantity 
	\begin{equation}
		\mathcal{E} = \frac{1}{2}u^{2} + (p + V)
	\end{equation}
	is constant along the flow lines. 
\end{theorem} 

\begin{theorem}[Bernoulli II]
	Consider an ideal fluid in a stationary irrotational motion in a domain $D$, under the action of a conservative force with potential $V$ independent of time. Then the quantity, 
	\begin{equation}
		\mathcal{E} =\frac{1}{2}u^{2} + (p + V) 
	\end{equation}
	is constant. 
\end{theorem} 

While the two theorems \textit{look} similar, the corresponding hypotheses are not identical. In the first Bernoulli theorem, we consider merely a stationary solution to the Euler equations. Under these hypotheses, while the quantity $\mathcal{E}$ is constant along \textit{each} flow line, the quantity may vary from one stream to another. On the other hand, the second Bernoulli theorem informs us that if the stationary solution is also \textit{irrotational}, then the quantity $\mathcal{E}$ is independent of the flow line. The two theorems imply the following consequences. 

\begin{corollary}[Bernoulli III]
	Consider an ideal fluid in stationary (irrotational) motion in a domain $\Omega$, independent of any external forces. Then the velocity is smallest when the pressure is the greatest, and vice-versa. 
\end{corollary} 
\begin{corollary}[Bernoulli IV]
	Consider an ideal fluid in stationary (irrotational) motion in a domain $\Omega$, independent of any external forces, where $\Omega$ is assumed to be a ``pipeline''. Then the velocity is greater (thus pressure is smaller) when the pipeline has a smaller section. 
\end{corollary} 

\begin{corollary}[Stevino]
	Consider a fluid at rest in the presence of a gravitational field $V = -gx_{3}$. Then $p = gx_{3}$. 
\end{corollary} 

Now we shift to studying conservation laws dealing with the vorticity of fluids. The physical picture is as follows. Suppose that we have two layers of fluids with different velocities, stacked on top of each other. 
\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering  
	\begin{tikzpicture}
		\draw (-5,0) -- (-2,0) -- (-2,0.2) -- (-5,0.2) -- cycle; 		
		\draw[->, thick] (-2,0.1) -- (-0.5,0.1); 
		\draw (-1,0) node[below] {$v_{1}$};
		\draw (-3,0.2) -- (0,0.2) -- (0,0.4) -- (-3,0.4) -- cycle; 
		\draw[->,thick] (0,0.3) -- (1.5,0.3); 
		\draw (0.75,0.3) node[above] {$v_{2}$};
	\end{tikzpicture}
\end{figure}

Since the fluid was assumed to be ideal (i.e., no interaction between the fluid particles outside of the incompressiblity clause), it is not possible for the two layers to transfer momentum between each other, and thus must move independently. In particular, this prevents the fluid from producing rotation on its own, and ensures that the fluid has no means to dissipate energy through rotation/friction. The following theorem of Kelvin formalizes the result using the notion of \textit{circulations}. 

\begin{theorem}[Kelvin]
	Let $\Omega\subset \mathbb{R}^{3}$ be a bounded region containing an ideal fluid with velocity field $u$. Let $C_{0}$ be a closed curve in $\Omega$ at $t = 0$, and let $C_{t} = \Phi_{t}(C_{0})$. Define the \textbf{circulation} as the quantity, 
	\begin{equation}
		\Gamma(C_{t}) = \oint_{C_{t}}u(t)\;dl. 
		\label{eq:circulation}
	\end{equation}
	Then 
	\begin{equation}
		\frac{d}{dt}\Gamma(C_{t}) = 0. 
		\label{eq:conservation_of_circulation} 
	\end{equation} 
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
	The proof of Kelvin's theorem will follow in two steps. First, we will prove that $\frac{d}{dt}\oint_{C_{t}}u \cdot dl = \oint_{C_{t}}D_{t}u\cdot dl$. Next, we will use Euler's equations in conjunction with the above result to establish the theorem. \vspace{-0.25cm} 
	\begin{enumerate}[itemsep =-2pt,label = (\arabic{*})]
		\item Let $[0,1] \ni s \mapsto x(s) \in C_{0}$ be a parametrization of the initial curve $C_{0}$. Then the map $[0,1] \ni s \mapsto \Phi_{t}(x(s))$ is a parametrization of $C_{t}$. Then we compute, 
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split}
				\frac{d}{dt}\oint_{C_{t}}u\cdot dl &= \frac{d}{dt}\int_{0}^{1}u(\Phi_{t}(x(s),t),t) \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Phi_{t}(x(s))\;ds \\
				&= \int_{0}^{1}D_{t}u(\Phi_{t}(x(s)),t)\cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Phi_{t}(x(s))\;ds + \int_{0}^{1}u(\Phi_{t}(x(s)), t) \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial t}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Phi_{t}(x(s))\;ds. 
			\end{split}
		\end{align*}
		The final integral can be evaluated as follows: 
		\begin{align*}
			\begin{split}
				\int_{0}^{1}u(\Phi_{t}(x, s), t) \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial t}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Phi_{t}(x(s))\;ds &= \int_{0}^{1}u(\Phi_{t}(x,s),t)\cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial s}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Phi_{t}(x(s))\;ds \\
				&= \int_{0}^{1}u(\Phi_{t}(x(s)), t) \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial s}u(\Phi_{t}(x(s)), t)\;ds \\
				&= \frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}u^{2}(\Phi_{t}(x(s)), t)\;ds\\
				&= 0, 
			\end{split} 
		\end{align*}
		where the second line follows from the definition of the velocity line in terms of $\Phi_{t}$, and the final line follows from the fact that since $C_{0}$ is a closed curve, $x(0) = x(1)$. Thus, we have shown the first claim. 
		\item Using the above result, since $D_{t}u = -\nabla (p + V)$, where $V$ is the potential for a conservative external force, we have 
		\begin{equation*}
			\frac{d}{dt}\Gamma(C_{t}) = -\oint_{C_{t}}\nabla (p + V)\cdot dl = 0. 
		\end{equation*} 
	\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}  
\begin{corollary}[Constant Vorticity Flux]
	Let $\Sigma_{t} \subset \Omega$ be a surface that moves with the fluid whose base is a closed curve $C_{t}$, $n$ the unit normal vector field to the surface, and $\omega$ the vorticity field. If $d\sigma$ denotes the surface area element on $\Sigma_{t}$, then 
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\omega \cdot n\;d\sigma = 0. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{corollary}
We can say more about the vorticity. First consider the following definitions. 
\begin{definition}[Vorticity Line]
	A \textbf{vorticity line} is a curve in $\Omega$ that is tangent at every point to the vorticity field $\omega$. 
\end{definition} 
\begin{definition}[Vortex Tube]
	Let $C$ be a closed curve in $\Omega$ that is not tangent to the vorticity field at any point. Then the set of all vorticity lines which cross $C$ transversally in each of its points is called the \textbf{vortex tube}. 
\end{definition} 
\begin{figure}[h!]
	\centering 
	\includegraphics[width=0.2\textwidth]{vortex_tube}
	\caption{The lines in the above figure illustrate the vortex tube for $C$.}
	\label{fig:VortexTubes} 
\end{figure}

\begin{theorem}[Helmholtz]
	Let $C_{1}$ and $C_{2}$ be two arbitrary cures encircling the same vorticity tube. Then 
	\begin{equation}
		\Gamma = \oint_{C_{1}}u\cdot dl = \oint_{C_{2}}u\cdot dl. 
	\end{equation} 
	$\Gamma$ is called the \textbf{strength} of the vortex tube. By Kelvin's theorem, $\Gamma$ is constant during the motion. 
\end{theorem} 
\begin{proof}
	Let $C_{1}$ and $C_{2}$ be two arbitrary closed curves, $S$ the surface of the vortex tube enclosed by the two curves, and $S_{1}, S_{2}$ the surfaces whose borders are given by $C_{1}$ and $C_{2}$, respectively. Let $\Sigma = S \cup S_{1} \cup S_{2}$. Since we assumed $u$ was sufficiently regular, and $\omega$ is the curl of $u$, $\nabla \cdot \omega = 0$. Hence if $V$ denotes the volume enclosed by $\Sigma$, then 
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{split}
			0 &= \int_{V}\nabla \cdot \omega\;dx = \int_{\Sigma} \omega \cdot n\;d\sigma \\
			&= \int_{S_{1} \cup S_{2}}\omega \cdot n\;d\sigma + \int_{S}\omega \cdot n\;d\sigma. 
		\end{split} 
	\end{align*} 
	Since by definition $\omega$ is tangent to $S$, the last integral identically vanishes. Applying consistent orientations on the boundary curves $C_{1}$ and $C_{2}$ of $S_{1}$ and $S_{2}$, respectively, and using Stokes' theorem, we obtain the desired conclusion. 
\end{proof} 

\paragraph{Conservation Laws when $\Omega = \mathbb{R}^{3}$} Now we consider conservation laws when $\Omega = \mathbb{R}^{3}$. We shall assume $u \in C^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3} \times [0,T])$ is a solution to the Euler equations with \textit{finite} energy (so that $u(x,t) \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})$ for all $(x, t)$). Moreover, we also assume that $\omega \in C^{1} \cup L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times [0,T])$. Then we make the following definition. 
\begin{definition}[Helicity]
	For any $t \in [0,T]$, for an ideal fluid in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with the above properties, we define the \textbf{helicity} of the fluid to be the quantity 
	\begin{equation}
		A(t) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}u(x,t)\cdot \omega(x, t)\;dx. 
		\label{eq:helicity}
	\end{equation}
	Intuitively, the helicity measures the extent to which vortex lines ``wrap'' and ``twist'' around the vortex tube. 
\end{definition} 
\begin{theorem}[Helicity as an Invariant Factor]
	In an ideal fluid under the action of conservative forces 
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{d}{dt}A = 0. 
	\end{equation*}
\end{theorem} 
In particular, we have additional invariant factors of ideal fluids under the action of conservative forces, which include \vspace{-0.25cm} 
\begin{itemize}[itemsep =-2pt]
	\item \textbf{Net Vorticity:} $\displaystyle \Omega = \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\omega\;dx;$ 
	\item \textbf{Momentum:} $\displaystyle 2 \int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}x \times \omega\;dx;$ 
	\item \textbf{Angular Momentum:} $\displaystyle -2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|x|^{2}\omega\;dx.$ 
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Potential and Irrotational Flows}
In this subsubsection, we investigate the properties of specific solutions to the Euler equations, namely irrotational ideals fluids. We start with the following definition. 
\begin{definition}[Potential Flow]
	A solution $u = u(x, t)$, to the Euler equations is called a \textbf{potential flow} if there exists some sufficiently regular function $\varphi(x, t)$ such that $u(x, t) = \nabla \varphi(x, t)$. 
\end{definition} 
Since under the right conditions of regularity, the curl of $u = \nabla \varphi$ is identically zero, $u$ is irrotational. Thus every potential flow is irrotational. However, the converse might not necessarily be true for nonsimply connected domains. We begin with a qualitative analysis of irrotational flows. 

\begin{prop}[Irrotational Flows on Bounded, Simply Connected Domains]
	On a bounded, simply connnected domain $\Omega$, all the irrotational flows reduce to the trivial one: $u = 0$. 
\end{prop} 
\begin{proof}
	The proof of this statement is essentially straightforward. Since $u$ is divergence free, and $u = \nabla \varphi$, one has that $\Delta \varphi = 0$. And since $u \cdot n = 0$ on $\partial \Omega$, $\partial u/\partial n = 0$ on $\partial \Omega$. Thus, $\varphi = \text{const.}$, whence $u \equiv 0$. Gometrically, this corresponds to the idea that it is impossible to extend to the interior of a bounded simply connected domain $\Omega$, a vector field tangent to $\partial \Omega$ in a smooth, divergence-free, curl-free way. 
\end{proof} 
Therefore, if we wish to study \textit{nontrivial} irrotational fields, we must either consider nonsimply connected or unbounded regions. In particular, we are interested in the notion of \textit{external domains}. 
\begin{definition}[External Domains]
	\textbf{External domains} are defined as the complement of a finite union of simply connected bounded regions (e.g., \textit{obstacles}). 
\end{definition} 

The consequences of such domains can significantly vary based on the dimension of the ambient space. E.g., consider the region depicted below. 
\begin{figure}[h!]
	\centering 
	\includegraphics[width=0.5\textwidth]{External_DomainI}
\end{figure}
In $\mathbb{R}^{2}$, the external domain is not simply connected so that irrotational flows are not necessarily potential flows. On the other hand, in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, the external domain is simply connected. So any irrotational flow $u$ is a potential flow: $u = \nabla \varphi$, where $\varphi$ satisfies the boundary condition $\partial u/\partial n = 0$ on $\partial \Omega$. Thus if we specify the asymptotic behavior $\lim_{|x| \to \infty}u(x) = U_{\infty}$, the uniqueness theorem for Neumann problems gives us a unique potential flow up to a constant.

Now let us return back to the case of $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ by considering the external domain to be the space $\Omega = \{x \in \mathbb{R}^{2}: x > 1\}$. Let $\varphi$ be the potential function associated with the velocity field $u$. We know that as $|x| \to \infty$, the obstruction becomes negligible so that $\partial \varphi/\partial x \sim 1$ as $|x| \to \infty$, which means that $\varphi \sim x = \rho\cos(\theta)$, where $(\rho, \theta)$ are polar coordinates on $\mathbb{R}^{2}$. Thus consider an initial ansatz of $\varphi(\rho,\theta) = A(\rho)\cos(\theta)$. Since $\varphi$ must be harmonic, we have the following: 
\begin{align*}
	\begin{split}
		\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial r^{2}}(A(\rho)\cos(\theta)) + \frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(A(\rho)\cos(\theta)) + \frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial \theta^{2}}(A(\rho)\cos(\theta)) &= 0 \\
		\implies 
		A''(\rho)\cos(\theta) + \frac{1}{r}A'(\rho)\cos(\theta) - \frac{1}{r^{2}}A(\rho)\cos(\theta) = 0. 
	\end{split} 
\end{align*} 
Thus we obtain the following ODE, 
\begin{equation*}
	\rho^{2}A''(\rho) + \rho A'(\rho) - A(\rho) = 0. 
\end{equation*}
Solving this ODE, we obtain a solution of the form 
\begin{equation*}
	\varphi(\rho,\theta) = \left(A\rho + \frac{B}{\rho}\right)\cos(\theta). 
\end{equation*}
Because of the obstruction at the unit circle, we require $\partial \varphi/\partial \rho = 0$ when $\rho = 1$. We compute, 
\begin{equation*}
	0 = \frac{\partial \varphi}{\partial \rho}\bigg|_{\rho = 1} = \left(A - \frac{B}{\rho^{2}}\right)\cos(\theta)\bigg|_{\rho = 1}  \iff A - B = 0 \iff A = B. 
\end{equation*}
Moreover, we want $\varphi \sim \rho\cos(\theta)$ as $\rho \to \infty$. Thus we get $A = 1$, whence $B= 1$. Therefore, the solution we obtain is 
\begin{equation*}
	\varphi(\rho,\theta) = (\rho + \rho^{-1})\cos(\theta). 
\end{equation*}
Figure (\ref{fig:external_domain_circle}) depicts the associated fluid flow around this obstruction. 

\begin{figure*}[h!]
	\centering 
	\begin{subfigure}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
	\includegraphics[height=3.2in]{fluid_flow_obstacleI}
	\caption{Fluid flow in the external domain without circulation.}
	\end{subfigure} 
	~
	\begin{subfigure}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
		\centering 
		\includegraphics[height=3.2in]{fluid_flow_obstacleII}
